Rather, Bayesian epistemology investigates _ideal_ degrees of belief as a _perfectly rational being_ would have them. This allows Bayesians to ignore the idiosyncrasies and limitations of actual belief distributions. The epistemological goal is to gain insight into which subjective probabilities we _should_ (or are best off to) assign in certain situations by studying perfectly rational degrees of belief. In this sense, subjective probabilities as studied in Bayesian epistemology are _normative_ .[] 

>  Alternatively, one could study instead objective degrees of justification or _evidential probability_ . See Williamson (, ) and Williamson (: ch. –). To some extent, evidential probability is quite similar to what sometimes goes under the heading 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

It is a matter of dispute which level of idealization serves the Bayesian’s epistemological goal best. To gain a sufficiently rich mathematical structure, it is usually supposed that perfectly rational degrees of belief conform to the standard axioms of probability. Against this assumption it is often objected that the underlying conception of perfect rationality gets stretched too much by supposing, in particular, that perfect rationality requires assigning subjective probability to every logical theorem. A subjective Bayesian who makes this assumption may insist that _perfect_ rationality does indeed require this kind of logical omniscience. But she then confronts the question of how insights about perfect rational beings so construed can be applied to actual epistemic subjects with limited epistemic capacities. In the context of our discussion, these types of issues are less pressing. We only need to presuppose that it makes sense to speak of the subjective probabilities a subject should or should not assign in a given epistemic situation. 

I will make an attempt to use ‘credences’ and ‘degrees of belief’ in a descriptive sense and ‘subjective probability’ in the more normative sense the Bayesian attaches to it. When the difference really matters, I might still talk about the subjective probabilities we _should_ assign in a given situation, even though the ‘should’ would not really be necessary as subjective probabilities carry already a normative force. 

On the other side of the spectrum, there are non-epistemic kinds of probability. Perhaps the most prominent kind is _objective (physical) chance_ . There are two important aspects of objective chances worth highlighting. First, objective chances vary with time (cf. Lewis ). _Before the campaign, the chances of the democratic candidate winning had been about fifty-fifty. When her opponent made a terrible mistake, her chances of winning were significantly raised. After the successful election, her chances of winning the selection became_  _and will remain there forever._ Hence, at different times, a proposition or sentence can have various different objective chances of being true. 

A second aspect of objective chances is a possible connection with indeterminism. If there are at a given time at a certain world _w_ nontrivial objective chances (i.e. chances different from and ), then we may suppose that the world _w_ is indeterministic in the sense that its history 

of ‘objective Bayesianism’. Cf. Williamson () and Williamson (a) (note: these latter two references are to Jon Williamson). 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

does not at every time determine its future. In this way, objective chances can be seen as tied up with the most fundamental laws of nature a world is governed by (cf. Schaffer ). Not everyone takes objective chances to be intertwined with indeterminism. There might be room for ‘deterministic chance’ applying to certain higher levels of the world and leaving a possible connection with indeterminism for a more specific kind of objective chance: chances applying to the most fundamental physical level (see e.g. Glynn for such a conception). I will mainly use ‘objective chance’ in this more fundamental sense, but as far as I can see, most of what we shall say would apply _mutatis mutandis_ to a more liberal conception of chance. (In section ..we shall see that the chances relevant for the evaluation of counterfactuals are most likely chances compatible with determinism.) 

Before we discuss how subjective probabilities and objective chances might be related to each other, we should briefly take a look at the concept of _conditional probability_ . Not only is Lewis’s Principal Principle formulated in terms of conditional probabilities, but for obvious reasons, they will also figure prominently in what is going to come later. 

The conditional probability of _A_ under _B_ can be thought of as the probability of _A_ on the assumption that _B_ is true. So, it is the probability of _A_ which results from ignoring the cases in which _B_ is false. To illustrate, suppose we consider a uniform probability distribution over a finite set of possible worlds. Then every world is assigned the same probability and the probability of a possibility is proportional to the number of worlds at which this possibility obtains. In such a situation, the conditional probability of _A_ under _B_ is simply the fraction of _A_ -worlds among the _B_ -worlds (=[|] _[A]_ |[∩] _B_ | _[B]_[|][).][][So, the conditional probability of] _[ A]_[ under] _[ B]_[ is given] by how much of the region in logical space at which _B_ is true is occupied by _A_ . Now, when we consider non-uniform distributions, we also need to consider the respective weights of the _A_ -worlds among the _B_ -worlds. For example, considering a fair coin, the conditional probability of it landing heads under the assumption that it is tossed is  _/_ , the fraction of headsworlds among the toss-worlds. But now suppose we consider a trick coin which lands heads about  _/_ of the time. In this case, the conditional probability of it landing heads on the assumption that it is tossed is  _/_ , 

>  Given a set _A_ , | _A_ | is its cardinality. 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

not  _/_ . The heads-worlds among the toss-worlds have two times more weight than the tails-worlds. 

Formally, the conditional probability of _A_ under _B_ , _P(A_ | _B)_ , can be defined in this way: 

**Conditional Probability.** _P(A_ | _B)_ := _[P][(] P[A] (_[∧] _B)[B][)]_ if _P(B) >_ . 

This definition can be given the following rationale. The numerator is the probability that _A_ and _B_ both obtain. This is the absolute probability of the _A_ -worlds which are also _B_ -worlds. Now, when we divide this probability by the probability of _B_ , we compare the probability of the _A_ -worlds which are _B_ -worlds to the probability of the _B_ -worlds and so get an expression of the fraction of _A_ -worlds among the _B_ -worlds weighted by the respective probabilities. 

The ratio definition of conditional probability leaves the conditional probability of _A_ under _B_ undefined if _B_ is assigned probability zero, for division by zero is not defined. There are various possibilities to deal with this issue. The first and obvious one is to accept the consequence that conditional probabilities are sometimes undefined. A second possibility is to look for a natural extension of the ratio definition covering the case in which the denominator is undefined. A possible convention is to stipulate that the conditional probability _P(A_ | _B)_ is if _P(B)_ = .[] To some extent, this stipulation is structurally similar to the stipulation that a conditional is vacuously true if its antecedent is impossible. Finally, it is possible to drop the ratio definition altogether and take conditional probabilities to be primitive (absolute probabilities can then be defined in terms of conditional probabilities).[] For our purposes, we will not have to choose between these options. 

With this in mind, let us now come to a possible connection between subjective probabilities and objective chances. It is a natural thought that subjective probabilities are constrained by objective chances: in some way or other, subjective probabilities should track objective chances. Suppose a fair coin is going to be tossed tomorrow. How likely should we think it to be true that it will come up heads?  _/_ , of course. Why? Because its present objective chance of coming up heads is  _/_ . On the other hand, 

>  This stipulation is sometimes made in the debate about conditionals. See footnote in chapter . 

>  For a defence of this approach, see e.g. Hájek (). 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

matching the objective chances is not always desirable. To see this, let us change the previous example slightly by supposing that the coin toss took place already. As before, I take the past objective chance that the coin would come up heads to be  _/_ . Given that I possess no further relevant evidence, it still seems mandatory to assign subjective probability  _/_ to the statement that the coin came up heads. But now assume further that I am told that the coin came up heads. This should raise my credence significantly, possibly to a value as high as . However, it should not alter my verdict concerning the past objective chances. So, this is a situation in which my credences should not follow my knowledge of the corresponding objective chances because I possess further relevant evidence. We can take from these preliminary considerations that a systematic description of how subjective probabilities relate to objective chances should steer a middle course between situations in which a match between subjective probabilities and known objective chances is rational and situations in which a divergence between the two seems to be preferable. 

A way of making the connection between subjective probabilities and objective chances precise is given by Lewis’s formulation of the Principal Principle.[] The Principal Principle can be seen as a systematic constraint on how our thinking about objective chances should influence our subjective probabilities. Let me present the Principal Principle here by quoting Lewis: 

**The Principal Principle** . Let _C_ be any reasonable initial credence function. Let _t_ be any time. Let _x_ be any real number in the unit interval. Let _X_ be the proposition that the chance, at time _t_ , of _A_ ’s holding equals _x_ . Let _E_ be any proposition compatible with _X_ that is admissible at time _t_ . Then 

**==> picture [169 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

To begin with, a couple of clarificatory remarks. First, _XE_ is the conjunction of _X_ and _E_ . Secondly, Lewis states his view in terms of reasonable initial credence functions. These are supposed to represent reasonable or fully rational epistemic states which have not taken in any (empirical) evidence. Once an initial credence function is conditionalized on a body of evidence, it represents a rational belief state possessing this 

>  See Lewis (). Some possible refinements are discussed in Lewis () and the postscript to Lewis () in Harper et al. (). For a more general discussion of the big picture, see Edgington (b). 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

evidence. So, to reiterate, the Principal Principle is directly concerned only with initial credence functions representing the beginning of a rational epistemic life. By assuming that later epistemic stages come from earlier ones by conditionalizing on the aquired evidence, we can use the Principal Principle to also draw inferences about non-initial credence functions. 

An important issue in clarifying the Principal Principle concerns the role played by evidence _admissible_ at the given time _t_ .[] Let us first see that allowing admissible evidence _E_ to enter the principle is better seen as a generalization than a restriction of the principle. If one were to omit _E_ from the principle, one would still arrive at a valid (but weaker) principle. The simpler principle would say that conditional on a statement of objective chance, an initial credence function should match the chance value. To instantiate this with an example: under the assumption that the objective chance of a coin landing heads is  _/_ and before we have aquired any (empirical) evidence, we should think it  _/_ -likely that the coin lands heads. As a matter of fact, the weaker principle is implied by the Principal Principle if we assume, plausibly, that the necessary proposition consisting of all possible worlds (and thus excluding none) is always admissible. 

The generalization achieved by allowing admissible evidence takes care of cases in which what we assume over and above the objective chances does not bear on the target proposition. It allows, for instance, that with respect to a future coin toss, we may additionally make an assumption about what happens in causally unrelated parts of the world. Of course, Lewis could not have generalized the Principal Principle all the way: some evidence must be inadmissible. For instance, evidence which directly reveals the truth-value of the target proposition must be inadmissible (as long as the considered chance value is below ). Under the twofold assumption that the chance of heads is  _/_ but that, as a matter of fact, the coin will come up heads, our credence in heads should be and not  _/_ , contrary to what the Principal Principle would suggest if all evidence were admissible. 

It is a difficult question which evidence should count as admissible. To a first approximation, a piece of evidence can be taken to be admissible at _t_ 

>  I am indebted to Alan Hájek for helping me a great deal to get clear about admissibility. 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

if it is solely concerned with the history up to _t_ and with what the laws of nature are (including general laws of chance). Histories are usually taken to be admissible, for what happened before a point _t_ at which the chances are taken cannot easily trump the information provided by the chances. If the statement under consideration is itself solely about the past and thus made true by the relevant history, it will have chance of being true at _t_ (the past is never chancy). Hence, the evidence provided by the history agrees with the evidence provided by the chances. Now, if the statement is about the future and we only possess information about the past and the present chances, it seems that our credences should go with the chances. The historical information may bear on what we think about the chances, say by indicating that a coin may be biased, but once the chances are fixed, it is hard to see how the historical information could trump the credences prescribed by the objective chances. For mixed cases of statements partly about the past and partly about the future, this line of thought does not go through as smoothly. And there are some hard cases, in which historical information may encode information about the future, say by suggesting that a time traveller testified that the coin will come up tails.[] 

Moving on, laws of nature and general facts about chance can be taken to be admissible for the following reason. Laws of nature do not seem to be chancy—they have always chance . The same can be held for general laws of chance, unless one opts for a Humean account of chance which allows for _undermining_ : positive chances of sequences of events which would imply that the present chances are different from what they actually are. If this possibility is granted, the Principal Principle is in need of modification (see Hall , Lewis and Thau for discussion and a possible way out). In connection with counterfactuals, we will have to say a little more on admissibility later (section .). 

Essentially, the Principal Principle then comes to this: conditional on the assumption that the objective chance of _A_ at time _t_ is _x_ and conditional on the admissible evidence at _t_ , one should (initially) have credence _x_ in _A_ . The principle can be seen as a specification of the idea that subjective probabilities are constrained by what we take the objective chances to be. 

>  See the discussion of ‘crystal balls’ in Hall (). For discussion relating the Principal Principle to possible knowledge of the future, see Hawthorne and Lasonen-Aarnio () and the response by Williamson (b). 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

_Prima facie_ , this principle is highly plausible, and it seems to allow for correct predictions with respect to a great variety of cases.[] 

## .Prior Epistemic Probabilities 

A first step to associate counterfactuals with certain conditional probabilities was taken by Adams (see Adams : ch. and Adams ). He considers the idea that counterfactuals are, in a sense to be made precise, _epistemic past tense_ forms of the corresponding indicative conditionals. More precisely, he suggests that the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals are tied to the prior epistemic probabilities of the corresponding forward-looking indicative conditionals, where forward-looking means that the conditional has a present tense antecedent and a future tense consequent formed with ‘will’. To give an example, our present credence in the counterfactual 

() If Oswald had not killed Kennedy, someone else would have is taken to be coordinated with our past credence in the indicative conditional 

() If Oswald does not kill Kennedy, someone else will. 

Since Adams took the probability of an indicative conditional to be the conditional probability of the consequent given the antecedent, we would look at the thesis that the subjective probability of a counterfactual is the past conditional probability of the consequent given the antecedent. In this section, I will try to clarify Adams’s proposal a little further and will also consider an interesting variant of it. 

The basic idea of Adams’s proposal is expressed in the following quotation: 

Whatever justifies the posterior affirmation of the counterfactual is then exactly the same as what did or might have justified the prior affirmation of the indicative, so the counterfactual can be regarded as a kind of _epistemic past tense_ , expressing 

>  But see Hawthorne and Lasonen-Aarnio (). They mention counterexamples to the Principal Principle which stem from the realm of the contingent a priori. Cf. also the discussion in Schulz (). As a possible source of the problem, see the considerations in Williamson () to the effect that contexts of subjective probability are hyper-intensional whereas contexts of objective chance are non-hyper-intensional. 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

not what _was_ the case at a prior time, but what _could have been affirmed_ at the prior time. (Adams : ) 

In the quoted passage, it does not become clear how the phrases ‘might have justified’ or ‘could have been affirmed’ are to be understood. The immediate surroundings of the quotation suggest that these phrases are only meant to deal with cases in which we have not asserted or not even considered the corresponding indicative conditional at a relevant prior time. If we abstract from possible restrictions of this kind and assume that our credences are defined over all relevant sentences at the required times, we arrive at the thesis that we should think about a counterfactual now what we once thought about the corresponding indicative conditional. 

That this proposal cannot be right is fairly obvious. Whatever the past epistemic state is in which the indicative conditional should be evaluated, we may gain information in the meantime relevant to the evaluation of the counterfactual. Someone may even tell us whether the counterfactual is true, or a crucial piece of evidence may become available which decides the truth-value of the counterfactual. In such cases, it is clear that our verdict about the counterfactual will depart greatly from our previous verdict about the corresponding indicative conditional. It would be very odd if what we presently think about a counterfactual were forced to inherit any mistakes we might previously have made about an indicative conditional. 

To illustrate the problem, consider the following variant of the Oswald/Kennedy example. Suppose we had thought before Kennedy’s assassination that there is a conspiracy plot to kill Kennedy. For this reason, we might have accepted the indicative conditional ‘If Oswald does not kill Kennedy, someone else will’. Now time passes and we learn that there actually never has been any such conspiracy. As a result, we do not accept the corresponding counterfactual ‘If Oswald had not killed Kennedy, someone else would have’. In such a scenario, our credence in the counterfactual does not go with our past credence in the forwardlooking indicative conditional because the latter credence was based on a belief we now think is false. 

Given this problem, one might wish to entertain an alternative interpretation, or rather a variant, of Adams’s proposal. Instead of associating our present credences in counterfactuals with a possibly mistaken past credence in the corresponding indicative conditional, we may consider 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

the idea that the right epistemic perspective from which to evaluate the indicative conditional is not our past epistemic state but rather our present epistemic state. Our credence in the counterfactual would then go by our present subjective probability concerning what was or would have been expressed by a past utterance of the indicative conditional. This seems to be a much more interesting proposal, for it would avoid the present problem and also seems to come with more initial plausibility. In order to properly evaluate it, we need to take a step back and contemplate the idea that counterfactuals might be past tense forms of the corresponding forward-looking indicative conditionals. 

To be precise, let us consider the following view: (past perfect) counterfactuals presently express what a forward-looking indicative conditional expressed at an earlier time (cf. Dudman , , Strawson and Woods : ch. ). Call this _the past tense view_ of counterfactuals.[] For instance, the suggestion would be that the two Kennedy-conditionals above, () and (), express the same content when the indicative conditional is uttered at an appropriate earlier time.[] Basically, the past tense view says that counterfactuals and future-looking indicative conditionals differ merely in temporal reference. Evidence for this view mainly comes from the observation, described in the introduction (section .), that ‘would’ can be seen as the past tense of ‘will’, which supports the idea that ‘would’-conditionals might be past tense forms of ‘will’-conditionals. Note, though, that this evidence is not fully conclusive, for other elements present in counterfactuals might interfere and tense in counterfactuals is, as we saw as well, not always interpreted temporally in counterfactuals. 

Now, what has the past tense view to do with the variant of Adams’s proposal we are presently envisaging? First of all, the past tense view implies 

>  In the literature, this view is sometimes referred to as the _relocation thesis_ . Cf. Bennett (: ). 

>  One may want to know what counts as an appropriate earlier time. A possible, though not fully specific answer would be that it is a point in time which lies shortly before the antecedent is made false in the actual world (assuming here that it is false). One may also doubt that such a point always exists—think of a counterfactual which concerns all times like ‘If gravity obeyed an inverse cube law, then the planets would not have stable orbits’ (thanks to Alan Hájek for this example). What is the appropriate earlier time? If one does not wish to border on inconsistency by saying that this point lies before the beginning of time (if such exists), one should perhaps say that in cases in which the counterfactual makes no specific temporal reference, there is no appropriate earlier time and the corresponding indicative conditional should be construed similarly as being about all times. It would then make no difference at which time it is evaluated. 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

Adams’s proposal. If a counterfactual expresses what an indicative conditional expressed at an earlier time, then it seems clear that our present credence in a counterfactual should be our present credence concerning what is expressed by the earlier utterance of the indicative conditional, for these two credences would concern the same content. Conversely, if Adams’s proposal were correct, this would be strong evidence for the past tense view. For suppose the past tense view were false. Then there would be a counterfactual and a corresponding forward-looking indicative conditional, where the latter would at no time express the same content. But if the expressed contents differ, we probably could have different credences concerning the two, in which case Adams’s proposal would be false. Essentially, then, it seems that the past tense view describes the underlying semantic principle needed for the presently envisaged variant of Adams’s proposal. 

In order to evaluate the past tense view, let us tease out one of its consequences. Quite generally, if a sentence _s_  differs from a sentence _s_  merely in temporal reference and the tensed elements in _s_  refer in a context _C_  to a time _t_ and the tensed elements in _s_  refer in a context _C_  to _t_ as well, then an epistemic subject who is aware of the passage of time should have the same epistemic attitude towards _s_  in _C_  as she has towards _s_  in _C_  unless she gains or loses relevant information while transiting from _C_  to _C_ .[] Call this the _continuity principle_ (cf. Schulz a). For instance, if I am aware of the passage of time and neither gain nor lose any relevant information, I should think today about ‘Today is my birthday’ what I thought yesterday about ‘Tomorrow is my birthday’. If our epistemic attitudes attach primarily to propositions, and the relevant pairs of indexical sentences are taken to express the same proposition in the two contexts respectively, then the continuity principle can be seen to follow from the more general fact that we should not change our beliefs unless we gain or lose relevant information. But since I do not want to preclude the view that our epistemic attitudes attach to propositions _as presented under the guise of a sentence_ and want to leave open the possibility that the relevant pairs of sentences do not express quite the same proposition or no proposition at all, I prefer the somewhat more cautious continuity principle. 

>  I assume that the sentences are not context-sensitive in other respects. 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

If we apply the continuity principle to the past tense view of counterfactuals, we find the following consequence. An epistemic subject with a certain credence in a forward-looking indicative conditional at a given time should have the same credence in the corresponding counterfactual at some later point in time, provided she is aware of the passage of time and neither receives nor loses any relevant information. If a counterfactual merely expresses what an indicative conditional expressed at an earlier time, our earlier credence in the indicative conditional should equal our present credence in the counterfactual in situations in which we neither gain nor lose any relevant information. We can use this consequence to test the past tense view. Note that the problem cases for Adams’s original proposal do not conflict with this consequence, for they were cases where we received information about the two conditionals (in the modified Kennedy case we learned that there actually was no conspiracy). The past tense view only predicts that our past credence in the indicative conditional should become our present credence in the counterfactual in those cases in which we do not receive any relevant information. This is a much weaker consequence, but as we shall see now, it is still too strong. 

Against the past tense view, consider the following case.[] Officer Shaker comes home in the afternoon. He finds a note in the kitchen saying ‘I will be back tonight’. Unfortunately, he cannot identify who has written this. It may have been his daughter Linda or his wife Elizabeth. It is much more likely that it was written by Linda, because Elizabeth told him that she would be going on a business trip and would not be back before the other day. But Elizabeth’s plans may have changed on short notice. In any case, Shaker seems right in thinking: 

() If Linda is not home tonight, Elizabeth will be. 

After this, Shaker receives a call from work and has to spend the whole night away from home. What should Shaker think at the dawn of the new day about the following counterfactual? 

() If Linda had not been home last night, Elizabeth would have been. Shaker has to consider two possibilities. Suppose first the most likely case that Linda wrote the letter. Then the counterfactual can be assumed to 

-  Bennett (: ) discusses an example which is similar in many respects. 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

be false: no one would have been home last night if Linda had not been home last night, for Elizabeth would still have been on her business trip without any intentions of coming home early. In the second scenario where Elizabeth wrote the letter we could assume the counterfactual to be true. Still, since the first case is much more likely, Shaker should think that the counterfactual is probably false. Yet he took the indicative conditional to be highly likely or even certain. He does not seem to have received any relevant information. If the two conditionals differed merely in temporal reference, Shaker’s different epistemic attitudes would not be rational. But they seem to be. 

Here is a possible diagnosis. The counterexample works because there is merely an evidential link between the antecedent and the consequent of the indicative conditional. Since Shaker knows that the letter was written either by his daughter or his wife and he has no reason to distrust its content, he can conclude that if Linda is not home tonight, Elizabeth will be. But the available information does not support any non-evidential connection (or lack thereof) between antecedent and consequent.[] As far as he knows, if Linda wrote the letter, Elizabeth has no intentions of coming home that night and her plans are independent of Linda’s whereabouts. Hence, later on, he has not much reason to think that if Linda had not been home, Elizabeth would have been. 

So, even the more plausible variant of Adams’s proposal cannot be quite correct. Sometimes, what we think about a counterfactual is not what we think about an earlier utterance of the corresponding indicative conditional. We may think it unlikely that if Linda had not been home, Elizabeth would have been. Still, we may think that what the indicative conditional ‘If Linda is not home, Elizabeth will be’ expressed at an earlier time is quite likely. It seems, then, that even the modified proposal promotes too strong a link between counterfactuals and corresponding indicative conditionals. What we would need is an even more modest suggestion. Instead of past or present credences about past utterances, one may consider credences in a hypothetical belief state appropriately related to our present beliefs. To this possibility we shall now turn. 

>  The bracketed qualification is present because counterfactuals may occasionally owe their truth to the absence of any connection between antecedent and consequent. For instance, Elisabeth could have thought that she will come home independently of what Linda does and therefore hold ‘If Linda did not come home, I would come home (and if she did, I would come home, too)’ (thanks to Alan Hájek for this point). 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

## .Hypothetical Epistemic Probabilities 

Adams (: ch. .) already contemplated the possibility that his proposal may not hold in full generality. This led him to conjecture that our present subjective probabilities of counterfactuals may rather go with certain _hypothetical_ subjective probabilities of the corresponding indicative conditionals, which may differ from our actual prior credences. Subsequently, this view was further developed by Edgington (, ).[] Let us have a look at how she describes it: 

[T]he conditional probabilities relevant to the assessment of subjunctive conditionals do not (typically) represent your present actual distribution of belief, but those of a hypothetical belief state in a different context, normally that of an earlier time, concerning, for example, whether someone else will kill Kennedy if Oswald doesn’t. (Edgington : ) 

Edgington suggests that our epistemic attitude towards the counterfactual is determined by our epistemic attitude towards the forward-looking indicative conditional ‘If Oswald does not kill Kennedy, someone else will’ in some hypothetical belief state suitably related to our present belief state. Presumably, such a hypothetical belief state will include what we now believe about the circumstances a short while before Kennedy was killed, e.g., that Oswald prepared himself for the assassination. However, it will not include our present belief that Kennedy was killed by someone, for otherwise the forward-looking indicative conditional ‘If Oswald does not kill Kennedy, someone else will’ would be acceptable in the hypothetical belief state, which would yield the wrong verdict that the counterfactual ‘If Oswald had not killed Kennedy, someone else would have’ is acceptable, too. Let me make a few comments about this approach. 

The hypothetical belief state approach is quite flexible and is not committed to the past tense view of counterfactuals, although it departs from it only slightly. It shares with the past tense view the more general assumption that the evaluation of counterfactuals is to be explained in terms of the evaluation of indicative conditionals, which are in turn assumed to go with the corresponding conditional subjective probabilities. This general assumption might still be disputable. A source of potential trouble is the 

>  Woods (: ch. ) seems to favour a similar position. 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

contingent a priori.[] Obvious a priori truths should not be denied in any belief state, or at least only under very specific circumstances. However, some a priori truths are contingent. Their contingency makes it possible that they can be truly denied in the consequent of a counterfactual. But then there are acceptable counterfactuals to which there are no natural hypothetical belief states in which the consequent is assigned a high conditional probability. 

For instance, consider the following example: 

- () If there had been a massive space impact early in evolution, there would have been no thinkers. 

This counterfactual seems to be acceptable (or could be made so by stipulating the relevant facts): if there had been a massive space impact early in evolution, conditions on earth would have been too unfriendly for higher animals to develop. However, in any belief state, the subjective probability that there is a thinker should be , for this is a fairly obvious a priori truth. If so, there is no reasonable hypothetical belief state in which the conditional probability of the consequent given the antecedent will be high: the conditional probability that there are no thinkers given that there was a massive space impact early in evolution will always be low. But then our present credence in the counterfactual does not correspond to the subjective probability of the consequent given the antecedent in a related hypothetical belief state. To reinforce the point, contrast the counterfactual with an indicative variant which tries to get at the same point: 

- () If there was a massive space impact early in evolution, there never were nor will be any thinkers. 

As long as we do not doubt that we are thinkers, the indicative conditional is not acceptable. Even on the assumption of the antecedent, we would rather doubt that the space impact had the said effect. 

Not much depends here on the choice of the example. All that is required is that there are some contingent propositions which should be assigned credence in any belief state which is a candidate for modelling 

>  The following argument is inspired by considerations in Jackson (: f.), Weatherson (), and Williamson (). 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

the relevant hypothetical belief state. The latter qualification is important, for it shows that we do not even have to presuppose that the example is strictly a priori. For instance, one may think that we could doubt that there is a thinker by supposing that we are all robots of sorts which only execute programs but do not really think. The point would then be that in evaluating a counterfactual like the one above, nothing seems to invite us to consider such outlandish cases. A belief state in which we doubt that we are thinkers does not seem to be a good candidate to model the relevant hypothetical belief state. 

Do the present considerations refute the hypothetical belief state approach conclusively? Probably not. What they seem to show is that the most straightforward interpretation of it cannot be quite correct. But there is still room for manoeuvre. Let me briefly mention one possible way out. 

Perhaps _hypothetical_ belief states are relevantly different from _ordinary_ belief states. Such a difference may make it possible that obvious a priori truths can reasonably be denied in a hypothetical belief state. Two-dimensional semantics could provide the resources to implement this idea. In a two-dimensional framework, instances of the contingent a priori are associated with two propositions, one being necessary, the other contingent (cf. e.g. Chalmers ). For instance, a sentence such as ‘There is a thinker’ can be associated (in a given context) with a _primary proposition_ which is necessary and a _secondary proposition_ which is contingent. It seems possible to hold that our subjective probabilities are usually sensitive to the primary proposition expressed by a sentence. To continue with the example, the suggestion would be that we can be certain that there is a thinker because our credence attaches to the primary proposition expressed by ‘There is a thinker’, which is necessary. Now, this framework may be brought to bear on the present issue in the following way. Perhaps hypothetical belief states differ from ordinary belief states by being sensitive to the secondary proposition expressed by the relevant sentences. As a consequence, it would be possible to assign in a hypothetical belief state a low credence to ‘There is a thinker’ because hypothetical credences would attach to the secondary proposition expressed by the sentence under consideration. In the present example, the secondary proposition is contingent and differs from the primary proposition, which is necessary. On this picture, counterfactuals 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

would invite us to think about the world as possibly non-actual, which may shift the perspective from which we evaluate the relevant sentences.[] 

At the present stage, it is not clear whether such a strategy will ultimately work. But it is likely that a fully developed account would (a) not be simple and (b) involve substantial theoretical commitments. Hence, it may not deliver an easily accessible principle which systematizes the data concerning uncertain counterfactuals on which further theorizing could then safely be based. Recall that we presently do not yet seek a theory of counterfactuals but merely a comprehensive way of capturing the relevant data so that we get a clear grip on what such a theory would need to explain. 

## .Prior Propensities 

The previous considerations can be taken to indicate that the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals do not stand in any systematic relation to the subjective probabilities of indicative conditionals. If one accepts this diagnosis, one may reckon that our credences in counterfactuals aim at something more objective. A natural way of fleshing this out would be to suppose that the evaluation of counterfactuals is guided by what we think about corresponding objective conditional chances. Skyrms () has developed a view along such lines, albeit for somewhat different reasons. More recently, Moss () has defended his approach. In this section, I will present Skyrms’s proposal and point to some residual issues, which we shall resolve in the next chapter. 

Skyrms introduces his suggestion as follows: 

My suggestion is that the prior probability account can be saved by one small change—the probabilities involved are the prior _propensities_ rather than the prior epistemic probabilities. [ _. . ._ ] [I]f we do not know for certain the values of the prior propensities, we may have to do with a weighted average—the expected prior propensities. The weights in this average will be epistemic probabilities, and we should use the best ones available—for this job—the _posterior_ epistemic probabilities. I will use PR for epistemic probabilities and pr for propensities. I will superscript _i_ and _f_ for prior (initial) and posterior (final) respectively. Let the double arrow, ⇒, symbolise the subjunctive conditional, and BAV be 

>  An approach of this kind could take further inspiration from the two-dimensional semantics for conditionals developed in Weatherson (). 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

‘Basic Assertability Value’. Then the theory that I am suggesting can be succinctly expressed thusly: 

∗∗∗ BAV _(p_ ⇒ _q)_ =[�] _j_[PR] _[f]_[ [][pr] _[i] j_[] ·][ pr] _[i] j[(][q]_[ given] _[ p][)]_[.] where the pr _[i] j_[’s are the appropriate prior propensity distributions. (Skyrms :] f.) 

Skyrms describes our present subjective probabilities of counterfactuals as aiming at the _prior objective conditional propensities_ of the consequent given the antecedent. It is notoriously difficult to say what exactly a propensity is.[] For the sake of simplicity, but also because I think it yields the most interesting account, I will tentatively equate propensities with objective chances (this assumption will be lifted in the next chapter). Thus, subjective probabilities of counterfactuals would be described as estimating certain prior conditional chances. To get a better grip on the proposal, let us apply this account to two examples. Consider the counterfactual 

() If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads. 

In Skyrms’s notation, lower case letter pr _[i]_ represents the prior chances and capital letter PR _[f]_ our present subjective probabilities. Suppose we believe outright that the coin is fair. So, we think that its conditional chance of landing heads given that it was tossed was .(formally: pr _[i] (q_ given _p)_ =  _/_ ). In Skyrms’s notation, this means PR _[f]_ [pr _[i]_ ] = . Applying the formula, we find 

() PR _[f]_ [pr _[i]_ ] · pr _[i] (q_ given _p)_ =  · . = .. 

In the present scenario, we should have credence .in the counterfactual () because we are certain that the objective chance of it landing heads given that it was tossed was  _/_ . This generalizes: if the relevant objective chances are known with certainty, the constraint recommends adjusting our credence in the counterfactual to what we take to be the past chance of the consequent conditional on the antecedent. For a great variety of basic cases, this seems to predict the intuitively correct values. 

Let us also take a somewhat more complicated epistemic situation to illustrate the complexity of Skyrms’s formula. Suppose we have credence
