 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

.that the coin was fair. So, PR _[f]_ [pr _[i]_ [] =][ .with][pr] _[i]_  _[(][q]_[ given] _[ p][)]_[ =][ ..] Suppose also that we have credence .that it was biased towards heads with a probability of .. In formal terms, PR _[f]_ [pr _[i]_ [] =][ .with] pr _[i]_  _[(][q]_[ given] _[ p][)]_[ =][ ..][At][this][point,][one][crucial][aspect][of][Skyrms’s][idea,] namely that counterfactuals are evaluated by _estimating_ prior conditional chances, shows up. Skyrms suggests that our credence in the counterfactual should now be raised to ., since it should be the average of the relevant two chances weighted by our credences that these are the correct chances: 

**==> picture [246 x 39] intentionally omitted <==**

Indeed, although intuitions may not confirm the exact values, it seems correct that the more likely we take it that the coin was biased towards heads, and the stronger we take it to be biased, the more confidence we should have in the counterfactual thought that it would have landed heads if it had been tossed. 

Before we turn to the details of Skyrms’s account, let us see how it allows us to deal with the problematic issue surrounding the contingent a priori. The crucial fact to be observed here is that instances of the contingent a priori can have a chance to be false. It is a priori that there is a thinker, but at one time in the past, there may have been a chance that there would not be any. The contingency of instances of the contingent a priori makes it possible that they can have a chance to be false. So, if evaluating counterfactuals involves estimating _objective_ conditional chances rather than hypothetical _subjective_ probabilities, it will no longer be a problem that instances of the contingent a priori can be denied in the consequent of a counterfactual. 

Skyrms states his view in terms of what he calls the _basic assertability value_ . Thus, it is presupposed that assertability comes in degrees, i.e. that a statement may be more or less assertable (in this he follows Adams , ). This is not an uncontentious assumption. It may well be that assertability is an all-or-nothing matter (cf. e.g. Williamson ). For instance, it would seem that the counterfactual ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads’ is plainly unassertable, although it gets a basic assertability value of  _/_ on Skyrms’s account (if the coin is taken to be fair). Given that we are looking for a characterization of the subjective 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

probabilities of counterfactuals, I will interpret Skyrms’s account in this way and talk about subjective probabilities instead of assertability values. 

There are some residual issues with Skyrms’s proposal. They mainly concern the exact nature of the notion of chance present in his account. In particular, we have to address Morgenbesser cases, the relation between chance and determinism and the possibility of counterfactuals with an antecedent contradicting the laws of nature. We also have to take a brief look at whether modus ponens holds for counterfactuals. As we shall see, each problem can be seen as providing information about how we have to understand the notion of objective chance for Skyrms’s proposal to be plausible. 

## _..Morgenbesser Cases_ 

To begin with, let us have a look at the original Morgenbesser case and a similar example discussed by Edgington (a):[] 

- () If I had bet on heads, I would have won. () If I had caught the flight, I would be in Paris now. 

Initially, we may have credence  _/_ in () because we assume that the past chance of winning conditional on betting was  _/_ . Similarly, we may be almost certain about () given that we take the past chance of being in Paris conditional on taking the flight to Paris to be very high. These credences are in accordance with Skyrms’s constraint, for they are an estimate of the past conditional chances. However, they seem to be unstable. Suppose we find out that the coin came up heads. We can then be certain that I would have won, because how the coin would have landed would not have been influenced by my betting on the outcome.[] An analogous situation can arise with respect to the second example as described by Edgington. Assume that we find out that the plane crashed because the on-board computer broke down. Given this discovery, we should have credence identical or close to in (), because if I had caught the flight, I would be dead now. Thus, our credences in counterfactuals 

>  The Morgenbesser example is reported in Slote (: , fn. ). 

>  There may be trick coins which are somehow sensitive to my betting behaviour. If necessary, we could stipulate that we are dealing with an ordinary coin. 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

may be raised and lowered by evidence concerning events which occur after the time with respect to which the objective chances are evaluated. 

Following a possible reaction considered by Slote (: , fn. ), Phillips () denies that the intuitions about Morgenbesser cases are genuine if the underlying chance process is truly indeterministic. I can see that this is a possible option (but cf. the defence in Edgington a). However, if possible, we should prefer a framework which is not hostage to fortune in the sense that it would not be flexible enough to account for Morgenbesser cases if necessary. This is why I treat the Morgenbesser cases here as genuine. But nothing in the final theory will commit us to this position. 

If genuine, Morgenbesser cases are a problem for Skyrms’s proposal, for they would show that our credences in counterfactuals cannot always be our best estimate of the past conditional chances. Sometimes certain facts would be held constant in the evaluation of counterfactuals although they are made true only after the time from which the relevant counterfactual worlds are allowed to depart from the actual course of events. As a result, the past conditional chances can be knowably high or low without our subjective probabilities of the corresponding counterfactual being high or low respectively. 

It is possible to amend Skyrms’s proposal slightly to accommodate Morgenbesser cases. We could adopt a suggestion by Edgington about how to handle the problem: 

The objectively correct value to assign to such a counterfactual is not (or not always) the conditional chance of _C_ given _A_ at the time of the fork; but the conditional chance, at that time, of _C_ given _A_ & _S_ where _S_ is a conjunction of those facts concerning the time between antecedent and consequent which are (a) causally independent of the antecedent, and (b) affect the chance of the consequent. (Edgington a: ) 

A brief clarificatory remark: by ‘the time of the fork’ Edgington means the point in time at which counterfactual worlds are allowed to depart from the actual history of the world in order to make the antecedent of the counterfactual true. This is the time at which we would expect the past chances to be taken. Now, the basic idea of Edgington’s is to enrich the condition relative to which the conditional chances are evaluated. Instead of the chance of the consequent given the antecedent, we would work with the chance of the consequent given the antecedent plus a 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

certain body of further information _S_ . The sentence _S_ will vary between different antecedents as it is concerned with information independent of the antecedent, and a sentence may be independent of one antecedent but dependent on another. For this reason, I think it would be more perspicuous to write _SA_ instead of _S_ . This way it would be explicit that _S_ is a function of the antecedent of the counterfactual. 

In the examples above, _SA_ will comprise the information that the coin landed heads or the information that the plain crashed. That is to say, _SA_ will specify the information we hold constant in the evaluation of counterfactuals about times after the time at which the past chances are taken. If we incorporate this idea, we could rightly predict, for example, that we should have a low credence in (): given that I took the flight plus the information that it crashed, the past chances of me being in Paris now would indeed be low. 

Edgington also offers an account of the information contained in _SA_ . According to her, it is information about what is causally independent of the antecedent. This can be motivated by the observation that the reason why we sometimes hold certain information constant seems to be that the antecedent would not have had an influence on it. For instance, whether or not I had bet, it would not have affected how the coin would have landed. In a similar vein, one could also suppose that probabilistic independence from the antecedent at the relevant time is what matters in these cases. In addition, Edgington proposes a second condition saying that _SA_ should only contain information relevant to the probability of the consequent. This condition is probably there in order to limit the size of _SA_ . Without it, _SA_ could be blown up by a lot of irrelevant information. 

Hájek () raises doubts about Edgington’s idea to identify _SA_ in terms of causal independence. However, in the context of our present discussion, the exact nature of such a set _SA_ is somewhat less important. Even if Edgington’s specific proposal about what makes up the set _SA_ has to be amended, her general structural proposal to conditionalize on _A_ together with a variable body of information _SA_ could still be correct. The set _SA_ might have to be defined in a slightly (or completely) different way, or it might not be definable at all. For now, it suffices to see that a proposal of this general kind could solve the problem with Morgenbesser cases for Skyrms, never mind whether Edgington’s specific proposal is already fully satisfactory. 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

## _..Counterfactuals and Determinism_ 

Questions about determinism and indeterminism bear in many ways on the theory of counterfactuals. In the present context, our focus will be on whether the chances in Skyrms’s evaluation constraint are chances which would require the world to be indeterministic. In order to answer this question, we will have to take a closer look at how the assumption of determinism influences the evaluation of counterfactuals. As we shall see, counterfactuals are not evaluated much differently under the assumption of determinism and so we will conclude that what we need is some kind of ‘deterministic chance’ which is largely indiscriminate between determinism and indeterminism. 

For present purposes, the following rough-and-ready definition of determinism will do: a possible world _w_ is _deterministic_ iff for all times _t_ , the history of _w_ up to and including _t_ together with the laws of nature obtaining in _w_ completely determine what happens in _w_ after _t_ . So, if _Ht_ is a complete expression of the history of _w_ up to _t_ (which is true at a world _w_[′] just in case _w_[′] shares its history with _w_ up to _t_ ) and _L_ is a complete expression of the laws of nature obtaining in _w_ , then _w_ is deterministic just in case for all times _t_ the conjunction of _Ht_ and _L_ implies everything which in fact happens in _w_ after _t_ . In a deterministic world, there are at the fundamental level no non-trivial objective chances: at every time, the chances that a certain proposition is true will be either or . 

Now, how are counterfactuals evaluated on the assumption of determinism? _Prima facie_ , it seems that this assumption does not make it much harder to evaluate counterfactuals and their evaluation does not seem to change a lot compared to indeterministic scenarios. In typical epistemic situations, we still think it about  _/_ -likely that _if the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads_ regardless of whether we assume the lottery to be governed by deterministic or indeterministic laws. 

To be sure, let us look at the issue in a little more detail. At first sight, one may think that if a world is deterministic, the set of relevant antecedentworlds to be considered in the evaluation of counterfactuals consists either solely of worlds at which the consequent is true or solely of worlds at which the consequent is false. The reason for this assumption would be that in a deterministic world, the laws of nature together with the antecedent of the counterfactual either imply the consequent or its negation. If something like this were true, then one might wonder whether 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

our uncertainty about a coin-toss counterfactual is simply grounded in our uncertainty about whether or not the antecedent-cum-laws imply the consequent. Our uncertainty would then not be informed by a certain conception of objective conditional probabilities which assume an intermediate value like  _/_ . 

As it stands, the present assumption cannot be correct, for the laws of nature together with the antecedent will in general not imply much, let alone a whole future which would always decide the consequent of the counterfactual. What is missing is information about initial conditions not yet specified by the antecedent. Perhaps the thought should rather be that the laws of nature together with the antecedent and a certain slice of the history of the actual world decide the consequent. Taking in a certain part of the history of the actual world is well motivated, for it seems that we typically hold a certain part of the past constant in the evaluation of counterfactuals. However, if the antecedent of the counterfactual is false, then any part of the history of the actual world together with the laws of nature is already inconsistent with the antecedent. In a deterministic world, a certain part of the history of the world together with the laws of nature already determines the whole future. If, by assumption, the antecedent is false, then its negation is implied by the history and the laws of nature. From this we can infer that the set of relevant worlds at which the antecedent is true cannot consist of worlds which share a certain history with the actual world and display the same laws of nature. 

In the debate about counterfactuals, there are two main reactions to this issue. On the first proposal, the assumption that the relevant antecedentworlds share a certain history with the actual world is given up (cf. Bennett , but Bennett : ch. now defends the opposite view). In numerous possibly undetectable ways the history of the relevant worlds will at any time be slightly different from the history of the actual world. On the second proposal advertised by Lewis (), the assumption that the actual laws of nature obtain at the relevant antecedent-worlds is given up. To make the antecedent true, small ‘miracles’ are allowed to happen at the relevant antecedent-worlds. They are, however, only miracles relative to the actual laws. Relative to the worlds at which they occur, they are not miracles and compatible with the laws at those worlds, which will thus be different from the actual laws, however slightly. 

Now, on both proposals, there is nothing in the concept of determinism which suggests either (a) in which way the history of the 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

relevant antecedent-worlds should differ from the history of the actual world or (b) which kind of miracle should happen at the relevant antecedent-worlds. Since neither the difference in history nor the kind of miracle is uniquely determined, the set of relevant antecedent-worlds will often consist (a) of worlds which depart in slightly different ways from the history of the actual world (for various departures from the history of the actual world may be equally relevant) or (b) of worlds at which slightly different miracles happen (for various miracles may be equally relevant). Hence, there is no reason to suppose that the set of relevant antecedentworlds will be homogenous in the sense of consisting solely of worlds which make the consequent true or solely of worlds which make it false. 

To reiterate, consider a coin-toss case again and suppose that it is a deterministic process. As a matter of fact, the coin was not flipped. How would the world have been if it had been flipped? It might have been flipped in many different ways, it seems. Within certain boundaries, direction and momentum of the flip could have varied freely. Given the symmetries of the coin, the set of possible flips would most likely have produced a number of heads-ups close to %. For this reason, we may expect about % of the relevant toss-worlds to be heads-worlds even under the assumption of determinism. 

If all this is correct, the chances in Skyrms’s evaluation constraint are best construed as chances which can be non-trivial even in deterministic worlds. Skyrms’s (: –) conception of propensity was indeed ‘deterministic’ in this sense. 

## _..Counterlegals_ 

There is a closely related issue. Counterfactuals may have an antecedent which contradicts the laws of nature (such counterfactuals are often referred to as _counterlegals_ ). A proposition incompatible with the laws of nature may never have a chance of being true. This seems clear for fundamental physical chances.[] But even if we avail ourselves of a certain kind of non-fundamental chance compatible with determinism, there is no guarantee that it will assign positive probability to negations of laws of nature. What this would indicate is that the chances we need should not 

>  Maybe not. Humeans like Lewis () think that chances can be undermined. Note, though, that undermining futures with a positive chance at some time do not seem to comprise all metaphysical possibilities. For this reason, deferring to a kind of ‘Humean chance’ would probably not solve our present problem. 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

only be compatible with determinism but also _global_ in the sense of being well defined for all metaphysically possible antecedents. 

How much of a problem are counterlegals? It seems clear that they are used less frequently than other counterfactuals. Still, counterlegals do not seem to be defective. What the world would be like if the gravitational constant had been different, say a little higher, seems to be a sensible question. So, an evaluation constraint on counterfactuals should accommodate the possibility that counterlegals may be justified with variable strength. 

Some philosophers think that the laws of nature are metaphysically necessary.[] However, if one thinks that the laws of nature are necessary, one will probably not think that counterfactuals whose antecedent is impossible are acceptable already on these grounds. In other words, they would not think that counterlegals are vacuously true, for this would probably make too many counterfactuals vacuously true. Hence, assuming the necessity of laws of nature would not free us from finding a conception of chance relevant to the evaluation of counterfactuals according to which laws of nature may have a chance of being false. 

## _..Probabilistic Modus Ponens_ 

As noted by Adams (: ), we seem to judge a counterfactual to be definitely false or incorrect if we take the antecedent to be true and the consequent to be false. In other words, modus ponens does not seem to be invalid for counterfactuals. In the context of a probabilistic evaluation constraint on counterfactuals, this leads to a problem, for the past conditional chances can be high even if the consequent turns out to be false despite the antecedent turning out to be true. 

Here is the problem in some more detail. Sometimes the world unfolds in a way which was at an earlier time quite unlikely to happen. For instance, it may have been quite likely that I would lose if I were to buy a lottery ticket. But suppose I bought a lottery ticket and won against all odds. In such a situation, it seems we should not invest a high credence in the counterfactual 

>  See e.g. Edgington (b), who also relates this idea to some themes in Kripke (), or Smith (). 

 the problem of evaluating counterfactuals 

## () If I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost. 

Arguably, our credence should actually be zero. 

If we take the evaluation of counterfactuals to be governed by an intuitive conception of past chances, then this is a problem. For what we think about the case is compatible with us taking the past conditional chances of losing to be high. It was probable back then that we would lose given that we were yet to buy a ticket. But on the present interpretation, Skyrms’s proposal would recommend a high credence in the counterfactual even if we take the antecedent to be true and the consequent to be false. Thus, a probabilistic version of modus ponens would fail: a high credence in the antecedent combined with a high credence in the counterfactual would not generally warrant a high credence in the consequent. (If counterfactuals have ordinary truth conditions, it is not hard to see that a pattern like this would induce failures of modus ponens.) Note also that this would happen rather frequently and occur for all sorts of counterfactuals independently of any specific features of antecedent or consequent. Hence, they could not be assimilated to counterfactual versions of McGee’s () counterexamples to modus ponens, which employ a structurally complex consequent. 

Skyrms (: f.) accepts this consequence and we should at least admit that intuitions may diverge on these cases because counterfactuals with an antecedent known to be true tend to be a little odd, most likely for pragmatic reasons. Although I take modus ponens to be (probabilistically) valid for counterfactuals, a weaker desideratum could, I think, be agreed upon by all parties: it would be nice to have a way of articulating an evaluation constraint on counterfactuals which is flexible enough so that we can have modus ponens if we want it. 

## .Conclusion 

We learned from the discussion of Adams’s epistemic past tense view of counterfactuals that their evaluation should not be tied too closely to the evaluation of indicative conditionals. Counterfactuals do not express what indicative conditionals express either presently or at some past point in time. The discussion of Edgington’s hypothetical belief state approach revealed that it might even be problematic to explain the evaluation of counterfactuals solely in terms of subjective probabilities. They seem to go by something more objective. 

the problem of evaluating counterfactuals  

Skyrms’s proposal solves some of these issues by describing the evaluation of counterfactuals as an estimate of the corresponding conditional objective chances. Some residual issues remain, however. It would be nice to get a clearer picture of the conception of objective chance needed to endorse the evaluation constraint in full generality. Our discussion has shown that the conception of chance involved should be largely indiscriminate between determinism and indeterminism and it should be global in the sense of being well defined for all metaphysically possible antecedents. Furthermore, it would be desirable if it were flexible enough to accommodate Morgenbesser cases and the validity of modus ponens. 

We will see in the next chapter that a notion of _counterfactual chance_ might be the best way of construing the concept of chance in Skyrms’s constraint. We will also see that it is the same notion which has been used by Gärdenfors () to generalize the notion of imaging as introduced by Lewis (). This will define an operation which ultimately gives us something like Edgington’s hypothetical belief states. Modulo minor side conditions, the two ways of developing an evaluation constraint on counterfactuals in terms of counterfactual chance will turn out to be equivalent. 

## **** 

## Counterfactual Chance 

In order to further develop the notion of chance in Skyrms’s proposal, it will be helpful to go back to the informal characterization of how counterfactuals are evaluated. When we ask how likely it is that _B_ would have been true if _A_ had been true, we seem to consider a number of relevant counterfactual scenarios in which the antecedent is true and estimate how many of them make the consequent true. This idea is already contained to some extent in Skyrms’s approach, for oftentimes the objective chances it appeals to will target a set of close antecedent-worlds, but the idea seems to be somewhat more general in not putting any prior constraints on the nature of the relevant antecedent-worlds. We shall see that there are two approaches one can follow in developing the informal characterization further. Common to both approaches are two factors: a conception of relevance and a conception of chance which is designed to possibly weigh the relevant worlds. 

The chapter is structured as follows. I start by discussing selection functions which can be used to describe the notion of relevance. Then a general notion of (metaphysical) chance is introduced which combines with relevance to provide a conception of counterfactual chance. With this in place, the notion of counterfactual chance can be used to formulate an evaluation constraint on counterfactuals which is structurally similar to Lewis’s () Principal Principle. After some further discussion, I will, in the second part of this chapter, take a look at Lewis’s notion of imaging. The chapter closes with some more general considerations about the methodology used in introducing the concepts we need. 

## .The Selection Function 

To begin with, let me introduce the framework of selection functions as described in Lewis (: ch. .). Based on an underlying set _W_ 

counterfactual chance  

of possible worlds, we may introduce a _selection function f_ which takes us from a world _w_ ∈ _W_ and a set of worlds _A_ ⊆ _W_ to a set of possible worlds _fw(A)_ ⊆ _W_ . The selection function is intended to model the notion of _relevance_ : the set _fw(A)_ can be thought of as the set of relevant _A_ -worlds from the perspective of _w_ . So, they are the worlds we should take into consideration when evaluating a counterfactual whose antecedent expresses a proposition true at exactly the worlds in _A_ . For our purposes, we may actually identify propositions with sets of possible worlds. 

There will be more to say on the notion of relevance in play (see section .), but for now it suffices to merely note the structure of how relevance could be modelled in terms of a selection function which assigns to a world _w_ and a proposition _A_ a set of relevant worlds at which _A_ is true. One could think of those worlds as being closest or most similar to _w_ , but as noted in the introduction, this is not necessarily an assumption we have to make. 

I should perhaps also say that I set aside a possible context-dependence of the notion of relevance. If counterfactuals are genuinely contextdependent, a natural place for accommodating this feature would be to let the selection function _f_ be dependent on context, so that the selected set of worlds, _fw(A)_ , could vary between contexts despite the world of evaluation and the input proposition staying constant (cf. the discussion in Lewis : sec. .and .). The reason why I omit representing a possible context-dependence in the semantic model is that I think issues of context-dependence are largely orthogonal to the problem set I am trying to solve. 

Further, let �•� be an _interpretation function_ which assigns to a sentence _φ_ the set of worlds at which _φ_ is true, that is � _φ_ � = { _w_ ∈ _W_ : _w_ |= _φ_ }.[] The interpretation � _φ_ � of a sentence _φ_ specifies the _truth conditions_ of the sentence. According to this conception of truth conditions, two sentences have the same truth conditions if they are true at exactly the same possible worlds or, in other words, if they are necessarily equivalent. Now, given a sentence _φ_ , the set _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ can be thought of as the set of relevant _φ_ -worlds with respect to _w_ . So, the present framework assumes that the set of relevant antecedent-worlds is a function of the truth conditions of the antecedent. If two antecedents are necessarily equivalent, the corresponding sets of relevant antecedent-worlds will be the same. 

>  The formula ⌜ _w_ |= _φ_ ⌝ is the formal expression of ⌜ _φ_ is true at _w_ ⌝. 

 counterfactual chance 

The selection function _f_ should be subjected to a variety of constraints. An obvious one is this: 

**Antecedent-Worlds.** Let _w_ be any world and _A_ a set of worlds. Then _fw(A)_ ⊆ _A_ . 

Simply put: Relevant antecedent-worlds are antecedent-worlds. If _A_ = Ø, i.e. the antecedent is impossible, the constraint implies that _fw(A)_ = Ø, i.e. that there are no relevant antecedent-worlds. This seems plausible. 

If, however, there are worlds satisfying the antecedent, we would like to secure the existence of relevant antecedent-worlds. A straightforward way to achieve this is to require that the selection function selects some antecedent-worlds unless there the antecedent is impossible: 

**Non-Emptiness.** Let _w_ be any world and _A_ a non-empty set of worlds. Then 

**==> picture [47 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

Another plausible constraint is known under the label ‘weak centering’ (cf. Lewis : ). 

**Weak Centering.** Let _w_ be any world in _A_ . Then 

**==> picture [45 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

In words: If the antecedent is true at the world _w_ of evaluation, then _w_ is a relevant antecedent-world. This constraint flows naturally from the idea that relevant antecedent-worlds are worlds which come from the world of evaluation by integrating the antecedent at small costs. If the antecedent is already true at the world of evaluation, the antecedent can be integrated at no costs at all. 

The following constraint is sometimes added as well (we will further discuss it in section ..): 

**Equivalence for Relevance.** Let _w_ be any world, _A_ and _B_ be sets of worlds. If _fw(A)_ ⊆ _B_ and _fw(B)_ ⊆ _A_ , then 

**==> picture [61 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

In words again: If the relevant _A_ -worlds are _B_ -worlds and the relevant _B_ -worlds are _A_ -worlds, then the relevant _A_ -worlds coincide with the relevant _B_ -worlds. Thinking of the relevant worlds as in some sense being the 

counterfactual chance  

closest worlds would motivate this constraint, for if the closest _A_ -worlds are _B_ -worlds and the closest _B_ -worlds are _A_ -worlds, then these sets of worlds must be the same. 

On the present conception, the set of relevant _A_ -worlds will usually contain many worlds. In contrast, Stalnaker () assumed that there is always exactly one relevant _A_ -world, with a possible exception for the case where _A_ is impossible, i.e. the empty set. More precisely, we are looking at this _uniqueness assumption_ : 

**Uniqueness.** Let _w_ be any world in _W_ and _A_ ⊆ _W_ . If _A_ ̸= Ø, then | _fw(A)_ | = . 

Uniqueness tells us that there is always exactly one relevant _A_ -world. If one assumes that relevance goes with what is most similar or closest, the assumption is that there is always a single most similar or closest world. Consequently, there cannot be two equally close worlds or a never-ending chain of closer and closer worlds. We will have to say more about the uniqueness assumption below. 

A straightforward consequence of the uniqueness assumption and weak centering is what has been called ‘strong centering’: 

**Strong Centering.** Let _w_ be any world in _A_ . Then 

**==> picture [54 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

Weak centering tells us that if _A_ is true at the world of evaluation _w_ , then _w_ is a relevant world. But if there cannot be more than one relevant world, then the only relevant world must be _w_ . Strong centering is not implausible even without the uniqueness assumption. The constraint may be derived from the thought that a world is closer to itself than to any other world and so must be the closest antecedent-world in cases in which the antecedent is already true at it. Note also that weak centering is an immediate consequence of strong centering. 

## .Relevance and Chance 

Let us consider a probability function Ch _w_ over the whole set of possible worlds _W_ .[] Given sets of worlds _A_ and _B_ , we may call the conditional 

>  As far as the underlying algebra of propositions is concerned, I assume that it is the powerset of _W_ . Ultimately, this may not be a realistic assumption. We should at least reckon 

 counterfactual chance 

probability Ch _w(B_ | _fw(A))_ the _counterfactual chance of B under A_ (relative to _w_ ). Here we conditionalize a chance function not on the proposition _A_ , but rather on the set of relevant _A_ -worlds _fw(A)_ (which will in general be a proper subset of _A_ ). For worlds _w_ and propositions _A_ , we may write Ch _[A] w[(]_[•] _[)]_[for][Ch] _[w][(]_[•|] _[f][w][(][A][))]_[to][have][an][expression][for][the][counterfactual] chance given a possible antecedent _A_ .[] We presuppose that _A_ is not the empty set, for otherwise the conditional chances would be undefined (more on definedness below). 

The counterfactual chance function weighs the relevant _A_ -worlds. It describes how likely _B_ is made by the relevant _A_ -worlds. Thus, it adds a more fine-grained structure to the set of relevant _A_ -worlds. Where the selection function provides only information about which worlds are relevant, the chance function adds information about how relevant each world is: some worlds may count more than others. Although we have defined counterfactual chances in terms of an absolute probability function Ch _w_ , only the conditional probabilities given sets of relevant worlds will matter, so one could also have started with these conditional probabilities directly (we come back to this below). It is of course somewhat arbitrary to call this notion ‘counterfactual chance’. The label would also have fitted a notion which is directed at the (physical) chance the consequent would have had if the antecedent had been true. But this is not what we shall subsume under the label ‘counterfactual chance’. 

Concerning the nature of this kind of chance, we may construe the chance function as concerning objective matters of fact just like we may take relevance to concern objective relations between possible worlds. But if one prefers, one could probably also see the chance function as encoding certain projections stemming from our practice of counterfactual reasoning in contexts of uncertainty (cf. the picture in Stalnaker ). 

with the possibility of chance gaps, that is propositions for which the chance function is not defined. It could be that some sets are non-measurable for formal reasons (note, though, that we may only have to presuppose finite additivity for Ch _w_ ) or it could be that for certain propositions no plausible chance-value can be found. 

>  The functions Ch _Aw(B)_ corresponds to the functions Gärdenfors (: ), Joyce (: f.), and Lewis () use to generalize the notion of imaging (more on this below). Others have considered similar functions, too. Following Bigelow (), who derives probabilities from a similarity metric over possible worlds, Bennett (: f.) introduces a notion which he calls _relative probability_ . Sobel had a conception of counterfactual chances as well (cf. the report in Lewis : –). 

counterfactual chance  

Such a notion of counterfactual chance has the right profile to accommodate the constraints which emerged from our discussion of Skyrms’s proposal. By having as their domain all metaphysically possible worlds, counterfactual chances are well defined for antecedents which contradict the actual laws of nature or the actual physical probabilities. As counterfactual chances are not identified with physical chances, they can be assumed to be non-trivial even when evaluated from the perspective of a deterministic world. And by being conditionalized on the set of relevant antecedent-worlds rather than, say, the set of all antecedent-worlds, we are free to assume that with respect to Morgenbesser cases only those worlds are relevant at which certain cotenable facts obtain. 

The counterfactual chance function is presently relativized to possible worlds. However, given that the selection function is already relativized in this way, it is not clear whether this is really necessary. For different worlds, the selection function may already take us to different sets of relevant worlds and will thus make sure that the counterfactual chances can be different. Relativizing the chance function to possible worlds would only be necessary if there could be two possible worlds _w_ and _w_[′] such that _fw(A)_ = _fw_ ′ _(A)_ , but for some _B_ , the counterfactual chance of _B_ under _A_ would be different for _w_ and _w_[′] . I cannot think of an example which would indicate that we might need this extra complexity, but neither do I know of any good reason why there should not be any. So, it is merely in order to simplify our discussion that I am mostly going to ignore this possible relativization. 

Let us see what more can be said about counterfactual chances. A basic feature they should have is that the counterfactual chance of a proposition under itself is always . This is already a consequence of the corresponding constraint for the selection function. Given that relevant _A_ -worlds are always _A_ -worlds, any probability function conditionalized on _fw(A)_ will assign probability to _A_ , for _fw(A)_ is a subset of _A_ . So we find: 

**Chance ** . For all _w_ ∈ _W_ and all non-empty _A_ ⊆ _W_ : 

**==> picture [78 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

The above constraint tells us somewhat trivially that _A_ should be accepted if it is entertained counterfactually, i.e. Ch _[A] w[(][A][)]_[ =][ .][We][may] also note that the weak centering condition will have the consequence that if _A_ is true at _w_ , the world _w_ can be given some weight under the 

 counterfactual chance 

counterfactual supposition of _A_ . If strong centering is assumed, then the counterfactual chance becomes extreme for cases in which _w_ |= _A_ : it will assign of to any proposition _B_ depending on whether _w_ |= _B_ , for Ch _[A] w_ would come from conditionalizing on the singleton set { _w_ }. 

So far, we have looked at some immediate consequences for counterfactual chances coming from constraints on the selection function. Let us now turn to some constraints one may wish to impose on the chance function directly. As counterfactual chances are defined by conditionalizing on the set of relevant worlds, only relevant worlds can be assigned positive counterfactual chance. A non-relevant _A_ -world will never be given any weight under the counterfactual supposition of _A_ . Now, the converse claim seems desirable as well: every relevant _A_ -world should be given some weight. So, if _w_ is a relevant _A_ -world, then _w_ should be assigned positive chance under the counterfactual assumption of _A_ . In a way, this may seem to be the whole point of the conception of relevance. A relevant _A_ -world is a world which we should, on ideal reflection, take into consideration in evaluating what would be the case if _A_ were true. Therefore, it seems that the counterfactual chances should not ignore any of the relevant _A_ -worlds. 

More formally, we are are looking at the requirement that 

- _(_[∗] _)_ Ch _w(_ { _w_[′] }| _fw(A)) >_ , for all non-empty sets _A_ and all _w_[′] ∈ _fw(A)_ . 

Any relevant _A_ -world (strictly speaking the corresponding singleton set) would receive positive chance under the counterfactual supposition of _A_ . 

A slightly stronger condition, known as _regularity_ , would be that Ch _w(_ { _w_[′] } _) >_ for all _w_[′] ∈ _W_ . That is to say, our chance function defined over all possible worlds would not ignore any world. If this condition holds, the chance function will assign positive weight to { _w_[′] } under any set of possibilities _A_ containing _w_[′] , for this can only raise but not lower the probability of _w_[′] . The converse does not hold: condition _(_[∗] _)_ does not imply regularity, for the selection function may happen to avoid selection of counterexamples to regularity. It would imply regularity if _(_[∗] _)_ is required to hold for all possible selection functions, or a sufficiently rich set thereof. As far as I can see, the range of possible selection functions we are presently envisaging, that is those which would provide a realistic model of the notion of relevance, is rich enough to inherit any possible problems with regularity (more on this below). 

counterfactual chance  

Regular probability functions assign positive probability to any possibility, that is to any element { _w_ } in their domain. As a consequence, any non-empty set is assigned a positive probability. So, for regular functions, possibility and positive probability coincide. We could then even drop the notion of a relevant _A_ -world and simply define a relevant _A_ -world as a world receiving positive counterfactual chance under _A_ . This would mean that the present theory would only have one primitive notion, the concept of counterfactual chance, with relevance defined in terms of it, where there are currently two independent, though interrelated, notions. 

The problem with regularity is that it cannot always be satisfied by standard probabilities in infinite settings. The issue is typically illustrated with the so-called _dart board problem_ . Suppose there is a dart board with infinitely many points on it and that a dart is thrown at the board in a completely random fashion. We would like to say (a) that it has a positive chance of hitting any of these points and (b) that for any point the chance is the same. But this is impossible in standard probability theory. We cannot assign the same positive probability to all the disjoint events described by ‘the dart hits point _i_ ’, for their disjunction would then sum up to a probability greater than given that there are infinitely many points _i_ (for more discussion, see Williamson ). 

Regularity does not cause any problems in finite settings. In infinite settings, it may be very hard if not impossible to articulate in any plausible way a sense in which counterfactual chances should not ignore relevant worlds.[] Now, if one allows the set of relevant worlds to contain more than one world, typically many worlds, I see no reason why this set could be assumed to be always finite (actually, it might be more realistic to suppose that it is in most cases infinite). To make the point, one could consider an infinite lottery which did not actually take place. What if it had? It seems plausible that there is for any of the infinitely many tickets a relevant world in which that ticket wins. So there would be infinitely many relevant antecedent-worlds. If one were to require, which may seem initially plausible, that all these relevant worlds should receive the same positive chance, then one would look at the same structure as in the dart board problem. For this reason, I do not think that _(_[∗] _)_ can be upheld in standard frameworks for probability. 

>  See Haverkamp and Schulz () for how a regularity constraint might be implemented in a comparative setting. 

 counterfactual chance 

As a matter of fact, I do not think that one has to invoke infinite lotteries in order to make the point. Just think of a standard coin toss case. If the coin had been tossed, which curve in space would its course have described? Arguably, there are infinitely many possible and equally relevant candidates, differing only in minuscule detail. 

In the light of the fact that regularity is problematic in infinite settings but unproblematic in finite environments, it is possible to weaken the constraint _(_[∗] _)_ by restricting it to finite cases: 

**Limited Conditional Regularity.** If _A_ is non-empty and _fw(A)_ finite, then Ch _w(_ { _w_[′] }| _fw(A)) >_ for all _w_[′] ∈ _fw(A)_ . 

The limited constraint applies when the set of relevant antecedent-worlds is finite. In such situations, every relevant world is assumed to receive positive counterfactual chance. This avoids the problems with regularity in infinite settings, but if the suspicision is correct that sets of relevant antecedent-worlds will rarely be finite, the weakened constraint helps shaping our picture of the counterfactual chance function only to a small extent. 

There are two further issues concerning the definedness of the chance function in certain situations. An immediate question is: What if the counterfactual assumption is impossible? More precisely: what should be the value of Ch _[A] w[(][B][)]_[ if] _[ A]_[is the empty][set?][We know from the previous] section that the set of relevant _A_ -worlds, _fw(A)_ , is empty if _A_ is empty. This is as it should be: if there are no _A_ -worlds, there can be no relevant _A_ -worlds. Given that we have defined counterfactual chances by conditionalizing on the set of relevant worlds, we would conditionalize on the empty set which always has probability zero. But according to the standard definition of conditional probabilities (see section .), they are undefined for zero-probability sets. Hence, counterfactual chances will be undefined for impossible assumptions. I suggest setting the counterfactual chances to in this case. So, we would make the following stipulation about counterfactual chances: 

**Vacuity** . For all _w_ ∈ _W_ and all _B_ ⊆ _W_ : Ch[Ø] _w[(][B][)]_[ :][=][ .] This constraint parallels the idea that counterfactuals are vacuously true if the antecedent is impossible. 

For all that has been said so far, the impossibility of an assumption may not be the only reason why counterfactual chances could go undefined. It might also happen that the absolute probability Ch _w(fw(A))_ of 

counterfactual chance  

a non-empty set of relevant worlds is zero. In this case, the counterfactual chance conditional on this set would, according to the standard definition, be undefined. This might be an unwelcome result if this set of worlds corresponds to a counterfactual supposition we are capable of entertaining. So, if there is a non-empty set of relevant _A_ -worlds, the counterfactual chance conditional on _A_ should, if possible, be defined. One condition which would secure this is the regularity constraint mentioned above, for it requires that only the empty set is assigned probability zero. As regularity is unproblematic in finite settings, the present issue is only a possible problem in infinite domains. Perhaps the best way to deal with it in this case would be to take conditional probabilities to be primitive and represent them with something like Popper measures (cf. Hájek ). We could then assume that the conditional probabilities given sets of relevant antecedent-worlds are mostly defined even for zeroprobability sets. Moreover, this would square well with the fact that our grip on counterfactual chances comes from our judgements about the conditional probabilities Ch _w(B_ | _fw(A))_ . We do not seem to have any direct access to absolute probabilities of the form Ch _w(B)_ . 

Lastly, let us consider a possible constraint which would simplify the notion of counterfactual chance greatly. It may seem that in evaluating a counterfactual supposition, we simply estimate the fraction of consequent-worlds among the relevant antecedent-worlds (Bennett : f. seems to make this assumption). For example, it may seem that we think it about  _/_ -likely that the coin would have landed heads under the counterfactual supposition of it having been tossed because we take about half of the toss-worlds to be worlds at which the coin lands heads. This line of thought would lead to the following uniformity condition: 

**Uniformity.** Let _w_ ∈ _W_ and _A_ ⊆ _W_ . If _fw(A)_ is finite and non-empty, then for all _w_ ∈ _W_ and all _B_ ⊆ _W_ : Ch _[A] w[(][B][)]_[ =][|] _[B]_ |[∩] _fw[f] ([w] A[(][A] )_ | _[)]_[|][.] 

The set of relevant _A_ -worlds, that is _fw(A)_ , needs to be finite and non-empty for the fraction to be well defined. As with the regularity constraint, there are technical problems with finding plausible extensions of the uniformity condition which cover the infinite case.[] I will not go into details about possible options. 

>  One problem is that if a probability function over a countably infinite set is countably additive (the probability of a countable union of disjoint sets is the infinite sum of the single probabilities), then there is no uniform distribution over this set. 

 counterfactual chance 

According to uniformity, the counterfactual chance of _B_ under _A_ would be the fraction of _B_ -worlds among the relevant _A_ -worlds. Basically, uniformity requires that every relevant antecedent-world (or every set of worlds of the same size) is assigned the same weight. We will further discuss and tentatively reject the uniformity constraint in section .. It will become apparent that thinking about proportions of consequent-worlds is probably only a good approximation to how counterfactuals are evaluated, but ultimately the real work needs to be done by a conception of counterfactual chances which is not reducible in such a simple way. 

## .A Principal Principle for Counterfactuals 

It seems that our credences in counterfactuals aim at the corresponding counterfactual chances. They specify a kind of ideal for what to think about counterfactuals. On the assumption that the counterfactual chance of the consequent under the antecedent is so-and-so, our subjective probability of the counterfactual should be so-and-so. As it turns out, this yields an evaluation constraint on counterfactuals similar to Lewis’s Principal Principle which says that conditional on the assumption that the objective chances of a proposition are so-and-so, our credence in the proposition should be so-and-so. A Lewis type formulation of the evaluation constraint is merely a different way of representing the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals as weighted sums of chance hypotheses as Skyrms did (modulo, as we shall see, admissible evidence). Hence, despite the difference in form, we continue to look for a sense in which the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals can be explicated as an estimate of a more objective kind of chance, with chance now being interpreted as counterfactual chance. 

I will simply start by presenting the evaluation constraint in the form of the Principal Principle and then explain it step by step. Recall that �•� is an interpretation function which assigns to any sentence _φ_ of a given background language the set of worlds � _φ_ � at which the sentence _φ_ is true. Recall further that a conditional is simple if it does not embed another conditional. 

**PP Constraint.** Let ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ be a simple counterfactual, let _w_ @ be the actual world, let _x_ denote a number in the unit interval, and let _Cr_ 

counterfactual chance  

be a rational initial credence function. Moreover, let _X_ be the sentence ⌜Ch[�] _w[φ]_ @[�] _[(]_[�] _[ψ]_[�] _[)]_[ =] _[ x]_[⌝][and let] _[ E]_[ be admissible. Then] 

**==> picture [113 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

whenever the conditional probabilities are defined. 

To simplify, let us ignore the role of admissible evidence for a moment; we will come back to it in the next section. The evaluation constraint is concerned with the conditional subjective probabilities of an initial epistemic state. Recall that an initial credence function represents an epistemic state which has not yet acquired any empirical information. For such an epistemic state, the evaluation constraint requires the following: conditional on the assumption that the corresponding counterfactual chances are _x_ , we should take the counterfactual to be _x_ -likely. 

As we have seen, counterfactual chances are relativized to worlds. Hence, in the evaluation constraint, a world needs to be specified relative to which the counterfactual chances are taken. The obvious choice is the actual world: when thinking about the counterfactual chances, we are interested in the counterfactual chances with respect to the actual world. This is an instance of the more general fact that when we think about what is the case, we are interested in what is the case at the actual world. Besides, I have restricted the principle to simple counterfactuals, i.e. counterfactuals which do not embed another counterfactual, in order to set aside a discussion of nested counterfactuals for which a special story might be needed.[] 

In contrast to Lewis’s Principal Principle, the present evaluation constraint for counterfactuals describes subjective probabilities as attaching to sentences. As mentioned earlier, this is a helpful way of circumventing the problem that counterfactuals may not have truth conditions. Taking credences to attach to sentences seems to be the most neutral way of expressing that counterfactuals are associated with certain subjective probabilities. To repeat, this is not meant to make an interesting claim about what the objects of credences fundamentally are. 

A minor point. Sentences can be context-sensitive. To do justice to the possible context-sensitivity of sentences, one would need to relativize 

>  The question which would need addressing is whether the import/export principle as introduced by McGee () is valid for counterfactuals. For some discussion, see Etlin (: .). 

 counterfactual chance 

credences to contexts when sentences are taken to be the objects of credences. So, one should speak of the credence a given epistemic subject assigns to a sentence _in a given context_ . I have omitted this further complexity from the evaluation constraint. 

## .Admissible Evidence 

Let us now take a closer look at admissible evidence. Just as with the original Principal Principle, it is clear that some restrictions need to be imposed on which additional assumptions we can make over and above what the counterfactual chances are without altering our verdict about the counterfactual. On the assumption that a fully reliable oracle tells us that ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ is true, our credence in ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ may be raised to and thus be different from what we might take the counterfactual chances to be. Even more clearly, the counterfactual under consideration will itself not be admissible. For instance, on the assumption that the coin would have landed heads if it had been tossed, our credence in this counterfactual should be rather than  _/_ , say, for the simple reason that _P(_ ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝|⌜ _(φ > ψ)_ ∧ _X_ ⌝ _)_ = no matter what _X_ is. Apart from ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝, counterfactuals related to ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ may also be inadmissible, for they may imply the counterfactual, imply its negation, or else have an evidential import on it. In sum, not everything can be admissible. 

It is worth observing that the notion of admissibility we need in the present evaluation constraint on counterfactuals is not the same as the one in the original Principal Principle. It may be helpful to introduce two separate labels at this point. Let _o-admissibility_ be the notion of admissibility required for the **o** riginal Principal Principle and let _c-admissibility_ be the notion we will need for the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. Perhaps the first thing to observe is that _c_ -admissibility is not time-relative. _O_ -admissibility is time-relative because objective chances are time-relative. If the objective chances are taken at a time _t_ , then a piece of information _E_ about a time _t_[′] after _t_ will typically be inadmissible as long as it is not independent of the target proposition. However, it may become admissible when the chances are taken relative to a point _t_[′′] later than _t_[′] . In contrast, counterfactual chances are not time-relative: what the counterfactual chances are may vary from world to world, but it does not vary with time. Formally, this is reflected in the fact that the 

counterfactual chance  

counterfactual chance function has a world-slot but not a time-slot. This is not to deny that counterfactuals may stand in an interesting relation to time. The antecedent of a counterfactual typically makes salient a point in time onwards from which the relevant counterfactual worlds are allowed to depart from the actual history. But this point is already fixed by the proposition expressed by the antecedent of the counterfactual, it it is not assumed to vary over time.[] 

A consequence of the temporal invariance of counterfactual chances is that whole world histories seem to be _c_ -admissible, whereas only certain chunks of history are _o_ -admissible at every time. Even if we know the whole history of a world but assume that only about half of the relevant antecedent-worlds make the consequent true, we should have an intermediate credence in the corresponding counterfactual. We need to be very cautious in making an assumption like this, though. On a semantic conception of possible worlds, on which a possible world closely corresponds to a totality of facts, the history of a world _w_ should not be identified with the world _w_ itself, for otherwise taking histories to be _c_ -admissible would have the consequence that every _w_ -truth is _c_ -admissible, including truths about counterfactuals, because on a semantic picture of possible worlds, they decide all propositions. In particular, if a given counterfactual is true at _w_ , it would itself be _c_ -admissible. But as we saw above, this cannot be the case. In saying that whole histories are _c_ -admissible, histories must be understood in a weaker sense according to which they only specify information about the course of spatio-temporal events taking place in the world.[] 

Information about the laws of nature seems to be both _o_ -admissible and _c_ -admissible. However, as far as the _c_ -admissibility of objective chances is concerned, we have to be careful. The role of objective chance in the original Principal Principle is played by conditional counterfactual chance in the evaluation constraint for counterfactuals. And information about (conditional) counterfactual chance will be _c_ -admissible just like 

>  One might want to allow the relevant point in time to be shiftable by the context of utterance. Even if this possibility were granted, this would not show that _o_ -admissibility is time-relative in the sense intended here. 

>  It might be possible to think of histories as possible worlds in Lewis’s (a) sense according to which they are maximally connected spatio-temporal objects. Such objects need not be assumed to decide all propositions, yet if one construes propositions as sets consisting exclusively of Lewis-worlds, they would do so. 

 counterfactual chance 

information about (unconditional) objective chance is _o_ -admissible. But the extent to which information about objective chance is _c_ -admissible will be somewhat limited. To illustrate why, suppose that at a certain time _t_ , it is determinate at a world _w_ that a given counterfactual is true.[] Then the objective chance at _t_ of the counterfactual being true will be . This information cannot be _c_ -admissible, for it would directly encode the truth-value of the counterfactual. Hence, particular information about objective chances of counterfactuals cannot always be _c_ -admissible. Note that this does not exclude information about objective chances of basic non-counterfactual propositions. Neither does it exclude information about the conditional objective chances of the consequent of a counterfactual given its antecedent. What will sometimes be excluded is information about the absolute chance of a counterfactual being true. 

As a matter of fact, it may well be the case that every piece of information which we can come to possess is _c_ -admissible. For instance, it is doubtful that we can come to know that there is a fully reliable oracle which announces that the coin would have come up heads if it had been tossed (if there can be such an oracle at all). It may even be that there is no way for us to know whether the coin would have come up heads if it had been tossed (this will be discussed more thoroughly in chapter ). So, although the assumption that the coin would have landed heads is inadmissible for the evaluation of various counterfactuals, in particular for the evaluation of ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads’, it seems that we cannot come to know this assumption. However, even if the present conjecture that we cannot come to know _c_ -inadmissible evidence is true, permitting only _c_ -admissible evidence and not all kinds of information in the evaluation constraint will still be the strongest thing we can do, for the evaluation constraint is concerned with assumptions we may make. And we can suppose things to be true which we cannot come to know. But if _c_ -inadmissible information is not in our epistemic reach, there will never be the need

But due to problems with regularity, one cannot assume that chance  _/_ implies determinacy. For this reason, only the converse of this claim is a plausible constraint: if it is determinate at _t_ whether _p_ , then the objective chance of _p_ at _t_ is either or . 

counterfactual chance  

to apply the notion of admissibility to a particular case which is concerned with our absolute, i.e. non-conditional, credences in counterfactuals. This would greatly diminish the importance of having such a notion. This issue will concern us again in sections .and .when we compare the present constraint with Skyrms’s proposal and the imaging constraint, which are both formulated without reference to a notion of admissibility. 

The fact that the original Principal Principle is stated in terms of the notion of admissibility is sometimes seen as a disadvantage, for it seems hard to get a sufficiently clear grip on this concept. (Recall, however, that utilizing the notion of admissibility is meant to generalize the principle. There is no problem in stating a weaker constraint without recourse to admissibility.) The same worry would apply to the present version of the evaluation constraint. (Note, however, that just as with the original principle, a weaker constraint without admissibility would be available by not permitting admissible evidence as a further conjunct.) Now, Lewis (: –) already reformulates the Principal Principle in a way which frees it from the notion of admissibility and he further improves on this principle in Lewis () to deal with the problem of undermining. I will briefly sketch this reformulation and then see whether we can make a similar move in the counterfactual case. 

The basic idea is to explicate the relation between chance and credence exclusively in terms of histories and a theory of chance. We have already made use of histories: a history _Ht_ describes a chunk of a world up to a certain time _t_ such that _Ht_ is true at two worlds _w_ and _w_[′] iff they share their history up to _t_ . A theory of chance _T_ describes how the objective chances in a world depend on the history of the world up to any time _t_ . So, for any history _Ht_ , a theory of chance will tell us what the objective chances are at the end of _Ht_ , i.e. at _t_ . A theory of chance can be implemented in various ways. Lewis () takes them to consist in the conjunction of all history-to-chance conditionals of the form ⌜ _Ht_ → _Pt(A)_ = _x_ ⌝, which tell us what the chances of a proposition _A_ are at a time _t_ when given a history up to time _t_ as input and where ‘→’ is a ‘strong conditional’ of some sort, possibly a counterfactual as in Lewis (). Alternatively, one could think of _T_ as specifying a global chance function for a world which delivers the chances at a time _t_ when conditionalized on the history up to time _t_ . If a particular history _Ht_ is combined with a theory of chance, then this yields a verdict about the objective chances at _t_ . Assuming further 

 counterfactual chance 

that histories and theories of chance are admissible, the original Principal Principle implies this simplification: 

**==> picture [115 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

where _Pt(A)_ is the chance of _A_ at _t_ as implied by ⌜ _Ht_ ∧ _T_ ⌝, _Ht_ specifies a history up to _t_ and _T_ is a theory of chance. What this says is simply: conditional on a history and a theory of chance, we should adjust our credence in a proposition _A_ to what the theory of chance says about the objective chances at _t_ given _Ht_ . Here we no longer use the notion of admissibility, although it is implicit in the justification of this variant that histories and theories of chance are admissible. 

This variant of the Principal Principle has been turned into the ‘New Principle’ (see Hall and Lewis ) in response to the problem of undermining. The problem of undermining arises if we assume in a Humean spirit that chance supervenes on particular matters of fact. Then it might be possible that at a given time _t_ a certain course of events _F_ has a positive chance of occurring which, were it to occur, would undermine the chances at _t_ , i.e. the chances at _t_ would be different from what they actually are. Given that _T_ would be undermined by _F_ , _T_ is actually incompatible with _F_ , for _F_ makes true a theory of chance incompatible with _T_ . Hence, conditional on _T_ , we should invest zero credence that _F_ will occur. On the other hand, the Principal Principle suggests that we should have a positive credence in _F_ , for _F_ has a positive chance according to _T_ . If undermining is possible, the latter principle seems to be false (see Thau for discussion of how the problem with undermining might generalize to non-supervenient conceptions of chance). The fix suggested by Hall () and Lewis () is to conditionalize the chances on the theory of chance, too. Thus we would get: 

**==> picture [125 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

The effect of this is to ignore undermining futures like _F_ : conditional on _T_ , they are no longer assigned a positive chance, for they are incompatible with _T_ . Although there would be more to say here, this brief sketch should do, for we shall soon see that the possibility of undermining has no analogue in counterfactual chances. 

Is there a principle similar in form to () which could substitute the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals? A natural way to approach this 

counterfactual chance  

question is to see what the analogues of a history and a theory of chance might be in the case of counterfactuals. 

As counterfactuals are not time-relative, there is no natural counterpart of a history in the evaluation of counterfactuals. Incidentally, the absence of time-relativity is also the reason why we do not have to worry about something like undermining. Without any futures, there are not going to be any undermining futures. Now, to some extent, the role of a history is played by the concept of relevance in the evaluation of counterfactuals (and which worlds are relevant depends to a large extent on the history of the world). The set of relevant worlds is what the counterfactual chances are relative to. If the relevant worlds share a common history, they can even be seen as specifying a history up to a time from which the counterfactual worlds are allowed to depart from the actual world. Let a theory of relevance be a theory which uniquely describes a selection function which provides for any world _w_ and proposition _A_ a set of relevant _A_ -worlds relative to _w_ . 

It is evident that the role of objective chances in the original Principal Principle is played by counterfactual chances in the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. In an analogous fashion, we may introduce theories of counterfactual chance which uniquely specify a chance function Ch which delivers, when conditionalized on a set of relevant _A_ -worlds, the counterfactual chance of _B_ under _A_ . If we now take the two components together, we can reformulate the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals in a similar fashion. 

So, let _R_ be a theory of relevance and let _T_ be a theory of counterfactual chance. Given propositions _A_ and _B_ , the conjunction of _R_ and _T_ will imply that the counterfactual chance of _B_ under _A_ is Ch _[A] (B)_ , for some counterfactual chance function Ch (I omit a relativization to worlds). So, for any reasonable credence function _Cr_ , we could require 

() Ch _[A] (B)_ = _Cr(A > B_ | _R_ ∧ _T)_ . 

Conditional on a conjunction of a theory of relevance and a theory of counterfactual chance, we should think about a counterfactual what the theory says about the counterfactual chance of the consequent conditional on the set of relevant antecedent-worlds. On the plausible assumption that a theory of relevance and a theory of counterfactual chance are admissible, this principle follows from the earlier PP-constraint just like the reformulation of the original Principal Principle follows from 

 counterfactual chance 

the latter. Thus, we gain a version of the constraint which no longer employs the notion of admissibility. In practice, considerations about admissibility are still going to be relevant, though, for in deciding whether the credence a given subject invests in a counterfactual can be justified by the principle, we do not get around the issue of deciding whether the subject possesses any inadmissible evidence. 

## .Comparison with Skyrms 

The PP-constraint closely mirrors the idea that evaluating a counterfactual amounts to an _estimate_ of the corresponding chances. Let us assume that our present credences _Cr_ come from an initial probability function _P_ conditionalized on the evidence _E_ we currently possess. Applied to a counterfactual, this gives us 

() _Cr(A > B)_ = _P(A > B_ | _E)_ . 

Representing our present credences in terms of an initial credence function makes the PP-constraint applicable. We may now partition _E_ according to the hypotheses _Hx_ compatible with _E_ which say for some _x_ that the counterfactual chance Ch _[A] w_ @ _[(][B][)]_[ is] _[ x]_[. Suppose this partition is finite. By the] laws of probability, we then have

In words: if we partition our evidence _E_ , then in order to see how likely _E_ makes a given statement, we may check how likely each partition cell makes this statement, weight this with the likelihood of the cell given _E_ and take the sum over all cells. 

Recall that _Hx_ specifies that Ch _[A] w_ @ _[(][B][)]_[ =] _[ x]_[. Given that] _[ Cr]_[ comes from] _P_ by conditionalizing on _E_ , we therefore have 

**==> picture [144 x 14] intentionally omitted <==**

Suppose further that no _Hx_ contains any inadmissible evidence. Then the PP-constraint is applicable to _P(A > B_ | _Hx)_ , which yields 

() _P(A > B_ | _Hx)_ = _x_ .
