counterfactual chance  

**==> picture [181 x 14] intentionally omitted <==**

So, in evaluating a counterfactual, we would estimate the counterfactual chances of the consequent given the antecedent by considering all possible candidate hypotheses about the counterfactual chances, see what they would prescribe, and weight the result by our credence that the hypotheses get it right. Although we needed a few steps to show that this follows from the PP-constraint, the fact that it follows is almost obvious. The PP-constraint tells us what to think under various hypotheses about the counterfactual chances. So, our unconditional judgement should be a weighted average of what the hypotheses prescribe. 

Structurally, this is exactly Skyrms’s constraint for counterfactuals with the notion of chance in his proposal being interpreted as counterfactual chance. So, modulo admissible evidence, the PP-constraint can be seen as a variant of Skyrms’s proposal. We should note, though, that if it is possible to possess inadmissible evidence, then this variant of Skyrms’s suggestion might give the wrong verdict, for it does not take such a possibility into account. It could then recommend uncertainty about a counterfactual despite the fact that we possess conclusive (inadmissible) evidence for or against the counterfactual. So, this variant can hold in full generality only under the proviso that it is not possible to acquire inadmissible evidence. 

## .Imaging 

There is an alternative route to an evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. It has a very natural motivation in term of suppositions (cf. Joyce : ch. ). Adams’s thesis can be seen as describing the subjective probabilities of indicative conditionals as probabilities of the consequent arrived at under the _indicative supposition_ of the antecedent. The idea would be that conditionalizing on an assumption adequately captures the effect an indicative supposition of the form ‘Suppose such-and-such _is_ the case’ has on a belief state. Given such a thought, it is a natural question whether we can arrive at an interesting evaluation constraint on counterfactuals by investigating the impact a counterfactual supposition of the form ‘Suppose such-and-such _were_ the case’ has on a belief state. It is clear that the two kinds of suppositions differ. We arrive at different belief states when we suppose indicatively that Oswald did not kill Kennedy or when we suppose counterfactually that he had not done it. Under an indicative 

 counterfactual chance 

supposition we hold on to what we already believe and merely add the indicative supposition to our stock of beliefs, whereas we often have to give up certain beliefs when integrating a counterfactual supposition. 

Lewis (: –) discusses an operation he calls _imaging_ which provides an interesting model of the effect a counterfactual assumption might have on a belief state (he further elaborates on it in Lewis ). Lewis conducts his discussion in the context of a Stalnaker semantics for conditionals. For this reason, it is presupposed that the uniqueness assumption holds. This means that there is to any world _w_ and proposition _A_ (which is not impossible) a unique world _wA_ , which is the only relevant _A_ -world. With the uniqueness assumption in place, it is not very hard to see how imaging works. The basic idea is simply that the subjective probability of _w_ gets shifted to _wA_ . For an epistemically possible world _w_ , we may ask what it would be like if _A_ had been true. This will take us to _wA_ . For another world _w_[′] , we may ask the same question. This will take us to _w_[′] _A_[. Doing this for all epistemically possible worlds, we arrive] at a set of worlds _w[i] A_[which are the epistemically possible candidates for] how the world would have been like if _A_ had been true. So, they are the worlds we should consider possible under the counterfactual supposition of _A_ . How much weight should be given to one of these worlds? Exactly as much weight was given to the worlds it originated from as the single relevant antecedent-world. 

To describe this formally, we follow Lewis in assuming, for simplicity, that the background set of possible worlds _W_ is finite. We also set aside the case in which _A_ is the empty set, i.e. the antecedent is impossible. Then given a probability distribution _P_ over _W_ representing a possible epistemic state, we can define the _image P[A] of P under A_ by setting 

**==> picture [99 x 24] intentionally omitted <==**

In this definition, the probability of a world _w_ gets shifted to the world _wA_ . Given that a world can be the closest _A_ -world for more than one world, a world _w_[′] may receive the probability of more than one epistemically possible world, which explains why we need to sum over all these worlds. Now, this defines the new probability of a single world _w_[′] . The probability of a whole set of worlds _B_ is then given by _P[A] (B)_ :=[�] _w_[′] ∈ _B[P][A][(][w]_[′] _[)]_[, i.e.] simply by the sum of probabilities over the worlds in _B_ . The image of _B_ under _A_ , _P[A] (B)_ , then describes how likely _B_ is under the counterfactual 

counterfactual chance  

supposition of _A_ . It is straightforward to verify that the image of _P_ is again a probability function, for the probabilities are only redistributed among the worlds in _W_ . 

Imaging seems to be a good candidate for describing the probabilities of counterfactuals. We would be looking at the hypothesis that 

**==> picture [80 x 12] intentionally omitted <==**

According to this idea, the subjective probability of a counterfactual is the probability of the consequent under the image of the antecedent. Structurally, this parallels Adams’s thesis for indicative conditionals. The difference is that instead of the conditional probability of the consequent given the antecedent, we have the probability of the consequent under the image of the antecedent. We have already seen that conditionalizing and imaging describe different processes (see Lewis : for a numerical example). 

The only problem with imaging is that it presupposes uniqueness. And the uniqueness assumption is, without any qualifications, not very plausible. Take a coin toss case with a fair coin and assume everything else is normal. Why should a heads-world be closer than a tails-world (or _vice versa_ )? It seems that no matter how we understand the notion of relevance or closeness, it will allow for ties: two worlds whose differences regarding the actual world are perfectly symmetrical. To reiterate with an example by Lewis (: ): is the closest world at which Verdi and Bizet are compatriots a world where Verdi is French or a world where Bizet is Italian? The two different ways of making them compatriots seem to require an equidistant departure from the actual world.[] 

It would therefore be nice to develop the notion of imaging without having to presuppose uniqueness. Simply dropping the uniqueness assumption would, however, put a hole in the definition of imaging: when we have a whole set _fw(A)_ of relevant worlds, we can no longer shift the probability of _w_ to a single closest world. What we would like to do, of course, is to shift it to the set _fw(A)_ . The question which arises is how the 

>  We are going to further discuss the uniqueness assumption in chapter . Perhaps I should warn the reader that although I am not going to adopt the uniqueness assumption for relevance, I will still defend a kind of Stalnaker semantics, so my doubts about uniqueness should not be taken to apply to Stalnaker type semantics in general. At this point, this mindset may seem somewhat paradoxical, but the air of paradoxicality will vanish once the general shape of the theory becomes clear. 

 counterfactual chance 

probability shifted to the set _fw(A)_ will be distributed over its members. Will any member be given equal share or are some worlds going to carry more weight than others? This is the same question which motivated the introduction of counterfactual chances. As we shall see in the next section, with the aid of counterfactual chances, imaging can easily be freed from the uniqueness assumption. 

## .Generalized Imaging 

Based on our conception of counterfactual chances, we can, as Gärdenfors (: –) has shown, define a generalized form of imaging (see also Joyce : ch. and Lewis : –). As indicated above, the idea would be to shift the subjective probability of a world _w_ to the set of relevant _A_ -worlds _fw(A)_ and then weight those worlds with the counterfactual chance Ch _[A] w[(]_[•] _[)]_[. Making a counterfactual supposition] _[ A]_[ would] thus be described in the following way. For every epistemic possibility _w_ , we would consider the set of _A_ -worlds, relevant from the perspective of _w_ , and weight these worlds with the counterfactual chance under _A_ . In order to arrive at the ultimate suppositional belief state, we would perform this procedure for every epistemic possibility _w_ and weight the results with the subjective probability of _w_ . What to think under a counterfactual supposition _A_ would depend on two factors: (i) what we take the actual world to be like and (ii) what we take the counterfactual chance under _A_ to be for every way the actual world might be like. Although not simple, this way of describing counterfactual suppositions comes with a reasonable amount of phenomenological plausibility. 

Formally, the _generalized image P[A] of P under A_ (I continue to use the symbol ‘ _P[A]_ ’ also for the generalized operation) can be defined by setting

For simplicity, we assume here that the underlying set of worlds is finite, so that the sum is always defined (otherwise the constraint would have to be explicated in terms of integrals). As before, the probability of an arbitrary proposition _B_ under the image of _A_ is defined by simply summing over the elements in _B_ , i.e. by setting _P[A] (B)_ :=[�] _w_[′] ∈ _B[P][A][(][w]_[′] _[)]_[.] 

Interpreted with generalized imaging in mind, the imaging constraint from the previous section would say that the subjective probability of a 

counterfactual chance  

counterfactual is the probability of the consequent evaluated from the perspective of the generalized image of the antecedent. More precisely, the hypothesis would be this: 

**Imaging Constraint.** Let ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ be a counterfactual whose antecedent is not impossible, i.e. � _A_ � ̸= Ø, and let _P_ be a subjective probability distribution. Then 

**==> picture [80 x 12] intentionally omitted <==**

Generalized imaging keeps all the virtues of imaging, but it no longer relies on the uniqueness assumption concerning relevant worlds. If we assume uniqueness, then the counterfactual chances will be extreme and assign probability to the single most relevant _A_ -world _wA_ and to all other worlds, for they would come from conditionalizing on _wA_ . So, the chance term will reduce to for _w_[′] = _wA_ and is otherwise, showing that generalized imaging does indeed generalize the original notion of imaging.[] 

Structurally, the epistemic state _P[A]_ representing what to think under the counterfactual supposition of _A_ can be seen as the hypothetical belief state Adams and Edgington were after. Added to the picture are counterfactual chances.[] Another difference is that the hypothetical belief state _P[A]_ makes the antecedent certain, i.e. _P[A] (A)_ = . This means that the idea of Adams and Edgington to evaluate a counterfactual like the corresponding indicative conditional in a related hypothetical belief state would get somewhat trivialized. With the antecedent _A_ being certain, evaluating an indicative conditional ⌜ _A_ ⇒ _B_ ⌝ in _P[A]_ would reduce to merely evaluating the probability of the consequent _B_ . This holds for the Adams/Edgington view on which an indicative conditional gets evaluated by the corresponding conditional probability, for if a probability function _P_ is such that _P(A)_ = , then _P(B_ | _A)_ = _P(B)_ . But this reduction also holds for most other analyses of indicative conditionals. All that it pre-

>  Edgington (: ) comes close to suggesting that objective chances provide something like the _right thing to believe_ about a given counterfactual. See also Edgington (: f.), where hypothetical belief states are compared to probability distributions over sets of relevant antecedent-worlds. 

 counterfactual chance 

supposes is that the conditional satisfies modus ponens and is implied by the conjunction of antecedent and consequent, for then, given _P(A)_ = , _P(A_ ⇒ _B)_ ≤ _P(B)_ (by modus ponens) and _P(B)_ ≤ _P(A_ ⇒ _B)_ (by the conjunctive property). Thus, in no interesting sense would the evaluation of counterfactuals depend on the evaluation of indicative conditionals. What could be kept from Adams and Edgington is the idea that the evaluation of counterfactuals takes us to a hypothetical belief state by counterfactually supposing the antecedent. 

## .Imaging and the PP-Constraint 

There are two ways we can arrive at an evaluation constraint for counterfactuals. Imaging shifts the subjective probability of an epistemically possible world _w_ to the set of relevant _A_ -worlds relative to _w_ , weighted by the counterfactual chance under _A_ . Alternatively, we can require, in the form of a Principal Principle, that for every hypothesis that the counterfactual chances are a certain way, our credence in the counterfactual should be this way. The two approaches surely seem similar. Might they actually amount to the same thing? As we shall see, the answer is a qualified ‘Yes’: in the absence of inadmissible evidence and ignoring a special kind of uncertainty about counterfactual chances, the two constraints agree with each other. But without these assumptions, they may differ and when they do, it seems the Principal Principle type formulation is the safer bet. 

Let us start with the role of admissibility. Here the situation is similar to how the PP-constraint compares to Skyrms’s proposal. If it is possible to possess inadmissible evidence, the imaging constraint is in danger of making the wrong predictions. As mentioned earlier, however, it is unclear whether we can ever come to possess inadmissible evidence. Let me elaborate on this a little more. Recall that we are talking about _c_ -admissibility here. It is clear that we can sometimes acquire evidence which is _o_ -inadmissible at a certain time _t_ . As time goes by, a lot of what used to be the future becomes known. It can therefore trump the information about the objective chances at _t_ . But whether we can receive evidence about counterfactuals which can trump the information provided by the counterfactual chances is another question. An oracle might tell us that a counterfactual is true. Can this kind of testimony ever yield knowledge? 

counterfactual chance  

If knowledge by testimony requires knowledge by the testifying subject, the question would be how the oracle can know. Unless we postulate some magical access to counterfactual facts, even an oracle probably has to make a counterfactual supposition and see what follows. But then it would be in the same business as we are and can be expected to comply with the evaluation constraint.[] 

The issue of testimonial knowledge through oracles is not easy to decide, for the epistemic capacities of an oracle usually remain underspecified. Moreover, not every kind of oracle may be metaphysically possible. One could construe oracles as enhanced versions of human beings with perfect logical skills and radically more computational power. As a helpful analogy, we may apply this to the position of an epistemicist about vagueness holding that vague sentences express propositions which are true or false as the case may be but which are unknowable by epistemic subjects like us in situations like ours (cf. Williamson ). Although the facts registered by vague sentences are in a certain sense unknowable, the epistemicist is not committed to hold that these facts could not be known by oracles construed as enhanced versions of human beings, for the unknowability of those facts may owe itself to the various limitations of our epistemic capacities. The question would then be whether the case of counterfactuals is similar to the case of vagueness. Is it merely due to our limited epistemic capacities that we cannot know how the coin would have landed (assuming here that there is such a matter of fact)? If one answers ‘Yes’, perhaps because one thinks that the uncertainty about such counterfactuals actually stems from vagueness, then an oracle could probably tell us the truth-value of such counterfactuals. If one answers ‘No’, perhaps because one thinks, as I do, that there is a more principled barrier to knowing such counterfactuals (for more, see chapters and ), then even an oracle may not be able to know. 

Testimony could also be a challenge for the imaging constraint in a less eccentric way. Suppose someone tells us that if a given coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads. Suppose further that she knows that the coin is some kind of trick coin which always comes up heads. We, on the other hand, do not know this. So antecedently to the testimonial 

>  If instead of knowledge we would talk about what can rationally be believed, the situation might change, for it might be that we can rationally believe an inadmissible proposition even if it is not an object of possible knowledge. 

 counterfactual chance 

information we had—in accordance with the imaging constraint—a credence of about  _/_ in the counterfactual. Now suppose we have no reason to distrust our informant and therefore come to believe the counterfactual. As a result, our credence in the counterfactual will now be high or even be . In our new epistemic state, are we still satisfying the imaging constraint? One may think that we do not by assuming that the probability of the consequent under the image of the antecedent has not changed. Thus, one might conclude that we have acquired a piece of inadmissible information. However, the situation is more complicated than it may seem. The problem is that the information at hand also allows us to make indirect inferences about what the world must be like for the counterfactual being known by our informant. Based on our knowledge (or high credence) that our informant must have inferred the truth of the counterfactual somehow, we could conclude that the coin cannot be fair or else the chance process cannot have been a relevantly indeterministic one. But once we have updated also our more basic evidence concerning matters of fact which might ground the truth of the counterfactual, it might be that we are again satisfying the imaging constraint, for all ways of integrating the counterfactual supposition that the coin had been tossed will now be such that the coin is somehow determined to come up heads. It could thus be that the imaging constraint is in good shape even with respect to possible evidence from testimony. If not, we could always fall back on the more cautious PP-constraint. 

Let us now turn to another possible difference. Imaging encapsulates the assumption that once we fix a world _w_ , there can be no uncertainty about what the counterfactual chances relative to _w_ are. For, in defining the image from the perspective of an epistemically possible world, we simply take the right counterfactual chances. But one may think that even given a world, we may still be uncertain about which worlds are the relevant antecedent-worlds and how they should be weighted. In contrast, the reformulation of the PP-constraint reveals that there does not seem to be a similar restriction on ways of being uncertain about the counterfactual chances, for the uncertainty about chances is a building block of this constraint. Whether this is a real difference might ultimately depend on our conception of a possible world in this context. The particular kind of uncertainty seems possible because we think of a world as something like a history of basic events or facts, which may leave it open in some cases what the counterfactual chances are. But possible worlds are often 

counterfactual chance  

construed as entities which simply decide every question. Thought of in this way, they will also fix the counterfactual chances. Epistemologically, the first conception sometimes seems more natural, but the second one is the one standardly used in semantics and metaphysics. We do not have to decide this question. This is in any event at best a minor difference, which is not going to matter for what follows. 

Modulo the two possible, but perhaps only apparent differences, the PP-constraint and the constraint in terms of imaging coincide. If we ignore admissible evidence, we only have to see that the imaging constraint is equivalent to the reformulation of the PP-constraint as an estimate of the counterfactual chances. We saw in section .that in this case the PP-constraint is equivalent to 

**==> picture [181 x 14] intentionally omitted <==**

Compare this with the constraint from generalized imaging: 

**==> picture [194 x 26] intentionally omitted <==**

Let us simplify for a moment the discussion by assuming that _B_ is a maximally specific proposition corresponding to a single possible world. Then we can ignore the outer sum in the last term. Now, if we also set aside possible uncertainty about the counterfactual chances relative to a world, we can rewrite the credence in a chance hypothesis as the sum of the credences in the worlds making this chance hypothesis true: 

**==> picture [250 x 29] intentionally omitted <==**

As in every world exactly one chance hypothesis is true, the double sum in the second term sums over all worlds without counting any world twice. Hence, it simplifies to[�] _w_[, which shows that] 

**==> picture [228 x 14] intentionally omitted <==**

This gives us the equivalence between the imaging constraint and the PP-constraint for the special case that the consequent of the counterfactual is maximally specific. 

For the more general fact, we merely have to establish that the PP-constraint prescribes to sum over disjoint propositions in the 

 counterfactual chance 

consequent of a counterfactual, i.e. that the subjective credence in ⌜ _A > (B_ ∨ _C)_ ⌝ for disjoint _B_ and _C_ is the sum of the credences in ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ and ⌜ _A > C_ ⌝. Then summing in () over the worlds in _B_ would indeed give us the credence of ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ as in (). To see this, we merely have to recall that the counterfactual chance function is a probability function and is as such additive, i.e. sums over disjoint propositions. Now, take a theory of counterfactual chance, _T_ . If we knew such a theory to be true, our credences in counterfactuals would, by the PP-constraint, match the values of the chance function and thus be additive too. But this also holds under the mere assumption of a theory _T_ . Now, as our ultimate credences in counterfactuals can be represented—ignoring admissibility—as a weighted average of our credences in theories of counterfactual chance like _T_ and the values of the resulting counterfactual chance function, this generalizes to the case where we are uncertain about the counterfactual chances. To put it another way: what holds under every assumption of a partition also holds unconditionally. 

In conclusion, the two constraints are equivalent given the two provisos that admissible evidence can be ignored and that the particular kind of uncertainty about world-to-counterfactual-chance dependence does not occur. Given this equivalence, I will in what follows often speak indiscriminately about ‘the evaluation constraint’ meaning either of the two constraints. 

## .Some Methodological Considerations 

Before closing I would like to make a few methodological comments. The aim of the present chapter was to find a constraint which systematizes the intuitive data concerning our credences in counterfactuals. I think the imaging constraint and the PP-constraint, which are for all means and purposes equivalent, achieve this aim in a satisfactory way. They are explicated in terms of the two concepts of relevance and chance. Jointly, they define a notion of counterfactual chance which weights the relevant antecedent-worlds. Counterfactual chances can be seen as an enhanced notion of relevance which does not merely identify the relevant antecedent-worlds but also assigns them certain weights. According to this picture, relevance comes in degrees. As discussed earlier, one could have a conceptually more economical approach by starting with 

counterfactual chance  

counterfactual chances and then define relevance as positive counterfactual chance. Because this strategy is as it stands limited to the finite case, I have opted for the slightly less economical approach which starts with two notions: a notion of relevance and a notion of chance. The idea in the background remains one of a graded conception of relevance. 

Grading the notion of relevance might be a cost the present theory comes with, for it introduces a further concept, the notion of chance, in order to add additional structure to the notion of relevance. All things being equal, it would be better to be able to do without this extra complexity. But currently I do not see how this might be possible. The two different types of approaches, generalizing imaging and generalizing Skyrms’s proposal in the form of a Principal Principle, both need a notion of counterfactual chance. The other existing accounts are either inadequate (Adams’s prior probabilities) or involve a concept of similar complexity (Edgington’s hypothetical belief states). So I think we have good inductive evidence that something like counterfactual chances are actually necessary to properly explicate the way counterfactuals are evaluated. 

There is another worry one might have about the theory in its present shape. Although we laid down a number of formal constraints on the selection function and on the chance function, we have not said much about the actual content of these notions. Now, asking for a definition would be asking too much, for these notions will be part of the primitive building blocks of the ultimate theory. Every theory is based on some primitive terms. But let me try to say a little more. 

The debate about the notion of similarity figuring in standard semantics for counterfactuals displays some general trends (see e.g. Lewis , and Williams for some discussion in the context of objective chance). Although I prefer to use ‘relevance’ instead of ‘similarity’, the results matter just the same. So, for instance, we can say that relevant antecedent-worlds typically share most or even all of their history with the actual world up to a certain point shortly before the antecedent is made true. We can say that relevant antecedent-worlds do not violate the actual laws of nature from the time on at which the antecedent is made true (or even at no time at all if we follow Bennett’s suggestion), provided that the antecedent is compatible with the laws of nature. We can also say that the antecedent is integrated at relatively small costs by preserving as many actual facts which are causally independent of it (the latter is something 

 counterfactual chance 

one would probably not want to say if one wishes to analyse causation in terms of counterfactuals). I do not go into detail about these issues, not because they would not matter to the present project, but because I feel I cannot add much to this debate. Whatever can plausibly be said about similarity as employed in standard semantics is something which can, by default, be said about the notion of relevance as employed in the evaluation constraints, too (there are some possible exceptions to this general rule which we shall have to discuss in chapter ; see e.g. .for a discussion of the limit assumption). 

Methodologically, the approach I favour for getting a grip on the desired notions of relevance and chance is to start with our credences in counterfactuals regarding particular cases and then ask what relevance and chance must be like to predict these credences via the evaluation constraints. This strategy is close in spirit to how Lewis conceived of how we should develop an adequate notion of similarity (‘Analysis ’ in the quote to follow refers to his analysis of the truth-conditions of counterfactuals in terms of overall similarity of worlds): 

The thing to do is not to start by deciding, once and for all, what we think about similarity of worlds, so that we can afterwards use these decisions to test Analysis . Rather, we must use what we know about the truth and the falsity of counterfactuals to see if we can find some sort of similarity relation—not necessarily the first one that springs to mind—that combines with Analysis to yield the proper truth conditions.[ _. . ._ ] In looking for a combination that will stand up to the test, we must use what we know about counterfactuals to find out about the appropriate similarity relation—not the other way around. [Lewis : ][] 

One might worry that a theory set up in this way has only little predictive power. It is true that certain judgments about particular cases are not predicted by the theory, but rather needed to get a grip on the primitive notions the theory employs. Nevertheless, I think significant predictive potential remains. First, there is a feedback loop concerning particular cases. Although some judgements about counterfactuals are needed to inform the notion of counterfactual chance, the grip gained through this procedure can be used to make predictions about other yet unconsidered cases. Secondly, there is the possibility of explaining general facts about counterfactuals. Just like Lewis uses similarity to explain 

>  Page reference to the reprint in Lewis (b). 

counterfactual chance  

how counterfactuals can have the logic they actually have, our credences in counterfactuals become explainable once we avail ourselves of the notion of counterfactual chance. It is a non-trivial fact that the whole pattern of credences a subject assigns over a range of counterfactuals can be accounted for on the basis of her estimate of a single notion of counterfactual chance. 

To see this strategy at work, let us consider the question of how counterfactual chances relate to objective physical chances. We have already seen in the course of our discussion of Skyrms’s proposal that the two notions are unlikely to coincide. Counterlegals, for example, suggest that the needed notion of chance has a broader domain than physical chances. Morgenbesser cases might show that the chances cannot always be the conditional chances given the antecedent, but must include further information regarding cotenable facts. Finally, given that counterfactual chances are assumed to be non-trivial even in deterministic worlds at which there may not be any non-trivial basic physical chances, this is another reason why counterfactual chances should not be identified with (conditional) physical chances. Nevertheless, physical chances can still be expected to have an influence on counterfactual chances. And with respect to many simple cases, the two notions might even be in full agreement. For instance, the counterfactual chance that a coin would have landed heads under the assumption that it had been tossed may well equal the corresponding conditional physical chance. 

There are hard cases which concern low-chance events which are, in a pre-theoretical sense, somewhat extravagant. Consider an example discussed by Hawthorne (): 

## () If the monkey had been put in front of a typewriter, it would not have produced _Hamlet_ . 

Assuming that all possible sequences of symbols the monkey might have typed in were equally likely, the problem is whether a world in which _Hamlet_ is produced has to be counted as relevant. There is no general agreement on this matter: Hawthorne sees no way of privileging non- _Hamlet_ -worlds over _Hamlet_ -worlds, whereas Williams () argues that _Hamlet_ -worlds are not relevant because they are in a certain sense abnormal. 

Do we have to side with Hawthorne on this matter? Although there is clearly a pull in this direction, this decision is not mandatory. The 

 counterfactual chance 

question turns on whether the counterfactual chances agree in such cases with the physical chances. Given that we do not have to identify the two notions, the present theory can accommodate either verdict. Following the suggested methodology, we would have to decide which credences are adequate with respect to the problematic counterfactuals. 

If we count some _Hamlet_ -worlds among the relevant antecedentworlds, the theory predicts that we should take a somewhat qualified attitude towards such a counterfactual by only saying something like 

() It is very likely that if the monkey had been put in front of a typewriter, it would not have produced _Hamlet_ . 

For in this case, the counterfactual chance would be high but not and so the evaluation constraint would recommend a high credence somewhat shy of certainty. Taking this attitude would accord well with the intuitive thought that the monkey produces sequences in a completely random fashion, so it might just as well have produced _Hamlet_ . 

On the other hand, if we are inclined to rule out _Hamlet_ -worlds, this will presumably be motivated by the impression that such a qualified attitude is unnecessarily modest. We could actually know that the above counterfactual is true. This would indicate that we should not consider _Hamlet_ -worlds to be relevant. If _Hamlet_ -worlds are excluded, the counterfactual chance given the the relevant antecedent-worlds will be . 

Thus, according to the present strategy, the fine-tuning of the notion of counterfactual chance depends on finding the right epistemic attitude towards these kind of counterfactuals. I find it very hard to say whether such counterfactuals are knowably true or merely very highly likely, so I will not attempt to decide this question. What is important in the present context is only to see how the notion of counterfactual chance can be informed partly by our uncertain judgements about counterfactuals. 

## .Conclusion 

The aim of this chapter was to find a fully general constraint which describes the pattern underlying the ascriptions of subjective probability to counterfactuals. The key concept we introduced for understanding this relationship is _counterfactual chance_ . It can be seen as the joint product of a conception of relevance and a conception of chance. Counterfactual chances can then be defined as the conditional chance of the set of 

counterfactual chance  

consequent-worlds given the set of relevant antecedent-worlds. With counterfactual chances in place, there are two ways of explicating an evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. We can follow the structure of Lewis’s Principal Principle and require the credence in a counterfactual to match the counterfactual chances conditional on any hypothesis about what the corresponding chances are. Essentially, we arrive at Skyrms’s proposal with the notion of chance being interpreted as counterfactual chance. Alternatively, we can—following Gärdenfors (), Joyce () and Lewis ()—generalize the notion of imaging to arrive at a notion of a _counterfactual supposition_ . Imaging is a way of hypothetically revising our credal state according to a counterfactual supposition by shifting the credence for every epistemic possibility to the corresponding set of relevant antecedent-worlds weighted by the chance function. With minor provisos, the two type of constraints turn out to be equivalent. Whereas the Principal Principle type formulation is a variant of Skyrms’s proposal, the evaluation constraint resulting from imaging is similar to the hypothetical belief state approach envisaged by Adams and Edgington, for the image under an assumption can be seen as a hypothetical belief state. Given the approximate equivalence between the two constraints we discussed in this chapter, it seems, then, that the two main trends in approaching the evaluation of counterfactuals converge to the same basic idea. 

The formulation in terms of imaging is closest in form to Adams’s thesis about indicative conditionals. Contrast 

() _P(_ ⌜ _A_ ⇒ _B_ ⌝ _)_ = _P(B_ | _A)_ with 

**==> picture [114 x 12] intentionally omitted <==**

We can see Adams’s thesis as saying that the subjective probability associated with an indicative conditional is the probability of the consequent evaluated within the scope of an indicative supposition, where an indicative supposition is assumed to have the effect of conditionalization. In a similar vein, the imaging constraint requires the subjective probability invested in a counterfactual to be the probability of the consequent arrived at within the scope of a counterfactual supposition. The two constraints differ in the kind of supposition associated with the two kinds of conditional, but they share their general structure. Both 

 counterfactual chance 

equate the absolute probability of a conditional with the probability of the consequent relative to a supposition of the antecedent. Call this the _suppositional feature_ of the two constraints. 

In the case of indicative conditionals, the suppositional feature of Adams’s thesis seems to be primarily responsible for why it is so hard to find a truth-conditional underpinning for this constraint, i.e. to explain the probability of an indicative conditional in terms of the probability of a proposition expressed by the conditional as a whole. Given that the evaluation constraint for counterfactuals shares the suppositional feature with Adams’s thesis, differing only in having substituted a counterfactual supposition for an indicative supposition, we may expect similar problems in the counterfactual case.[] Is the evaluation constraint compatible with counterfactuals having ordinary truth conditions or do we need an entirely different story in order to explain the intuitive credence assignments? It is fairly easy to see that the evaluation constraint cannot be explained in terms of the standard truth conditions for counterfactuals (cf. section .in the upcoming chapter). But what about other candidate truth conditions? These questions will concern us in the next chapter, where the challenge the evaluation constraint poses for an assignment of truth-conditions to counterfactuals will be analysed in more detail. 

>  Those familiar with Lewis’s () discussion of imaging will recall that the imaging constraint in terms of the non-generalized notion of imaging can be satisfied if counterfactuals are given Stalnaker’s truth conditions. So, endorsing the uniqueness assumption for the selection function puts one immediately in a position to explain the imaging constraint. The problem becomes acute only when the notion of imaging is generalized in order to avoid the uniqueness assumption. We will come back to this point in our discussion of the triviality results in chapter . 

## **** 

## A Puzzle About Counterfactuals 

It is well known that indicative conditionals give rise to a puzzle concerning their truth conditions (see Edgington , Edgington : ff., and also Stalnaker ). Until very recently, the dominant assumption was that counterfactuals can stay out of such trouble. Modulo possible refinements and minor modifications, the standard truth conditions for counterfactuals seemed to be largely correct, while the door is wide open for how an adequate theory of indicative conditionals might finally look like. Some philosophers even take a radically bifurcated stance according to which indicative conditionals have no truth conditions whereas counterfactuals have something like the standard truth conditions (Bennett , Gibbard ). Given that the two kinds of conditional are formed with the same connective, it seems, prior to investigation, somewhat unlikely that there is a big problem concerning truth conditions for indicative conditionals and nothing like that for counterfactuals. 

The troublemaking data concerning the subjective probabilities of indicative conditionals are generated by the intuitive plausibility of Adams’s thesis that indicative conditionals are evaluated by the corresponding conditional probabilities. Now, we have found in the previous two chapters that the intuitive way counterfactuals are evaluated is governed by a similar hypothesis which also brings into play conditional probabilities, albeit in a somewhat different way. It is a natural question whether the data captured by this hypothesis yield a similar problem for the assignment of truth conditions to counterfactuals. As we shall see shortly, this is indeed the case. 

In the debate about indicative conditionals, there are two ways of identifying the problem of truth conditions. There is a simple and 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

straightforward puzzle about the inferential properties of indicative conditionals concerning the question whether an indicative conditional ⌜¬ _A_ ⇒ _B_ ⌝ follows from the corresponding disjunction ⌜ _A_ ∨ _B_ ⌝. This is the puzzle discussed by Edgington and Stalnaker, which was mentioned above. In addition, there are the so-called triviality results initiated by Lewis (), which are formal results showing that Adams’s thesis cannot be upheld on a truth conditional semantics once certain fairly weak assumptions are made. The puzzle and the triviality results are not unrelated. The puzzle can actually be seen as a less formal reformulation of a very basic triviality result. Given that the puzzle is much more accessible and it is far easier to determine what it shows and what it does not show, we will focus in this chapter on how a similar puzzle for counterfactuals can be generated. I will defer discussion of possible triviality results until later (see chapter ), for they are better discussed against the backdrop of the theory I ultimately defend. 

The chapter proceeds as follows. To motivate the problem, it starts with a discussion of how the evaluation of counterfactuals challenges standard semantics. Then I develop a more general puzzle about truth conditions for counterfactuals. Once the puzzle is identified, its background assumptions will be analysed and we will look at a possible means of escape. Finally, the puzzle for counterfactuals will be compared to the original puzzle for indicative conditionals. 

## .The Problem for Standard Semantics 

To get a feel for how the evaluation of counterfactuals challenges the possibility of assigning adequate truth conditions, let us consider the problem for standard semantics in some more detail. Recall that on the standard account, a counterfactual is true iff the consequent is true at all relevant antecedent-worlds. With the formalism developed in the previous chapter, we can state these truth conditions more precisely in the following form: 

**Standard Semantics.** Let _φ_ , _ψ_ be sentences and _w_ ∈ _W_ . Then 

_(w_ |= ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ _)_ ≡ _(_ ∀ _w_[′] ∈ _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ : _w_[′] |= _ψ)_ . 

What this makes explicit is that the truth conditions are specified relative to a world _w_ . The selection function then provides a set of relevant 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

antecedent-worlds relative to _w_ . With the universal quantifier governing the specification of truth conditions, we see that on the standard account, counterfactuals express a restricted kind of necessity, where the restriction is induced by the relevant antecedent-worlds (we come back to this aspect in the discussion of the duality thesis; see .). Note also that on the standard account, counterfactuals with an impossible antecedent are always true, for in this case there are no relevant antecedent-worlds which makes the universal quantification vacuously true. With the standard truth conditions being explicated, we get a coarse-grained model of the proposition expressed by counterfactuals when we focus on the set { _w_ : _w_ |= _A > B_ }, i.e. the set of possible worlds at which the counterfactual is true. 

Here is an example Edgington (: ) uses to illustrate the problem the standard account has with the evaluation of counterfactuals. A dog almost always but not quite always attacks and bites when strangers approach. Jacky does not approach. Based on the information we have, it seems we should take the following counterfactual to be likely but not certain: 

() If Jacky had approached, she would have been bitten. But this verdict of high probability without certainty cannot be predicted by the standard account: 

Arguably in all of my examples above [ _. . ._ ], the counterfactuals would not come out as highly probable, but as known to be plain false on these truth conditions. Consider the dog that almost always bites when strangers approach. [ _. . ._ ] It’s not the case, and we take it not to be the case, that in _all_ the relevant worlds in which I approached I was bitten. So the truth condition is not satisfied, and we believe it is not satisfied: we think it’s certainly false that if you had approached you would have been bitten, according to the truth conditions. (Edgington : ) 

Since the dog bites almost always but not quite always, there seem to be some relevant worlds at which he does not bite. As a consequence, the standard truth conditions are not satisfied. This shows that the counterfactual would be false according to the standard truth conditions. Moreover, the relevant epistemic subject should, according to the standard account, take the counterfactual to be false, since the subject can be assumed to possess all the empirical information needed to recognize that the standard truth conditions are not satisfied (in this case, this is most prominently the information that the dog not quite always bites when 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

strangers approach). So, according to standard semantics, the subject should have a low credence, perhaps as low as , that the counterfactual is true. But this runs contrary to our inclination of assigning the counterfactual a high credence in the given epistemic situation. 

A note of caution. The foregoing argument may appear to be a little quick. There seem to be ways of enriching the given story such that the standard account is in a position to explain the high credence we are inclined to assign to the counterfactual. Here is one such way. Suppose that whether the dog attacks depends in a complex but fully deterministic way on the clothing of the stranger. Most patterns of colour and shape make the dog attack, but there are some rare combinations which calm the dog down. Now, suppose that this kind of dependency is known. However, it is totally unknown which combinations of colour and shape make for aggression and which do not. In such a situation, the standard account seems capable of predicting a high credence in the counterfactual that the stranger would have been bitten had she approached. Why is this so? We should reason as follows. Either the stranger’s clothes satisfy the pattern which cause the dog to attack or they do not. Since we know the general fact that most patterns cause aggression without knowing anything more specific, we should think it likely that the stranger’s clothes are of the aggression causing type. If the clothing is of the aggression type, then we can assume that the dog attacks in all relevant worlds in which the stranger approaches, for whether the dog attacks depends only on the clothing. On the other hand, if the clothing is not of the aggression type, we can actually assume that the dog attacks in no relevant world in which the stranger approaches. Since the first case is most likely, we should think it likely that the dog bites in all relevant worlds in which the stranger attacks. Hence, we should think it likely without being certain that the standard truth conditions are satisfied. 

This example indicates that there are cases in which the standard account is well equipped to predict the right amount of uncertainty about a given counterfactual. However, the specific way in which these cases are constructed prevents this line of argument from being generalized. They rely on the fact that the uncertainty derives from uncertainty about purely factual information and not from an only partial overlap of the relevant antecedent-worlds with the consequent-worlds (compare the preliminary discussion of possible sources of uncertainty in the introductory chapter). Hence, there are still plenty of ways of setting up the case which do not 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

allow for a response along these lines (cf. Edgington : ). For example, think of the following two possibilities. To begin with, suppose that whether the dog bites is a completely random business: it simply attacks strangers with a high frequency, but selects its victims on a random basis. If this is so, then there are no unknown particular features of the actual world which either turn all relevant worlds into worlds at which the dog attacks or turn them into worlds at which it does not. Rather, worlds at which the dog attacks are equally relevant as worlds at which it does not. Therefore, only most but not all relevant worlds will be worlds at which the dog bites, and this will be knowably so. As a second case, suppose that whether the dog bites depends in a very subtle but deterministic way on the manner of approach. Although this time the selection of victims is a deterministic process, this does not necessarily help the standard account, since it may not be determinate in exactly which way the stranger would have approached if she had approached (see also the discussion in section ..). In many cases, the class of relevant worlds will contain various worlds in which the stranger approaches in slightly different ways reflecting her normal ways of moving. Most of these approaches will cause the dog to bite, but some of them pass by peacefully. Again, the standard account is forced to predict a low credence in the relevant counterfactual. 

In sum, there are two ways in which the distribution of consequentworlds among the relevant antecedent-worlds may be inhomogeneous. Either the counterfactual worlds can unfold in various different ways after the time the antecedent has been made true or there are already various different ways a world can make the antecedent true (see also section .). If our uncertainty about a counterfactual is rooted in one of these two possibilities, the standard account has trouble explaining the uncertainty. 

Having argued informally so far, it should be clear that a difference between the probabilities assigned to counterfactuals and the probabilities assigned to the proposition corresponding to the standard truth conditions is also a direct consequence of the evaluation constraint developed in the previous chapter. Suppose it is known that most but not all of the relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. Then the counterfactual chances of the consequent given the antecedent are known to be high but less than . Plugging in the evaluation constraint, it follows that we ought to assign a high credence to the counterfactual. At the same time, we know that not all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds, and so we are in a position to know that the standard truth conditions 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

do not obtain. Hence, our credence that they obtain should be . If we stipulate ‘□[→] ’ to have the standard truth conditions, i.e. as abbreviating the right-hand clause in their specification above, we see that the evaluation constraint implies that the credences in counterfactuals and in the standard truth conditions sometimes diverge: 

- () For some sentences _A_ and _B_ and a subjective probability function _P_ : _P(_ ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ _) > P(_ ⌜ _A_ □[→] _B_ ⌝ _)_ .[] 

Now, we may ask whether, by the evaluation constraint, the probability of a counterfactual can sometimes be lower than the probability that the standard truth conditions obtain. To see that this is not the case, suppose that our credence in ⌜ _A_ □[→] _B_ ⌝ has a certain value, _x_ . Then, our credence that the counterfactual chance of _A_ under _B_ is is at least _x_ , for all relevant antecedent-worlds being consequent-worlds implies that the counterfactual chances are . If we now estimate our credence in the counterfactual via the evaluation constraint, we will assign probability to it in a number of epistemic possibilities whose total probability is _x_ . So our credence in the counterfactual will at least be _x_ . This shows that the subjective probability of a counterfactual is bounded from below by the subjective probability that the standard truth conditions obtain: 

- () Always: _P(_ ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ _)_ ≥ _P(_ ⌜ _A_ □[→] _B_ ⌝ _)_ . 

In a similar vein, we may ask whether we can also find a lower bound for the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals. As Joyce (: ) observes, a lower bound is given by the subjective probability of ⌜¬ _(A_ □[→] ¬ _B)_ ⌝. This is quite intuitive: if we invest some credence in a counterfactual, we should at least to the same degree assume that it is not the case that the negation of the consequent is a (restrictedly) necessary consequence of the antecedent by being true at all relevant antecedentworlds. More formally, it is clear that if we invest some credence _x_ in a counterfactual, this means, by the evaluation constraint, that our estimate of the counterfactual chances amounts to _x_ . But on every chance hypothesis which assigns a positive value to _B_ given _A_ , the chance of ¬ _B_ given _A_ will not be and so our credence in ⌜ _A_ □[→] ¬ _B_ ⌝ will be , from which 

>  I hope the fact that I use the symbol ‘ _>_ ’ ambiguously both as a sign for the natural language counterfactual and for the greater-than-relation does not cause any confusion. 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

it follows that our credence in ⌜¬ _(A_ □[→] ¬ _B)_ ⌝ will be . Hence, our total credence in ⌜¬ _(A_ □[→] ¬ _B)_ ⌝ will at least be _x_ · : 

() Always: _P(_ ⌜¬ _(A_ □[→] ¬ _B)_ ⌝ _)_ ≥ _P(_ ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ _)_ . 

From the two inequalities, we may draw some preliminary conclusions about the inferential profile of counterfactuals. Given that the subjective probability of a counterfactual is always at least as high as the subjective probability that the standard truth conditions obtain but sometimes higher, we can infer that counterfactuals should be implied by but not imply the standard truth conditions. In other words, the truth conditions should be strictly weaker than the standard truth conditions.[] Conversely, we can learn from the lower bound that the truth conditions of a counterfactual should at least require some of the relevant antecedentworlds to be consequent-worlds, for otherwise they would not imply ⌜¬ _(A_ □[→] ¬ _B)_ ⌝. 

A natural way of respecting these two lessons would be to assume that only a certain number of relevant antecedent-worlds have to be consequent-worlds. Bennett (: ff.) suggests such a weakening.[] He calls it the ‘near-miss proposal’: 

**Near Miss.** A counterfactual is true just in case the fraction of consequent-worlds among the relevant antecedent-worlds is sufficiently high. 

This way a counterfactual can be true even if not all relevant antecedentworlds are consequent-worlds. At the same time, some antecedent-worlds must be consequent-worlds for the counterfactual to be true. 

It is not hard to see, however, that theories like the near-miss proposal backfire (cf. Edgington ). Although they respect the two basic relations in which counterfactuals should stand to the standard truth conditions, they are not flexible enough to explain the whole range of credences in counterfactuals. Let us start by considering a case in which we do not take the number of consequent-worlds to be sufficiently high. We can 

>  Hájek () provides arguments to the effect that counterfactuals actually have truth conditions stronger than the standard ones, suggesting that counterfactuals are strict conditionals. For how to accommodate at least one prominent argument of Hájek’s, see section .. 

>  An account somewhat similar in spirit but different in structure is developed by Williams () in response to Hawthorne (). 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

assume that a fraction of about % is (by far) not sufficiently high. Now, we may think it about %-likely that if the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads, because we take about % of the relevant antecedent-worlds to be consequent-worlds. However, this assignment of subjective probability cannot be justified on Bennett’s proposal, for it should be obvious that the number of relevant consequent-worlds is not sufficiently high. On Bennett’s proposal, we should rather be certain that the counterfactual is false. Secondly, consider a lottery which is so large that the fraction of losing tickets exceeds the threshold in Bennett’s proposal. Then we can, according to Bennett’s proposal, be certain that if we had bought a lottery ticket, we would have lost. Intuitively, though, we cannot be certain, for we cannot rule out that we would have won against all odds. Thus, it seems that Bennett’s proposal over-generates certainty in some cases (where the fraction of relevant antecedent-worlds is above the threshold but below ) but still under-generates uncertainty in other cases (where the fraction is below the threshold but above ).[] 

Note that nothing seems to depend here on where we put the threshold in Bennett’s proposal. Effectively, then, the proposal by Bennett covers almost all natural ways of weakening the standard truth conditions. This gets us already very close to the puzzle about counterfactuals, for it seems that the standard truth conditions are too strong while natural ways of weakening them do not work either. Let us now see how we can extract a precise argument out of this difficulty. 

## .The Puzzle 

We just saw that high subjective probability of a counterfactual is compatible with low subjective probability that the standard truth conditions obtain. Somewhat surprisingly, the situation changes in the case of certainty. Certainty about a counterfactual seems to require certainty that the standard truth conditions obtain. This is a consequence of the evaluation constraint. In order to be certain about a counterfactual, we must not give any credence to the possibility that the counterfactual chance of the consequent under the antecedent is less than , for otherwise our total credence in the counterfactual could not be , as it is a weighted average of 

>  For more problems with Bennett’s proposal, see Edgington (: f.). 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

the values assigned by the chance hypotheses. So, we must be certain that the counterfactual chance is , which means that all relevant antecedentworlds must be consequent-worlds. Hence, we must be certain that the standard truth conditions obtain. We therefore find: 

() Always: If _P(_ ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ _)_ = , then _P(_ ⌜ _A_ □[→] _B_ ⌝ _)_ = . 

If we take the two features together, high probability of a counterfactual seems to be compatible with low probability that the standard truth conditions obtain, but certainty about a counterfactual seems incompatible with less-than-certainty that the standard truth conditions obtain. The first feature suggests that counterfactuals must be weaker than the standard truth conditions, but the second property seems to require that counterfactuals must be at least as strong as the standard truth conditions. In rough outline, this is the puzzle generated by the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals. 

Our puzzle can be illustrated with the lottery example. In normal circumstances, we can be fairly confident about the counterfactual 

() If I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost, 

while at the same time even be certain that the standard truth conditions do not obtain. Let us now ask under which conditions we can know or be certain that this counterfactual is true. A typical situation would be one in which we know that the lottery was rigged in such a way that my ticket is left without any chance of winning. But now observe that in such a situation the standard truth conditions obtain and can be known to obtain. So, the picture changes for counterfactuals which are certain: we cannot be certain about a counterfactual without being in a position to be certain that the standard truth conditions obtain. 

In addition, let us focus on a counterfactual which seems to be certain and show that it relates differently to the standard truth conditions in situations of uncertainty. Presently, we can be certain about the counterfactual 

() If Obama had lost, McCain would have won. 

Now, a typical situation in which we cannot be certain about this counterfactual would be one in which there is a promising third candidate. In such a case, we may only think it likely that McCain would have won if Obama had lost, because there are some relevant ways in which the 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

third candidate could have won. But then we can actually be certain that the standard truth conditions do not obtain despite being justified in assigning a high credence to the counterfactual. 

If we generalize these two observations, we arrive at the following puzzle: 

**The Puzzle.** For most counterfactuals _c_ : 

- (J) It is possible to possess a degree of justification less than for _c_ without being justified (not even indirectly) to the same or a higher degree that the corresponding standard truth conditions obtain. 

- (K) It is not possible to know _c_ without being in a position to know that the corresponding standard truth conditions obtain.[,] 

I present the puzzle here in somewhat more objective terms: knowledge and degrees of justification. It is equally possible to state the puzzle in terms of certainty and degrees of subjective probability. I prefer the more objective characterization because I take it to be more basic, but not much will hang on this choice. Let me also be explicit about the sense of possibility invoked in (J) and (K). It is simply meant to be metaphysical possibility. The same applies to the principles about epistemic access later on in this section. 

I have restricted the puzzle to most counterfactuals, since certain trivial counterfactuals do not satisfy the first assumption. To see this, consider 

- () If someone had robbed the bank, someone would have robbed the bank. 

It seems that we are always in a position to know that the standard truth conditions of this counterfactual obtain, for its trivial structure makes it plain that the relevant antecedent-worlds are always consequent-worlds. Moreover, we may always be fully justified in believing this counterfactual. But then there are already two reasons why (J) does not hold, for (J) requires the possibility of being uncertain about the counterfactual as well as the possibility of being uncertain about the standard truth conditions. 

>  I adopt a broad conception of being in a position to know comprising everything which can be known on the basis of our actual knowledge. Thanks to Miguel Hoeltje for this recommendation. 

>  For the sake of brevity, I often use phrases like ‘to know _c_ ’ in a slightly deviant sense (different from e.g. ‘she knows John’) meaning ‘to know that _p_ ’ for a ‘ _p_ ’ such that _c_ = the proposition that _p_ . 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

After having flagged this kind of exception, I will henceforth ignore, for ease of exposition, the need for such a restriction. 

The puzzle about counterfactuals is a general challenge for assigning adequate truth conditions to counterfactuals. Assumption (J) suggests that the truth conditions for counterfactuals cannot imply the standard truth conditions. If they did, we could infer the standard truth conditions from the counterfactual and thereby transfer our justification for the counterfactual to the standard truth conditions. Thus, counterfactuals cannot have the standard truth conditions. On the other hand, (K) indicates that the truth conditions of counterfactuals should imply the standard truth conditions. Otherwise there would be a situation in which the counterfactual is true but the standard truth conditions do not obtain. Moreover, nothing seems to stand in the way of knowing that such a situation obtains. But then it would be possible to know that a counterfactual is true without being in a position to know that the standard truth conditions obtain for the simple reason that the standard truth conditions do not obtain.[] So, no truth conditions weaker than the standard truth conditions seem to be adequate either. This, then, is the puzzle for counterfactuals: they seem to imply and not to imply the standard truth conditions. 

The reasoning to the contradiction makes use of two principles linking epistemic attitudes towards propositions with their comparative strength. **Implication and Epistemic Access.** Let _C_ and _D_ be propositions. 

- a. If _C_ entails _D_ , then it is not possible to be justified to a certain degree that _C_ is true without being justified (at least indirectly) to the same or a higher degree that _D_ is true. 

- b. If _C_ does not entail _D_ , then it is possible to know that _C_ is true without being in a position to know that _D_ is true.[] 

The rationale for these two principles is as follows. The first one is based on the idea that justification can be transferred by competent deduction. So, if _C_ entails _D_ , and we are justified to a certain degree that _C_ is true, 

>  Here I assume factivity for being in a position to know: if we are in a position to know that _p_ , then _p_ . 

>  Edgington (: ) discusses similar principles in application to indicative conditionals. 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

then competently inferring _D_ from _C_ can be supposed to transfer our justification for _C_ onto _D_ . The second principle may be rendered plausible by the following line of argument. If _C_ does not entail _D_ , then there is a possible situation in which _C_ is true but _D_ is false. Now, we may come to know that such a situation obtains. But then we would know that _C_ is true without being in a position to know that _D_ is true for the simple reason that _D_ is false. 

Before we further assess the plausibility of these principles, let me show how they provide the resources to give a direct argument to the effect that most counterfactuals lack truth conditions. Before that, a clarificatory remark about the terminology is in order. The question of whether conditionals have truth conditions is typically equated with the question of whether they express propositions. In this context, propositions are presupposed to encapsulate truth conditions so that we can assume a sentence to express a proposition just in case it has certain truth conditions. Moreover, both notions are construed in a _bivalent_ way. With this in mind, an argument to the conclusion that most counterfactuals lack truth conditions is quickly at hand. Let _c_ be a counterfactual which satisfies the two assumptions of the puzzle. To begin with, we find, by (J) and part (a) of the implication principle: 

- () If _c_ has truth conditions, its truth conditions do not entail the standard truth conditions. 

Suppose _c_ has truth conditions. Then it expresses a proposition. By (J), it is possible to be justified to a certain degree that this proposition is true while not being justified to the same or a higher degree that the standard truth conditions obtain. But the first part of the implication principle directly implies that this could not be the case if _c_ entailed the standard truth conditions. 

Application of part (b) of the implication principle to (K) yields: 

- () If _c_ has truth conditions, its truth conditions entail the standard truth conditions. 

Again, suppose that _c_ has truth conditions. Then it expresses a proposition. By (K), it is not possible to know that this proposition is true without being in a position to know that the standard truth conditions obtain. But then, by part (b) of the implication principle, the truth conditions of _c_ 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

must imply the standard truth conditions.[] Finishing off the argument, the two conditional statements now jointly imply that _c_ does not have truth conditions. 

What can we do? In the following section, we will see that the background principles linking implication (or the lack thereof) with justification and knowledge probably do not hold in full generality. This provides a small leak in the argument one may be able to exploit. We will explore this possibility later in chapter . 

## .A Means of Escape 

How plausible are the general principles linking implication and epistemic access? I will sidestep discussion of some of their details which are important but do not strike me to offer a key for a possible resolution of the puzzle. To make the principles more precise, one would need to address (i) which conception of entailment is presupposed, (ii) which conception of propositions is assumed and (iii) how epistemic attitudes are taken to interact with propositions. For instance, on a conception of propositions as sets of possible worlds, entailment amounts to being a subset, but a very complicated story is needed about how epistemic attitudes attach to propositions so conceived to explain why we are often justified to have different epistemic attitudes in relation to sentences which are necessarily equivalent. 

An analogue of the first principle is usually assumed in formal theories of justification and Bayesian epistemology. In standard probability theory, a logical consequence of a statement is never assigned a probability lower than the original statement. However, it should be noted that formal theories of justification are usually highly idealized. In particular, they tend to assign probability to every logical truth. Real epistemic subjects are not logically omniscient and often need to undergo a cognitive process of a certain kind, of which competent deduction is a paradigm, to transfer their justification for a given statement to one of its logical consequences. I have tried to accommodate this fact by introducing the idea of indirect 

>  We may note that the assumption that the counterfactual _c_ has truth conditions is only used to make the implication principles applicable to it. Opponents of truth conditions for counterfactuals would therefore need to explain why these principles lose their plausibility when applied to non-truth-conditional contents. 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

justification requiring only that logical consequences gain indirect justification from the justification of the statements from which they can be derived. To a good approximation, indirect justification may be construed as standing in a similar relation to justification as being in a position to know stands to knowledge. Still, the weakened principle probably needs further modification, but as far as I can see, this will not offer a promising route of escape to solve the puzzle. 

For instance, one might approach the puzzle by holding that the truth conditions of counterfactuals are not transparent to ordinary speakers. If so, it could be that ordinary speakers evaluate counterfactuals in a way which diverges from how they would have evaluated them had they known about their truth conditions (which are, we may assume, the standard truth conditions). To some extent, such a line would constitute an error theory about counterfactuals. On the other hand, I agree that ordinary speakers are not semantically omniscient in the sense of being capable of specifying the truth conditions of counterfactuals in terms of relevant possible worlds, for example. For one, they may lack the conceptual resources to do so. For another, even upon aquiring the necessary concepts, developing a semantic clause remains a difficult task not everyone can successfully carry out. Fortunately, there seems to be room for steering a middle course between a full error theory and an assumption of semantic omniscience. Even if speakers lack knowledge of independently specifiable semantic clauses, they can still be expected, by default, to proportion their credences to the logical strength propositions have in relation to other propositions and to what they believe the world is like. Applied to counterfactuals, this would mean that if a speaker has knowledge of facts, say about a third candidate in an election campaign, which would clearly invalidate certain truth conditions, but the speaker nevertheless assigns the counterfactual a high credence, then this is, by default, evidence that counterfactuals do not have the proposed truth conditions. 

The second principle strikes me as much more problematic. First of all, it is inconsistent with the existence of unknowable truths as expressed by Fitch-sentences. To see this, take _C_ to be an unknowable truth. Since _C_ is a truth, it is consistent and does not imply everything. Let _D_ be a proposition not implied by _C_ . Application of the second implication principle yields that it is possible to know that _C_ is true without being in a position to know that _D_ is true. However, the latter will not be possible 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

for the simple reason that it is, by the choice of _C_ , not even possible to know that _C_ is true. 

A similar point can be made on the basis of consistent but unknowable propositions, for the fact that Fitch-sentences are true was used above only to guarantee consistency.[] As an example, consider ‘There are no thinkers’. This sentence is false, but consistent, and so won’t imply everything. By the second principle, it would be possible to know that there are no thinkers. However, this is impossible: if someone were to know that there are no thinkers, there would be a thinker, namely her, and so the proposition that there are no thinkers would be false, and hence could not constitute knowledge. 

There is another source of counterexamples to this principle which does not involve unknowable truths. The principle is also challenged by knowable propositions which can only be known by being in a position to know a stronger proposition. Consider, for example, the following case. Let _C_ be the proposition that snow is white and assume _D_ to be the proposition that snow is white and there is a thinker. The proposition _C_ does not imply the proposition _D_ , for there are possible worlds in which snow is white but in which there are no epistemic subjects. But it is not possible to know _C_ without being in a position to know _D_ : if one knows that snow is white, one is always in a position to know that snow is white and there is a thinker, for the latter conjunct expresses a (contingent and perhaps a priori) truth which we are always in a position to know. So, this indicates that there are counterexamples to the principle which do not involve unknowable propositions but rather pairs of knowable propositions which differ in strength but not with respect to their knowability conditions.[] It need not even be that _D_ is strictly stronger than _C_ . If _D_ is any proposition which we are always in a position to know, such as ‘There is a thinker’ (or ‘I exist’), then any _C_ which does not imply _D_ , such as ‘Snow is white’, will already constitute a counterexample. 

There is also a formal argument by Rosenkranz (: ) to the same conclusion. Consider a sentence with the following structure: 

**==> picture [89 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

>  Thanks to Alan Hájek for pointing this out. The example which follows is also due to him. 

>  Cf. also Edgington (: ) who reckons with certain counterexamples to this principle stemming from Moore-paradoxical sentences. 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

where the operator ‘ _K_ ’ formalizes ‘is known at some time by someone’. The crucial fact to observe is that 

() _K(p_ ∧ _(Kp_ ⊃ _Kq))_ 

implies ⌜ _Kq_ ⌝ if we assume that the knowledge-operator distributes over conjunction, for in this case we find 

() _Kp_ ∧ _K(Kp_ ⊃ _Kq)_ , 

which, by the factivity of knowledge, further implies 

() _Kp_ ∧ _(Kp_ ⊃ _Kq)_ . 

Now, this entails ⌜ _Kq_ ⌝, while the original statement does not imply _q_ . In sum, ⌜ _p_ ∧ _(Kp_ ⊃ _Kq)_ ⌝ does not entail _q_ , but knowledge of the former implies knowledge of the latter, i.e. it is not possible to know the former without knowing the latter. Therefore, the present case is also a counterexample to the second principle. 

The failure of the second principle points to a possible means of escape from the puzzle. Suppose counterfactuals have truth conditions which are weaker than the standard truth conditions. But suppose further that our epistemic access to counterfactual truths is limited: we can only know that a counterfactual is true if we are in a position to know that a stronger proposition is true, namely that the standard truth conditions obtain. Such truth conditions could do justice to the first assumption of the puzzle, for counterfactuals would not imply the standard truth conditions. However, by having a more demanding epistemic profile, counterfactuals would satisfy the second assumption of the puzzle as well. Weaker truth conditions are not hard to find. The real question would be how counterfactuals could receive an epistemic profile with the desired properties. As remarked above, we will take up this question in chapter after we have discussed in the next chapter whether the puzzle might actually show that the probability of a counterfactual should not be construed as a probability of the counterfactual taken as a whole. 

## .Comparison with Indicative Conditionals 

The puzzle about counterfactuals has been closely modelled after a corresponding puzzle for indicative conditionals. In this section, I will briefly 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

make this connection explicit and comment on a few similarities and differences. 

In the case of indicative conditionals, the puzzle concerns the so-called _‘or’-to-‘if’ inference_ (I got this label from Edgington ). The question is whether the following pattern is valid: 

() _A_ or _B_ . 

() Therefore, if not _A_ , then _B_ . 

Basically, this inference states that indicative conditionals are implied by the corresponding material conditional, for exchanging ⌜¬ _A_ ⌝ for _A_ and eliminating the double negation in the conditional, it suggests that ⌜¬ _A_ ∨ _B_ ⌝ implies the indicative conditional. Assuming further that indicative conditionals imply the material conditional (which they do as long as they obey modus ponens), indicative conditionals would have the material truth conditions. The plausibility of the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference thus supports the material analysis. 

As we shall see, situations in which the material analysis struggles make the inference seem invalid. Stalnaker () has concluded from this that the inference is only pragmatically valid but semantically invalid.
