Edgington () (see also Edgington : ff.) uses the Janus-faced behaviour of the inference to construct an argument to the effect that conditionals do not have truth conditions. Edgington’s argument takes the form of a puzzle. It was the blueprint for the puzzle about counterfactuals developed in the previous section. Let us take a closer look at how the two puzzles relate to each other. 

Despite its intuitive plausibility, the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference appears to be invalid when we focus on situations of uncertainty. Suppose there are three suspects: the butler, the gardener and the cook. It is very likely that the gardener did it and so quite unlikely that the butler or the cook did it. Still, the hypothesis that the cook did it is comparatively much more likely than the remote thought that the butler murdered his master. In this scenario, we should think it very likely that either the gardener or the butler did it (simply because it is already very likely that the gardener did it), but we cannot be certain as there is a chance that the cook committed the crime. Intuitively, we should not infer that if the gardener did not do it, the butler did, for if the gardener did not do it, the cook probably did it. This is strong evidence against the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference. 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

Interestingly, the inference looks much better when the disjunctive premise is certain, while both disjuncts remain at least somewhat uncertain.[] To illustrate, suppose that the cook is not a suspect any longer. Then we can be certain that either the gardener or the butler did it. In this situation, we seem to be entitled to infer that if the gardener did not do it, the butler did. So, under conditions of certainty about the disjunction (but uncertainty about the disjuncts), the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference seems fine. 

Just like the problematic features for counterfactuals derive from the evaluation constraint, the puzzle about the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference can be traced back to a feature of conditional probabilities: the probability of a disjunction ⌜ _A_ ∨ _B_ ⌝ can be high while the corresponding conditional probability of _B_ given ⌜¬ _A_ ⌝ is low, but if the probability of ⌜ _A_ ∨ _B_ ⌝ is , the conditional probability of _B_ given ⌜¬ _A_ ⌝ must be as well. So if Adams’s thesis is correct and indicative conditionals are evaluated by the corresponding conditional probabilities, then it could be explained why the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference fares well in situations of certainty, but breaks down when the premise is uncertain even though it may deserve an arbitrarily high subjective probability. 

In conclusion, Edgington’s argument against truth conditions for indicative conditionals starts with two observations: (a) high subjective probability that the material truth conditions obtain is compatible with low subjective probability of the corresponding indicative conditional while (b) certainty that the material truth conditions obtain is incompatible with less-than-certainty about the corresponding indicative conditional.[] The first assumption suggests that the material truth conditions are not as strong as the truth conditions of the indicative conditional. Otherwise we should always take the indicative conditional to be likely when we take the corresponding material truth conditions to obtain. In contrast, the second assumption indicates that the truth conditions of the indicative conditional cannot be stronger than the truth conditions of the material conditional. Otherwise there would be 

>  If one disjunct is certain as in ‘ +  = or  +  = ’, the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference would force us to evaluate indicative conditionals with a certainly false antecedent, which is oftentimes a hard thing to do. 

>  If one is unsure whether indicative conditionals are true or even truth-evaluable when the antecedent is certainly false, then principle (b) should be qualified by requiring that the material conditional’s certainty does not owe itself to the certain falsity of its antecedent. 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

a possible situation in which the material truth conditions obtain but the truth conditions of the indicative conditional do not obtain. Now suppose we become certain that such a situation obtains. Then we would be certain about the material truth conditions but uncertain about the indicative conditional contradicting feature (b). Conclusion: indicative conditionals show an epistemic behaviour which one would not expect if they had an ordinary truth conditional content. In a nutshell, this is how the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inferences challenge truth conditional accounts of indicative conditionals. 

The general structure of the problem for indicative conditionals is largely the same as the one we identified for counterfactuals. There are two differences, though. First, indicative conditionals are compared with the material conditions, whereas counterfactuals are compared with the standard truth conditions for counterfactuals. Second, indicative conditionals are compared to truth conditions which are potentially weaker, whereas counterfactuals are compared to truth conditions which are potentially stronger. 

Changing the truth conditions that counterfactuals are compared to is essential for developing a similar puzzle about counterfactuals. It is quite clear that the puzzle about indicative conditionals cannot be extended in a direct way to counterfactuals. For that to be possible, certainty about the material truth conditions would have to be sufficient for certainty about the corresponding counterfactual. But this is simply not the case. To see this, recall an example from chapter . Officer Shaker can be certain that either Linda or Elizabeth was at home last night, for he found a note saying ‘I will be back tonight’ which was written either by Linda or Elizabeth. However, Shaker cannot be sure that if Linda had not been home last night, Elizabeth would have been, for if Linda wrote the note, then Elizabeth probably had no inclination to come back early independently of Linda’s whereabouts. For this reason, we cannot always be certain about a counterfactual on the basis of certainty about the corresponding material truth conditions. 

Actually, much simpler counterexamples can be constructed from cases in which certainty about the material truth conditions is grounded either in certainty that the antecedent is false or in certainty that the consequent is true, for we can neither be certain about a counterfactual on the basis of being certain that its antecedent is false nor on the basis of being certain that its consequent is true. But since the corresponding 

 a puzzle about counterfactuals 

hypothesis for indicative conditionals is sometimes restricted to cases in which certainty about the material truth conditions is not based on certainty about some of its constituents, it is important to note that there are counterexamples which could not be excluded by a similar restriction. 

It might be that the fact that the puzzle for indicative conditionals does not directly affect counterfactuals has created the illusion that there is no problem of truth conditions for counterfactuals. For instance, Bennett (: ff.) takes this fact to show that there is no corresponding problem about truth conditions for counterfactuals. But drawing this conclusion has probably been a little premature. One may even wonder whether Bennett has missed the relevant dimension of comparison: in extending the argument to counterfactuals, one should not compare a counterfactual with the material conditional but rather with a proposition which relates to a counterfactual in a way similar to how the material conditional relates to the indicative conditional.[] Counterfactuals do not stand in an interesting relation to the material conditional, but they do stand in an interesting relation to the standard truth conditions. 

## .Conclusion 

In this chapter, we have taken a closer look at how the data about the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals challenge not only standard semantics, but truth conditional accounts in general. It seems that under conditions of certainty, counterfactuals must at least be as strong as the standard truth conditions, whereas under conditions of uncertainty, they appear to express a weaker proposition. Taken together, it seems counterfactuals should and should not imply the standard truth conditions. 

In analysing the puzzle generated in this way, we have seen that it relies on two background assumptions concerning the epistemic impact of implication relations, at least one of which seems problematic. If counterfactuals were weaker than the standard truth conditions but had a more demanding epistemic profile which would be such that they could 

>  In a similar vein, Edgington () counters Bennett’s objection by pointing out that she intends the argument to be run on the relevant hypothetical belief state rather than the actual belief state. So, by holding that counterfactuals behave like indicative conditionals in a hypothetical belief state, she intends to generalize the problem with the ‘or’-to-‘if’ inference to counterfactuals. 

a puzzle about counterfactuals  

only be known if one is in a position to know that the standard truth conditions obtain, then counterfactuals could have truth conditions while still satisfying the two assumptions of the puzzle. 

This is admittedly only a small loophole in the argument against truth conditions. So before we explore how one might be able to exploit it, we shall discuss what might seem to be the more natural conclusion to be drawn from the puzzle. One may simply take the argument against truth conditions to be sound: the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals cannot be explained in terms of truth conditions. Rather, they should be given a non-standard explanation. Given the suppositional nature of the evaluation constraint, one may even think that looking for a propositional explanation was wrong-headed from the start. As we shall see, following up on this thought does not necessarily mean that counterfactuals do not have truth conditions (though this is an option), for setting up the puzzle presupposes that the subjective probabilities concern the counterfactual as a whole. If one can give an alternative explanation, one regains some territory to assign truth conditions to counterfactuals which would then not have to carry the burden of explaining the data about subjective probabilities of counterfactuals on which the puzzle is based. How all this might work will be the topic of the next chapter. 

## **** 

## Restriction and Modification 

The data concerning the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals make it hard if not impossible to account for them in terms of truth conditions. It is therefore a natural move to step back and ask whether they should really be seen as reflecting the likelihood of a proposition expressed by the counterfactual as a whole. One might even take the puzzle about truth conditions for counterfactuals as a reductio of this idea. But what could an alternative account look like? 

On the linguistic level, we have treated epistemic expressions as if they take the whole counterfactual as a single argument. This reflects the assumption that the data concern the epistemic properties of a proposition expressed by the counterfactual as a whole. However, this is not the only way one can think of the interaction between operators and conditionals. Partly motivated by the problems of accounting for the similarly puzzling data concerning indicative conditionals, an alternative picture has been developed: the restrictor view of conditionals. Originating from a suggestion by Lewis () and further developed in the works of Kratzer (see the collection of papers in Kratzer ) and others, the restrictor view requires a fairly radical departure from standard theories of conditionals by questioning the fundamental assumption that conditionals are to be seen as two-place connectives. The basic idea is that ‘if’-clauses are restrictors of operators rather than connectives. On this picture, the semantics of ‘if, then’-constructions is not given by assigning to ‘if, then’ a function which operates on two arguments, the semantic value of the antecedent and the semantic value of the consequent. Rather, the semantics of conditionals can only be given in relation to an operator the ‘if’-clause can restrict. For this to be a general theory of conditionals, 

restriction and modification  

one needs to assume that there is always an operator present in the logical form of conditionals. 

Although the restrictor view of conditionals is meant to apply both to indicative conditionals and to counterfactuals, it receives its main motivation from certain indicative constructions. In particular, the question of whether counterfactual ‘if’-clauses can restrict epistemic modals expressing present epistemic modality has, as far as I am aware, not been addressed in any detail. For this reason, this chapter investigates whether the restrictor view can be put to use to explain the puzzling data concerning the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals. Given the potential of the restrictor view to explain how conditional probabilities could be expressed by indicative conditionals, there is reason to hope that it might yield an account of the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals, too, for they seem to flow in a similar way from a certain conception of conditional chances. As it turns out, the idea of a restrictor has to be generalized if such an explanation is to be had. What we will need is the idea of a _modifier_ which can modify an operator in a way which is not restriction. 

The chapter is structured as follows. After explicating the restrictor view in some more detail, we will see why the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals cannot be explained in terms of restriction. I will then introduce the idea of a modifier. As an interlude, I will briefly compare the resulting theory to two similar approaches: the suppositional view and a dynamic semantics of counterfactuals. We then turn to two potential problems for the view, a smaller and a bigger one. The latter is the main reason why I think it is worthwhile to continue to look for a propositional explanation of the problematic data. 

## .The Restrictor View 

To set the stage, here is a brief description of the restrictor view of conditionals. Lewis (: f.) developed the idea that ‘if’-clauses might be capable of performing a restrictor function in order to explain the intuitive truth conditions of sentences with the following structure: 

() Always/Usually/Sometimes, if it is raining, my roof leaks. Lewis argues that ‘if’-clauses occurring in sentences of this kind are best interpreted as restrictors of the adverbial quantifiers. The observation 

 restriction and modification 

behind his argument is the following. The truth conditions for a construction with ‘always’ seem to correspond to a universally quantified material conditional: 

() It is always the case that either it is not raining or my roof leaks, 

but in order to get the truth conditions for ‘sometimes’ right, we would have to assume that the conditional works like a conjunction, for only then the sentence comes out true iff 

() There is a time at which it is raining and my roof leaks, 

which are the intuitive truth conditions. So, there is no single conditional meaning which is supplied both under ‘always’ and under ‘sometimes’. And it would be implausible to assume that the embedded conditional clause varies its meaning depending on the quantifier under which it is embedded (but see Higginbotham ). 

Even worse, for quantifiers like ‘often’ or ‘usually’, it seems virtually impossible to find a conditional meaning which would correspond to the intuitive truth conditions. The observation there is essentially the same as the argument for restricted quantifiers (cf. Barwise and Cooper ) in constructions like 

() Most people enjoy watching TV. 

The latter sentence is best analysed as a restricted quantification of the form 

() [Most _x_ : _Fx_ ][ _Gx_ ], 

where the predicate ‘ _F_ ’ is taken to restrict the domain of the quantifier (square brackets here indicate the supposed syntactic phrase structure). Contrast this with an analysis like the following: 

() [Most _x_ ][ _Fx_ → _Gx_ ], 

where the quantifier would take a conditional as a single argument. For ‘most’ it is known that there is no (classical) connective which would provide the intuitive truth conditions on this syntactic analysis. Given the similarity in meaning between 

() Often, if it is raining, my roof leaks 

and 

restriction and modification  

() Most cases in which it is raining are cases in which my roof leaks 

it is not surprising that we face similar difficulties if we assume that ‘often’ quantifies over a whole conditional. The natural alternative would be to take the ‘if’-clauses to be a restrictor of the quantifier in close analogy to cases of restricted quantification. 

On a restrictor analysis, the interaction of ‘if’-clauses with adverbial quantifiers could be given a unified account. The structure of Lewis’s sample sentences would be something like the following, with _Q_ representing an arbitrary quantifier: 

() [ _Qt_ : it is raining at _t_ ][my roof leaks at _t_ ]. Thus, in the logical form of these sentences, we would not find a two-place conditional connective any longer. As Lewis puts it: 

I conclude that the _if_ of our restrictive if-clauses should not be regarded as a sentential connective. It has no meaning apart from the adverb it restricts. (Lewis : ) 

The proposal has natural extensions for other kinds of expressions. Most important in the present context is its extension to epistemic phrases expressing different degrees of likelihood. Here one could assume analogously—as Lewis () already suggests—that an expression of probability might get restricted by the antecedent of the conditional. Consider: 

() Probably, if I buy a lottery ticket, I will lose, 

and the possibility of giving it a restrictor analysis: 

- () [Probably: I buy a lottery ticket] [I will lose]. 

The truth conditions seem very close in meaning to a clear case of restricted quantification: 

- () Most cases in which I buy a lottery ticket are cases in which I lose. 

If the latter is given a restrictor analysis, it is a natural thought to consider the same analysis for the former, too. If the probability function associated with ‘probably’ is uniform, the two types of sentence can be taken to be equivalent. 

 restriction and modification 

To account for the general case in which ‘probably’ may not quite function like ‘most’ because it weighs some cases more than others, something has to be said about the effect of restricting an expression of probability. The first thing which springs to mind is to account for it in terms of conditionalization (cf. Kratzer and, for more details, Égré and Cosiz ). Given this assumption, it could be explained how embedding conditionals under probability operators results in expressions of conditional probability. 

## .Simple Conditionals 

Lewis does not make a universal claim about ‘if’. What he says is compatible with the possibility that ‘if’ sometimes behaves like a two-place sentential connective. Kratzer has generalized Lewis’s proposal.[] She claims that ‘if’ is always a restrictor and never a sentential connective: 

The history of the conditional is the story of a syntactic mistake. There is no two-place _if . . . then_ connective in the logical forms for natural languages. _If_ -clauses are devices for restricting the domains of various operators. Whenever there is no explicit operator, we have to posit one. (Kratzer : ) 

For many cases, Kratzer () assumes that indicative conditionals are implicitly modalized by an epistemic necessity operator. Consider an utterance of a simple conditional which does not contain any overt operator the ‘if’-clause might restrict: 

- () If Jacky arrived in New York yesterday, she contacted Jack. 

On Kratzer’s account, the logical form of what is said by this utterance typically looks like this: 

- () [MUST: Jacky arrived in New York yesterday] [she contacted Jack]. 

Utterances of indicative conditionals which are not explicitly modalized will typically express a restricted kind of epistemic necessity.[] 

>  See Kratzer (), Kratzer () (updated in Kratzer : ch. ) and perhaps most importantly Kratzer () (updated in Kratzer : ch. ). 

>  There is no need to assume that the covert modal behaves like the overt modal ‘must’. Without this assumption, one can, for instance, allow that indicative conditionals sometimes 

restriction and modification  

Assigning truth conditions to simple, unmodalized counterfactuals is less straightforward, but the basic idea of taking them to restrict an implicit operator can still be brought to work.[] Given that the truth conditions of counterfactuals do not generally coincide with the truth conditions of the corresponding indicative conditional, counterfactual ‘if’-clauses would not be taken to restrict an epistemic necessity operator, but rather a different operator better suited to generate plausible truth conditions for counterfactuals. The difference between the two kinds of conditional could be explained by assuming that they typically select different implicit operators. What makes this project challenging is the fact that restrictable operators which could provide plausible truth conditions for counterfactuals are not so easy to find. 

With some sophistication, however, it is possible to derive the standard truth conditions for counterfactuals (see Kratzer ).[] To capture the variable strictness of the standard truth conditions, one would need to assume a more complex implicit operator. Informally speaking, the operator would have to express something like _close necessity_ , so that the consequent only needs to be true at all the close or relevant antecedentworlds. On this idea, the logical form of a counterfactual would roughly look like this: 

() [CLOSELY NECESSARY: Antecedent] [Consequent]. 

Restricting an operator of close necessity to worlds at which the antecedent is true would generate a set of close antecedent-worlds. Saying that all of those are consequent-worlds would then be exactly what the standard truth conditions require. 

have the material truth conditions by interpreting the necessity operator in a vacuous way. Cf. Kratzer (). 

>  It would also be possible to hold that counterfactuals always contain an overt modal the ‘if’-clause can restrict, namely the modal governing the consequent. In the case of simple counterfactuals, this is typically ‘would’. If one wants to derive the standard truth conditions in this way, one could adopt the view that ‘would’ expresses something like close necessity as explained below. There is a further possibility which only becomes visible once the idea of modification, to be explained below, has been developed. Once in place, it would then be possible to hold that counterfactual ‘if’-clauses do not restrict a covert (or overt) operator, but rather modify it. 

>  An alternative picture is developed by von Fintel (), who takes counterfactuals to be strict conditionals with a contextually restricted domain, such that counterfactuals will effectively often have truth conditions very similar to the standard truth conditions. 

 restriction and modification 

It will help to see how this works in detail. The basic idea is that a modal can, in addition to a world of evaluation _w_ and a domain of worlds _Dw_ , be sensitive to an ordering source ≤ _w_ .[] An ordering source tells us which worlds are closer than others. 

Modelled after Lewis’s (: ) specification of truth conditions for counterfactuals, Kratzer defines a notion of necessity which is relative to an ordering of worlds in the following way: 

**Necessity (Kratzer).** A proposition _p_ (expressed by _α_ ) is a _necessity_ in _w_ with respect to _f_ and _g_ (�NECESSARY _α_ � _[w]_[,] _[D][w]_[,][≤] _[w]_ = ) iff for all _u_ ∈ _Dw_ , there is a _v_ ∈ _Dw_ such that (i) _v_ ≤ _w u_ and 

(ii) for all _z_ ∈ _Dw_ : if _z_ ≤ _w v_ , then _z_ ∈ _p_ . 

So, roughly speaking, a proposition is said to be a necessity if it obtains at all ≤ _w_ -closest worlds in the domain _Dw_ . Since Kratzer wants to allow for worlds getting closer and closer without there being some closest worlds—thus denying the limit assumption (see section .)—the definition has to be a little more complex. For a proposition to be necessary, it is required that there is to any world _u_ in the domain a ≤ _w_ -closer world _v_ such that the proposition holds at all worlds ≤ _w_ -closer than _v_ . In other words, if we approach _w_ along the ordering relation, we find from a certain point on only _p_ -worlds. 

For specific settings of the domain and the ordering parameter, certain familiar kinds of necessity result. If ≤ _w_ relates all worlds, then the impact of the ordering source vanishes and necessity converges to ordinary necessity over the domain _Dw_ . If we further let _Dw_ be the set of all metaphysically possible worlds, we see that broad metaphysical necessity turns out to be a limiting case. The implicit modal in the restrictor view’s analysis of counterfactuals, however, will require a non-trivial ordering as employed in standard semantics for counterfactuals. A necessity operator of this kind would express something like close necessity. 

>  I simplify here somewhat the proposal in Kratzer (). Instead of a set of worlds as the domain, Kratzer works with a richer notion of a _conversational background_ which is construed as a set of propositions, alias sets of worlds. But in the semantics for modals which interests us, only their intersection matters, which is simply a set of worlds. Similarly, she has a richer notion of an ordering source, but in the semantics only the ordering relation induced by it plays a role. 

restriction and modification  

This gives us already a fairly general theory of conditionals. They are a device for restricting various operators or quantifiers. In unembedded contexts, they typically express a strict epistemic conditional in the indicative case and the standard truth conditions in the counterfactual case by restricting an implicit necessity operator. Embedded under an overt operator or quantifier, they restrict the corresponding domain to those cases at which the antecedent holds. 

It is important to note that giving a semantics of counterfactuals in this way is not challenged by the puzzle about their truth conditions, for the subjective probabilities are, at least as far as they are reflected in the linguistic behaviour of epistemic expressions, no longer construed as being concerned with the probability that these truth conditions obtain (I will comment on how this extends to credences in counterfactuals in section .). Saying 

() It is likely that if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost is not taken to attribute high probability to the proposition the counterfactual expresses when it occurs unembedded. Actually, it is not taken to attribute probability to the counterfactual at all. Rather, it is taken to attribute probability to the consequent relative to a domain of possibilities which has been restricted by the antecedent. Thus, the restrictor view promises to explain the data about the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals without having to give up standard semantics for counterfactuals when they occur unembedded. 

## .Counterfactual ‘If’-Clauses as Restrictors 

As it stands, the restrictor view of counterfactuals cannot be quite adequate. As we shall see in this section, it would assimilate counterfactuals to indicative conditionals in contexts in which they are both embedded under an epistemic expression. To see the problem, let us look at the classic Oswald/Kennedy example (see Adams ) embedded under a probability operator: 

- () _It is likely_ that no one else would have killed Kennedy if Oswald had not done it. 

Assuming here that the epistemic modal is restricted by the counterfactual ‘if’-clause would yield an interpretation according to which most 

 restriction and modification 

present epistemic possibilities in which Oswald did not kill Kennedy are such that no one else killed Kennedy.[] But this is an undesirable consequence: In every present epistemic possibility someone killed Kennedy, for it is common knowledge that someone killed Kennedy. Restricting the epistemic possibilities to those in which Oswald did not do it will not alter this fact. In all of those, it will still be the case that someone else killed Kennedy, since they are merely a subclass of our original class. As a consequence, () would come out as false. But it seems to be true. 

In fact, the present analysis would have the following statement come out true: 

- () _It is certain_ that someone else would have killed Kennedy if Oswald had not done it. 

This would turn out to be true, for Kennedy is killed by someone else at all worlds which are presently live possibilities. So, when ‘it is certain’ is evaluated with respect to this domain and applied to the proposition that someone else killed Kennedy, then the resulting expression comes out true. But, of course, the present statement should come out false: not only is the embedded counterfactual not certain to be true, it is likely to be false. 

The following may lead to a diagnosis of the problem. Applying the restrictor analysis to the interaction between epistemic modals and counterfactual ‘if’-clauses would assimilate () to its indicative version: 

() _It is likely_ that no one else killed Kennedy if Oswald did not do it. 

This ascription of epistemic probability is indeed incorrect and its incorrectness may be explained by assuming the ‘if’-clause to restrict the probability operator, for at no present epistemic possibility at which Oswald did not kill Kennedy is it the case that no one else did. 

Assuming that counterfactual ‘if’-clauses restrict epistemic modals in the same way as indicative ‘if’-clauses are supposed to do does not leave room for the differences between the two kinds of conditionals 

>  To simplify, I assume here that the relevant epistemic possibilities are equally likely. Otherwise ‘it is likely that’ would not necessarily go by what is the case at most epistemic possibilities. 

restriction and modification  

when they are embedded under epistemic operators. The same probability ascriptions would always be true of both kinds of conditional. So, we are driven to the conclusion that the semantic relation between epistemic modals and counterfactuals cannot be accounted for on the model of domain restriction. 

The problem with the restrictor analysis seems to be that it treats counterfactual ‘if’-clauses just like indicative ‘if’-clauses when they interact with a modal operator. But Oswald/Kennedy type examples suggest that at least with respect to epistemic modals, the behaviour of counterfactual ‘if’-clauses and indicative ‘if’-clauses comes apart. This happens in very much the same cases in which an unembedded indicative conditional is evaluated differently from the corresponding counterfactual. Explaining the difference in the unembedded case is not a problem, for, as we have seen, it may be assumed that different implicit operators are selected. But once the operator is explicit and the same in both cases, there is no room anymore for a possible difference.[] 

One assumption I have made is that epistemic phrases such as ‘it is likely’ still express present epistemic possibility under the salient interpretation when they attach to counterfactuals. One way of seeing that this is indeed the case is to focus on cases where the probability ascription is coordinated with an ascription of probability to an ordinary proposition. For instance, consider 

- () _It is likely_ that Oswald shot Kennedy and that no one else would have done so if Oswald had not done it. 

Given that it is clear that we want to express high present epistemic probability concerning a past event as far as the first conjunct is concerned, the probability ascription to the second conjunct must be given the same interpretation. 

>  There is one possibility I have ignored here: one could assume that in the counterfactual case the ‘if’-clause continues to restrict an implicit operator which scopes under the probability operator (for general discussion of doubly modalized conditionals, see e.g. Geurts , Kratzer : f., and Swanson ). But then the attributions of probability to counterfactuals would be explained as attributions to a proposition expressed by the counterfactual as a whole just as if we had fed in the standard view. However, because of the problems with this possibility, we are currently looking for a solution in which the restrictor view could do some essential work. 

 restriction and modification 

Alternatively, one could also consider epistemic attitude verbs as in the following example: 

- () _Jacky is fairly confident_ that no one else would have killed Kennedy if Oswald had not done it. 

As present tense epistemic attitude verbs do not have interpretations on which they do not go by the present epistemic attitudes of the subject, there is no room for manoeuvre here for arguing that embedding counterfactuals under such expressions might make another interpretation salient which no longer concerns the present epistemic possibilities. 

Another assumption I have made is that the epistemic phrases under consideration are effectively only sensitive to a domain of discourse and not to an ordering source. This is a standard assumption. Still, one may wonder whether one could get around the present problem by allowing the ordering source to play a role as well. However, I cannot see how this might be possible. The problem seems to be due to a structural feature which cannot be outmanoeuvred by bringing an ordering source into play. When we evaluate a counterfactual, we often consider worlds which are not live (epistemic) possibilities. In the Oswald/Kennedy example, we consider worlds at which no one killed Kennedy although it is not a live possibility that no one killed Kennedy. So, the restricted domain of discourse with respect to which the epistemic phrase is evaluated should at least sometimes contain worlds which are not contained in the original domain to which the epistemic modal is sensitive. But this effect cannot be achieved by an ordering, for independently of its effects, the restricted domain will always be a subset of the original domain. What we would need is a partial expansion of this domain. This observation gives rise to what might be a better proposal: counterfactual ‘if’-clauses do not simply function as restrictors, they interact with epistemic domains in a more complex way by partially expanding them. To this idea we now turn. 

## .Counterfactual ‘If’-Clauses as Modifiers 

Epistemic phrases interact with counterfactual ‘if’-clauses in a way which is not restriction. If one thinks about it, this may not be much of a surprise. After all, we evaluate indicative conditionals and counterfactuals in quite different ways. It was to be expected that this difference would 

restriction and modification  

be preserved when they are embedded under an epistemic modal. So, if we wish to stick to the basic idea of the restrictor view, we should allow counterfactual ‘if’-clauses to interact with epistemic phrases in a similar but different way. 

At this point, it might be helpful to go back to our discussion of indicative versus counterfactual suppositions in chapter . Indicative and counterfactual ‘if’-clauses seem to invite us to do different things. An indicative conditional is evaluated by temporarily adding the antecedent to our stock of beliefs and then asking whether the consequent is true in this more informed belief state. There is a natural connection with the restrictor view here. Adding a proposition to one’s stock of belief is like restricting one’s belief state to those cases in which the proposition is true. If a belief state is modelled somewhat coarse-grainedly as a set of epistemically possible worlds, then adding a proposition conceived of as a set of worlds amounts to the intersection of the two sets or, equivalently, to the restriction of the original domain to the subset in which the proposition is true. Moreover, the fact that making an indicative supposition goes by conditionalization corresponds on the linguistic level to the fact that restricting an expression of probability results in an expression of conditional probability. 

Counterfactual ‘if’-clauses also invite us to make a supposition, but a counterfactual supposition is different from an indicative one. Making a counterfactual supposition often requires us to temporarily suspend certain beliefs while holding others constant. For instance, when we make the counterfactual supposition that Oswald had not killed Kennedy, we suspend our present belief that Kennedy was killed by someone but we hold onto our present beliefs about the circumstances a short while before Kennedy’s assassination. In contrast, when we make the indicative supposition that Oswald did not kill Kennedy, we do not suspend our belief that Kennedy was killed by someone. As we have seen, where the effect of an indicative supposition may be described in terms of conditionalization, the effect of a counterfactual supposition could be described in terms of imaging. Integrating a counterfactual supposition takes us to the image of our present belief state under this supposition. Taking this to the linguistic level, the idea would be that counterfactual ‘if’-clauses do not restrict epistemic phrases but sometimes perform a linguistic analogue of imaging. To have a name for this, let us say that counterfactual ‘if’-clauses might _modify_ the domain of epistemic expressions. 

 restriction and modification 

To get an idea of how this might work formally, let us start by formalizing the semantics of the restrictor view. At its centre, the restrictor view contains the idea that ‘if’-clauses restrict the domain of the modal in the scope of which they occur. Syntactically, the restrictor view requires that the ‘if’-clause combines with the modal to form a more complex modal rather than that it first interacts with the consequent to form a conditional sentence which would then be the single argument of the modal. Once we assume a syntactic analysis which gives us this result, the semantics only needs a clause which deals with the interaction of an ‘if’-clause with a modal. To keep things simple, let us model the domain of modals as sets of possible worlds and let us ignore other parameters they might be sensitive to. Then the semantic clause for restriction by an ‘if’-clause might simply look like this (cf. Kratzer : ): 

() �MODAL : A� _[E]_ = �MODAL� _[E]_[∩][�] _[A]_[�] . 

The semantic value of a modal restricted by an ‘if’-clause _A_ relative to an epistemic domain _E_ is the semantic value of the modal relative to the domain _E_ intersected by the semantic value of _A_ . This clause gives us the desired result that the ‘if’-clause functions as a restrictor on the domain of the modal. Of course, to derive the semantic value of the restricted modal we need a clause for the modal expression relative to a parameter and, quite obviously, the semantic value of _A_ . But the latter two values will be derivable independently of what we say about conditionals. 

Given a semantic clause along these lines, its application to complete conditional constructions including a consequent is straightforward. If we have a conditional governed by a modal which is syntactically analysed according to the restrictor view, the semantic clause dealing with restriction does all the interesting work: 

() �[MODAL : A][ _B_ ]� _[E]_ = �MODAL� _[E]_[∩][�] _[A]_[�] � _B_ �. 

The antecedent of the conditional restricts the domain of the modal by intersecting it. The only further thing which happens is that the restricted modal takes the consequent as an argument. 

To give the semantics of the envisaged view according to which counterfactual ‘if’-clauses can sometimes act as modifiers rather than restrictors, we can keep the structure of the semantics of the restrictor view. What would have to change is the operation of restriction: instead of accounting for it in terms of intersection, we would have to postulate 

restriction and modification  

a more complex operation _F_ which describes how a counterfactual supposition _A_ gets integrated into an epistemic state _E_ yielding a new belief state _F(E_ , _A)_ . To identify the general structure, it might for the moment be better to leave it at an abstract level, but we shall soon see that we can account for modification in terms of imaging. In addition, it helps to assume that the operation of modification is visible on the syntactic level. I will write ‘::’ if we are dealing with modification instead of restriction. Then the relevant semantic clause which accounts for the interaction between a modal and a counterfactual ‘if’-clause could look like this: 

**==> picture [180 x 13] intentionally omitted <==**

The semantic value of a modal modified by a counterfactual ‘if’-clause _A_ relative to a domain _E_ is the semantic value of the modal evaluated relative to the domain modified by the semantic value of _A_ . 

Now, in our discussion of imaging, we have focussed on how to define the image of a probability function. In order to explicate the desired operation of domain modification, we have as input not a probability function but only a domain consisting of a set of possible worlds.[] But this actually makes our life easier, not harder. The difficult task in defining imaging was to explain the probability shift. Explaining how the domain changes is straightforward. Recall that the basic idea of imaging was to consider for every epistemic possibility _w_ the set _fw(A)_ of relevant antecedent-worlds. If we do this for all epistemic possibilities, we arrive at the domain the image is defined on. These are all the worlds _w_[′] for which there is some epistemic possibility _w_ in our original domain such that _w_[′] is in _fw(A)_ , i.e. is a relevant antecedent-world relative to _w_ . Our modified domain of epistemic possibilities would thus be the set of worlds for which it currently is an epistemic possibility that they are a relevant antecedentworld. So, we can define the modified domain _F(E_ , _A)_ of a domain of epistemic possibilities _E_ and the content _A_ of a counterfactual ‘if’-clause as follows: 

**==> picture [124 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

>  Some epistemic expressions, most notably expressions of probability, may not (only) be interpreted relative to a domain but relative to a probability function. In such cases, imaging as defined in the previous chapter would already fit the required structure. 

 restriction and modification 

Here we represent the modified domain as resulting from summing up all the sets of antecedent-worlds which are relevant relative to at least one world we take to be epistemically possible (in a dynamic setting, Starr : Sect. .uses this approach to define expansions of domains; cf. also Veltman ). 

To complete the account, let us now also state the clause for how a modal expression could get modified in the context of a counterfactual: 

() �[MODAL :: A][ _B_ ]� _[E]_ = �MODAL� _[F][(][E]_[,][�] _[A]_[�] _[)]_ � _B_ �. Given that the modal’s initial domain is _E_ , the counterfactual antecedent’s semantic value would modify this domain to _F(E_ , � _A_ � _)_ . Relative to this modified domain, the modal would then be evaluated by feeding in the semantic value of the consequent as its main argument. Call this way of explaining the interaction of counterfactuals with epistemic modals the _modifier view_ . 

To see the explanatory potential of this approach, let us sketch how it resolves the problem posed by the Oswald/Kennedy case. Recall the two sample sentences 

- () It is likely that if Oswald did not kill Kennedy, someone else did. 

- () It is likely that if Oswald had not killed Kennedy, someone else would have. 

The problem is that the first ascription of probability is fine whereas the second is not warranted. If we were to assume that the epistemic phrase is restricted in both cases by the ‘if‘-clause, we would not be able to mark this difference. This problem disappears once we assume that the epistemic phrase is restricted only in the first case but modified in the second one. For the counterfactual to be likely, it would need to be likely that someone else would have killed Kennedy from an epistemic view point obtained by counterfactually supposing that Oswald had not killed Kennedy. But from this perspective, someone killing Kennedy is not likely. Modification is capable of reflecting this way of evaluating counterfactual thoughts by allowing partial expansions of our domain of epistemic possibilities. 

restriction and modification  

## .Variants of the View 

The restrictor view and its extension, the modifier view, is just one representative of a family of views which provide non-propositional explanations of the data about the subjective probabilities of conditionals. There are at least two further approaches which deserve mentioning: _dynamic semantics_ and _the suppositional view_ of conditionals. In this section, I will briefly explain why I think that these two views can to a large extent be seen as variants of the restrictor view. I also think that they face similar problems (to which we will turn in the next two sections), but I will not attempt to argue for this further claim. 

Start with dynamic semantics of conditionals. In a dynamic framework, the basic notion is that of a _context-change potential_ of a sentence. It is designed to capture the effect an affirmative utterance of that sentence would have on a given context. A context can be seen as a state of information _s_ represented by a set of possible worlds. The semantic role of a sentence _φ_ is taken to be a function from contexts to contexts describing how the sentence changes a context. The update of a state _s_ with a sentence _φ_ is typically written as _s_ [ _φ_ ]. Updates can be _classical_ in the sense that the update _s_ [ _φ_ ] is always the intersection of _s_ with a set of worlds coming from a classical interpretation � _φ_ � of _φ_ , i.e. for all _s_ , _s_ [ _φ_ ] = _s_ ∩ � _φ_ �. But not all sentences need to have a classical context-change potential. A state _s_ is said to _accept_ a sentence _φ_ , formally _s_ |= _φ_ , iff the update of _s_ with _φ_ does not change _s_ , i.e. _s_ [ _φ_ ] = _s_ (cf. Veltman ). Acceptance means that the information provided by _φ_ is already contained in _s_ . If the update by _φ_ is classical, this means that _s_ is contained in � _φ_ �, i.e. _s_ seen as a proposition implies � _φ_ �. 

In a dynamic framework, the semantics of conditionals is very similar to the restrictor view: the antecedent is taken to temporarily update the context with the antecedent and it is then checked whether the consequent is accepted in the updated context (cf. Gillies , ). So, a conditional ‘if _φ_ , _ψ_ ’ is accepted in a context _s_ iff the consequent is accepted in the context resulting from updating the original context with the information contained in the antecedent, i.e. iff _s_ [ _φ_ ] |= _ψ_ . Effectively, the antecedent is taken to restrict the original context and it is then checked whether the consequent is accepted in the restricted context (in a non-dynamic setting, this semantics corresponds to a strict epistemic 

 restriction and modification 

conditional, i.e. to a material conditional governed by an epistemic necessity operator). 

A dynamic semantics for counterfactuals can be given along the lines of the modifier view. Here one would say that a counterfactual is accepted in a context iff the consequent is accepted in the context resulting from the original context by modifying it with the information of the antecedent (cf. Starr , Veltman ; see also von Fintel ). There are at least two interesting differences from the restrictor view as sketched in the previous sections. One is that a dynamic semantics of conditionals can be supplemented with a genuinely dynamic notion of consequence (see Veltman : sec. .for discussion of various ways of doing so). Another is that modals attaching to conditionals are typically reconstructed as having narrow scope over the consequent only. The antecedent of the conditional is first taken to create a temporarily updated context relative to which the modalized consequent can then be evaluated. But the ultimate effect on the interpretation of the modal is the same. Both on the domain restrictor view and on the dynamic view the modal is interpreted relative to the restriction of the original domain by the information contained in the antecedent. 

There is also a close connection between the restrictor view and the supposition view of conditionals.[] On the suppositional view, the core function of an ‘if’-clause is to express a supposition in the scope of which the consequent is to be evaluated. Epistemic attitudes towards a conditional are not categorical attitudes towards a proposition expressed by the counterfactual as a whole. Rather, they are conditional attitudes towards the consequent of the conditional relative to the supposition of the antecedent. For instance, having a high credence towards a conditional is 

>  The suppositional view has already a fairly long history. Quine () mentions the suppositional view and attributes it to Philip Rhinelander. Von Wright (: ch. ) may contain the first systematic development of it. Other early proponents of the suppositional view include Adams (, ), Dummett (, : –) and Belnap (). More recent proponents include Barnett () and Edgington (, ). Certain authors like Bennett () and Gibbard () accept the suppositional view for indicative conditionals, but reject it for counterfactuals. A special case is Woods (), who accepts the suppositional view only for indicative conditionals which do not have ‘will’ as the main auxiliary verb in the consequent. Until recently, there was a stronger focus on indicative conditionals, though Adams () has already a chapter on counterfactuals. But over the last years Barnett () and Edgington () have discussed the counterfactual case in more detail. 

restriction and modification  

explained as having a high conditional credence in the consequent under the assumption of the antecedent. The suppositional view offers a parallel story for assertions of conditionals and other kinds of speech-acts. Asserting a conditional is not taken as a categorical assertion, but rather as an assertion of the consequent relative to the supposition of the antecedent. 

As we have seen, restriction and modification are linguistic analogues of indicative and counterfactual suppositions. Restricting the domain of an epistemic expression has the same effect on the domain as making an indicative supposition would have on a belief state which is adequately represented by that domain. The same holds for the relation between modification and a counterfactual supposition. The suppositional view can therefore be seen as a non-linguistic variant of the restrictor view. 

The suppositional view is compatible with how the restrictor view explains embeddings of conditionals under restrictable operators. Actually, this is probably the most natural way proponents of the suppositional view can account for such embeddings. The difference between the two views shows up in how they account for unembedded conditionals. While the restrictor view posits an implicit operator for the ‘if’-clause to restrict, proponents of the suppositional view typically hold that conditionals do not have (ordinary) truth conditions. However, it is not hard to see how the semantics of the restrictor view could be modified to yield this result. One could simply not posit an implicit operator for unembedded conditionals. In the interpretation of simple conditionals, the operator slot would be left open (we may call such interpretations _open interpretations_ ). Simple conditionals would then not have truth conditions. They would only express a conditional construction whose modal force is left unspecified. It would, of course, be a further question whether one could derive all the properties the supposition view attributes to conditionals from open interpretations of conditionals. 

One advantage of the suppositional view is that it allows for an explanation of the subjective probabilities of conditionals. As we have noticed in passing, the restrictor view merely explains how conditionals embed under epistemic phrases which might express possibility, high probability or certainty. So, the restrictor view, or rather its extension in the form of the modifier view, has a story about why the complex construction 

() It is likely that if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost 

 restriction and modification 

can be true. It is not designed to explain how it is that we are _fairly certain_ about the embedded counterfactual when it occurs unembedded: 

() If I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost. 

It is clear that what we are not fairly certain about is that the standard truth conditions obtain. So, it is not an option for the restrictor view to account for our credences by construing them as credences in a proposition which would be expressed by the counterfactual if we took it to be implicitly modalized by a necessity operator so that it would have the standard truth conditions. In contrast, the suppositional view is designed to have an explanation for this type of case. Our credences are not construed as ordinary credences in a proposition; they are rather taken to be a specific kind of conditional credence attaching to the consequent in the scope of counterfactually supposing the antecedent. If the restrictor view could adopt this story by saying that our intuitions about credences derive from open interpretations of conditionals, it would gain significantly in explanatory power. 

The absence of truth conditions on the suppositional view becomes a problem when conditionals are embedded in contexts where the ‘if’clause cannot restrict the embedding phrase, say when the conditional occurs as a conjunct in a larger construction (see the exchange between Edgington and Kölbel ). Here it faces a variant of the FregeGeach problem. Taking conditionals to be implicitly modalized in such constructions as it is assumed on the restrictor view would avoid this kind of problem, although we will see below that there may still be a more specific problem of embedding for the restrictor view, too. 

## .The Lack of Independent Evidence 

On the restrictor view, indicative conditionals interact with a variety of quantifiers and other operators in the same way as they interact with epistemic modals. Thus, the interaction between epistemic modals and indicative ‘if’-clauses is not described as an isolated phenomenon, but rather as an instance of a much more general pattern. Given this, one would expect that the operation of modification which can be employed to explain the interaction of epistemic modals with counterfactual ‘if’-clauses will show up at other occasions as well. Somewhat surprisingly, this does not seem to be the case. As far as I can see, the 

restriction and modification  

modifier view lacks independent evidence. Let us look at some relevant cases. 

Start with the classical case of adverbs of quantification employed in Lewis to highlight the restrictive function of ‘if’-clauses. The problem here is that adverbs such as ‘sometimes’, ‘always’, ‘often’, or ‘usually’ do not happily combine with counterfactuals. Compare 

() Sometimes/Always/Often/Usually, if I buy a lottery ticket, I win with 

- () ? Sometimes/always/often/usually, if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have won 

and 

- () ? Sometimes/always/often/usually, if I were to buy a lottery ticket, I would win. 

In the indicative case, the ‘if’-clause can be interpreted as restricting the adverbial quantifiers. Substituting the indicative clause with its counterfactual variant, either past or present, yields sentences which are hard to interpret.[] The counterfactuals may not be completely uninterpretable, though. With a bit of a stretch, it might be possible to hear them as saying, for instance, that there are certain times _t_ , such that if I had bought a ticket at _t_ , I would have won. But note that if such interpretations are available, the counterfactual ‘if’-clause does not seem to restrict, let alone modify the adverbial quantifier. The constructions used to motivate the restrictor view in the indicative case cannot be used to motivate the modifier view in the counterfactual case. 

In the indicative case, there is some evidence that ‘if’-clauses might be able to restrict nominal quantifiers. Consider, for instance, 

- () Most letters are answered if they are shorter than pages. () Few people like New York if they didn’t grow up there.[] 

>  That these counterfactuals are problematic was brought to my attention by Manfred Krifka in discussion after a talk in Berlin in . I used to think that these constructions are strictly uninterpretable. Various participants at a workshop on conditionals in Konstanz in persuaded me that this may not be the case. 

>  These examples are given in von Fintel and Iatridou () where they are attributed to Heim and/or Kratzer. 

 restriction and modification 

It seems quite natural to hear these sentences as equivalent to variants of them in which a relative clause restricts the quantifier phrase: 

- () Most letters which are shorter than pages are answered. 

- () Few people who didn’t grow up there like New York. 

Contrast the indicative constructions with their counterfactual counterparts: 

- () Most letters would have been answered if they had been shorter than pages. 

- () Few people would have liked New York if they had not grown up there. 

In neither case does the counterfactual ‘if’-clause modify the domain of quantification. Nor does it seem to restrict the domain in a way analogous to the indicative case. Where () has an interpretation on which the domain consists of the letters shorter than pages, the domain in (), ()’s counterfactual variant, seems to consist of all (contextually salient) letters, not somehow of the letters satisfying the antecedent of the counterfactual. Rather, most of all letters are said to have the complex counterfactual property of having been answered if they had been shorter than pages. 

In the literature about the interaction between conditionals and deontic modals, there is evidence that indicative conditionals with a deontic modal governing their consequent sometimes receive an interpretation according to which the ‘if’-clause is taken to be a restrictor. Consider, for example, 

- () If Beryl is smoking marijuana, we should report her to the police.[] 

There seems to be an interpretation of () according to which it expresses a conditional obligation rather than an unconditional obligation made on the assumption of Beryl smoking marijuana. 

The possibility of such an interpretation is clearly absent in the counterfactual case: 

>  This example is due to Geurts (). 

restriction and modification  

- () If Beryl had been smoking marijuana, we should have reported her to the police. 

More importantly even, we do not get a reading on which the counterfactual ‘if’-clause would somehow modify the deontic modal.[] 

So far, we have seen pairs of cases in which the counterfactual variants could neither be interpreted as modifiers nor as restrictors. Under certain constructions of objective modality, we seem, however, to find cases in which counterfactual ‘if’-clauses act as restrictors. Consider: 

- () There was a high chance that Chelsea would have won if the referee had not denied them a penalty. 

If one finds that this example is an expression of past high conditional objective chance—which is an intuitively plausible verdict—then we could account for this by assuming that the counterfactual ‘if’-clause restricts the chance operator. It is important to note that in order to get this interpretation, we would only need the counterfactual ‘if’-clause to be a restrictor and not a modifier, for simple conditionalization is achieved through the restriction of a probability function, not somehow through a modification of its domain. 

All in all, it seems that one cannot gather independent evidence for the modifier view. The way counterfactual ‘if’-clauses interact with certain epistemic phrases would be a fairly isolated phenomenon. How much of an objection is this against the modifier view? Perhaps the first thing to note is that the modifier view derives a further bit of evidence from the fact that it is a natural extension of the restrictor view. Jointly, the two views form a homogeneous package which provides a highly unified account of the two sets of data. Both in the indicative and in the counterfactual case, the puzzling constructions are explained by challenging the logical form usually attributed to them. But given that the modifier view of counterfactuals is based on a much smaller amount of evidence, there is clearly room for thinking about an alternative explanation of the relevant data. We shall now turn to what strikes me to be a deeper problem for the modifier view (and perhaps for the restrictor view in general). 

>  ‘Should’ can also be given an epistemic interpretation as might be salient in ‘If Beryl had been smoking marijuana, we should have noticed’. The claim made in the text is meant to concern deontic interpretations only. 

 restriction and modification 

## .The Problem of Embeddings 

There is a certain class of embedded occurrences of counterfactuals for which the restrictor view is designed to have an explanation. These are embeddings under various operators, in particular epistemic operators, which can be restricted or modified by an ‘if’-clause. As we shall see, there are other, fairly ordinary contexts like conjunction or existential generalization, where restriction or modification is not (always) a plausible option. It is at this point where the possibility of assigning truth conditions to conditionals by positing an implicit operator becomes a valuable tool, for one may assume that in such contexts conditionals contribute the truth conditions which would result from their implicit modalization. 

Although this suggests that the restrictor view has a ready-made story for embedded conditionals—the ‘if’-clause either restricts an overt or a covert operator—I will now present an argument which suggests that the problem cannot always be resolved in this way. I will focus here on counterfactuals only, but the structure of the problem should make us strongly suspect that there is a corresponding problem in the indicative case. What I will try to show is that the present solution leads to a certain kind of unwanted _transition failure_ . If counterfactuals play a vastly different role when they occur in the scope of certain epistemic operators and when they are embedded in other contexts, then certain transitions between these two kinds of context will not be possible. Intuitively, however, these transitions seem to be possible as we shall see. 

Let us start by considering the case in which the standard truth conditions are assigned to counterfactuals because the counterfactual ‘if’clause does not modify an overt operator. As will be obvious from the discussion to follow, our considerations will equally apply to close relatives of the standard truth conditions and in fact to many other possible truth conditions as well. But for ease of exposition, and since it is the most interesting case anyway, I will describe the problem with respect to the standard truth conditions. 

There are two important kinds of context with respect to which we can expect that counterfactuals would need to be assigned truth conditions. We have seen in the previous section that ordinary objectual quantifiers are not modifiable by counterfactual ‘if’-clauses. With respect to certain truth-functional connectives like conjunction, it does not even seem to be 

restriction and modification  

an option that they are somehow modified by counterfactual ‘if’-clauses. So, these expressions generate contexts for which the present strategy of explaining embeddings of counterfactuals in terms of truth conditions is likely to be employed. 

To illustrate these two kinds of context, I will focus on existential quantification and conjunction as in the following two examples: 

- () There is someone who would have got angry if I had criticized him.[] 

- () There is blood on the kitchen knife and if the cook had done it, there would have been blood on the kitchen knife. 

On the present suggestion, the embedded (open) counterfactual in the first example has the standard truth conditions within the scope of the existential quantifier. So, the sentence would be true just in case there is someone who gets angry at all relevant worlds at which I criticize him. Similarly, the counterfactual in the conjunction would contribute the standard truth conditions. The conjunction would then be true just in case there is blood on the kitchen knife and at all relevant worlds at which the cook commits the murder, there is blood on the kitchen knife. 

Now, to begin with, compare the counterfactual in the first example to the following occurrence of a closed variant of it within the scope of an epistemic operator: 

- () It is quite likely that if I had criticized Bobby, he would have got angry. 

For the sake of argument, suppose that this is a case in which the counterfactual ‘if’-clause ‘if I had criticized Bobby’ modifies the epistemic operator ‘it is quite likely’. According to the modifier view, the present example can be true if we take most but not all relevant worlds at which Bobby is criticized to be worlds at which he gets angry. Thus, for the example to be true, it does not have to be the case that we have sufficient information to be justified in thinking it likely that the standard truth conditions are satisfied. 

>  The choice of this example is inspired by the discussion of embeddings in Kölbel (). 

 restriction and modification 

The counterfactual in the present example comes from the open counterfactual used in example () above by substituting ‘Bobby’ for the quantified variable. Nevertheless, according to the modifier view, the two counterfactual ‘if’-clauses perform quite different roles in the two constructions. Within the environment of the existential quantifier, the counterfactual would contribute the standard truth conditions because the counterfactual ‘if’-clause will be taken to restrict or modify an implicit necessity operator (or alternatively the overt ‘would’; cf. fn. of this chapter). In the scope of the epistemic modal, however, the counterfactual ‘if’clause is taken to modify the epistemic operator. Modifying the epistemic operator will not be equivalent to taking the ‘if’-clause to first modify a covert necessity operator and then to apply the epistemic modal to the resulting sentence, for the resulting sentence will have the standard truth conditions and the interaction of epistemic modals and counterfactuals cannot be explained in terms of the standard truth conditions. 

Now, if such different roles are assumed, then certain transitions between the different sentential contexts are not possible. To see this, let us add the following example: 

- () It is quite likely that there is someone who would have got angry if I had criticized him. 

This example results from the original existential quantification (sample sentence ()) by applying the epistemic operator ‘it is quite likely’ to it. Now, intuitively, the present example is acceptable on the grounds of example (). If we think it quite likely that Bobby would have got angry if I had criticized him, then we seem to be entitled to think it quite likely that someone would have got angry if I had criticized him. The reason for this is simple: existential generalization remains to be valid when it comes to counterfactuals. So, from 

- () If I had criticized Bobby, he would have got angry 

the sample sentence 

- () There is someone who would have got angry if I had criticized him 

follows logically. The problem is that we seem to be justified in performing this inference even within the scope of a probability operator. 

restriction and modification  

However, the transition from () to () does not come out as justified on the modifier view. Since the counterfactual ‘if’-clause in () occurs within the scope of the existential quantifier (and is an open sentence anyway), it cannot be taken to modify the outer epistemic operator. As a consequence, the quantified counterfactual contributes in () the same proposition as () expresses in its standalone use. So, it will be true if it is likely that there is someone who is angry at all relevant worlds at which he is criticized. But taking this proposition to be likely is not always justified on the grounds of having a high relative credence that Bobby would have got angry on the supposition that I had criticized him. Having this modified epistemic attitude does not require having a high credence that Bobby gets angry at _all_ relevant worlds at which he is criticized. So, if Bobby is in the present context the only candidate for being criticized, then it is clear that having this modified epistemic attitude should not incline us to think it likely that there is someone who gets angry at _all_ relevant worlds at which he is criticized. The phenomenon of unwanted transition failures indicates that the modifier view cannot adequately deal with embedded counterfactuals as exemplified by (). 

To clarify the problem a little further, let us look at it from a slightly different angle. The sample sentences we are considering have the following surface structure: 

**==> picture [112 x 26] intentionally omitted <==**

In example (), a probability operator is applied to a counterfactual whose antecedent and consequent contain a singular term. And sample sentence () results from this example by existentially generalizing into the position of the singular term from within the scope of the probability operator. 

The way we use these sentences suggests that we take their surface structure to be genuine. By accepting a sentence of the first form, we feel entitled to accept a sentence of the second form based on the idea that if a certain proposition is likely, then one of its logical consequences will be likely as well. This is what is reflected in the fact that if we take a counterfactual to be likely, we feel entitled to accept an existential generalization on it to be likely, too. 

Now, the modifier view has it that the two sample sentences have quite different logical forms, and, most importantly, the logical role of 

 restriction and modification 

the counterfactual ‘if’-clause is taken to vary significantly in the two examples. To a good approximation, we may represent the logical forms that the present version of the modifier view assigns to these examples as follows: 

**==> picture [94 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [130 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

In the first example, the counterfactual ‘if’-clause is taken to modify the probability operator. In the second example, however, the overt probability operator cannot be modified. Rather, the counterfactual ‘if’-clause needs to be taken to restrict or modify a different operator. On the semantic level, the second example is then equivalent to assuming the counterfactual to contribute the standard truth conditions (where ‘□[→] ’ represents, as before, the counterfactual connective of standard semantics): 

**==> picture [107 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

So, we see that on the modifier view, the probability operator does not govern two sentences one of which follows logically from the other. As a consequence, the modifier view cannot provide a rationale for being justified in transiting from the first example to the second. The different roles assumed by the modifier view to be played by the counterfactual in these two constructions blocks the possibility of such a transition. Thus, the modifier view has to assume certain transition failures which are, as far as I can see, simply not there. 

Once the general structure of this problem is recognized, it is not hard to find more cases of unwanted transition failures. Consider, for instance, the inference from a counterfactual to a conjunction which conjoins this counterfactual with a trivial conjunct: 

- () It is likely that if the cook had done it, there would have been blood on the kitchen knife. 

- () It is likely that  +  = and if the cook had done it, there would have been blood on the kitchen knife. 

Assume that the first example is accounted for by taking the ‘if’-clause to modify the probability operator. As in the previous case, the second example should not be accounted for by assuming the ‘if’-clause to modify 

restriction and modification  

the probability operator, for otherwise one would need to move the sentence ‘ +  = ’ into the scope of the ‘if’-clause. For this reason, the strategy under present consideration for dealing with embeddings is to assume that the counterfactual conjunct contributes the standard truth conditions. But then we are not justified in transiting from the first to the second example, for we may accept the first example without taking it to be likely that at _all_ relevant worlds at which the cook does it, there is blood on the kitchen knife. So, on the modifier view, the application of the probability operator to the conjunction would yield a much stronger probability statement than its application to the single counterfactual. However, the conjunction and the single counterfactual seem to be epistemically equally likely. 

Similarly, consider also the following case. On the basis of 

() It is certain that there is blood on the kitchen knife and 

- () It is likely that if the cook had done it, there would have been blood on the kitchen knife, 

we seem to be entitled to accept 

- () It is likely that there is blood on the kitchen knife and if the cook had done it, there would have been blood on the kitchen knife.[] 

Now, if the likelihood of the counterfactual is explained by assuming ‘it is likely’ to be modified by the counterfactual ‘if’-clause but the counterfactual contributes the standard truth conditions when it is part of the final conjunction, then the conjunction could be unlikely despite the fact that the two starting ascriptions of subjective probability are correct. This consequence is unattractive. 

Up to this point, we have discussed the problem for the modifier view as it arises when the modifier view is combined with the standard truth conditions for simple counterfactuals. But a more general conclusion is warranted in the light of the structure of the problem. It seems that the problem is primarily caused by assigning counterfactuals different roles in contexts which may be epistemically closely related. For this reason, 

>  There is an underlying fact about probabilities grounding this entitlement: if _P(A)_ =  and _P(B)_ = _x_ , then _P(A_ ∧ _B)_ = _x_ . 

 restriction and modification 

we can expect that it will also arise for assignments of truth conditions other than the standard truth conditions as long as these truth conditions are not apt to explain the interaction of counterfactuals with epistemic phrases and so require counterfactual ‘if’-clauses to sometimes modify epistemic phrases. 

In sum, the situation may be assessed in the following way. The modifier view gains explanatory potential by combining the possibility of explaining the interaction of counterfactuals with epistemic operators as a certain kind of modification while still allowing counterfactuals in other contexts to contribute the standard truth conditions. However, despite its flexibility, this strategy of dealing in two radically different ways with different kinds of embedded counterfactuals has a serious drawback: it cannot explain why we are often justified in transiting between two contexts for which different explanations are given. Even worse, it predicts that we are not justified in doing so. 

## .Conclusion 

Let us take stock. Counterfactuals embed under epistemic operators similar to how indicative conditionals do. Perhaps the currently most popular way of explaining the data in the indicative case is to assume that ‘if’clauses act as restrictors in the problematic cases. This leads naturally to the question whether the interaction between epistemic operators and counterfactuals can be explained in an analogous way. However, the most straightforward explanation fails. Counterfactuals cannot be taken as restrictors in the relevant constructions. But it is possible to extend the restrictor view by allowing counterfactual ‘if’-clauses to act as modifiers. As it turns out, a theory of modification can be developed by explaining the operation of domain modification along the lines of imaging. However, the resulting view is not without problems. First, there does not seem to be any independent evidence for the assumption that counterfactual ‘if’-clauses can act as modifiers. Secondly, and more importantly, the view still faces a problem of embedding, for it fails to account for natural transitions between contexts in which an ‘if’-clause is supposed to act as a modifier of an overt epistemic phrase and an inferentially related context in which it has to be taken to restrict a different covert operator. This problem would not occur if we were able to give a propositional 

restriction and modification  

explanation of the way counterfactuals embed under epistemic operators. So, it seems worth exploring possible ways we might explain the problematic data in terms of propositions after all. This will be the task for the upcoming chapter. Nevertheless, the modifier view remains a serious fall-back option should all attempts to give a propositional explanation ultimately fail. In this case, one may simply have to live with its costs, which could perhaps be further reduced by some pragmatic manoeuvres. 

## **** 

## Counterfactuals and Arbitrariness 

Here is where we are. Counterfactuals interact with epistemic phrases in a surprising way reflecting a pattern which suggests that our thinking about counterfactuals is coordinated by a certain conception of conditional counterfactual chances (chapter ).
