We can pin this pattern down in the form of an evaluation constraint resembling Lewis’s Principal Principle: on the assumption that the counterfactual chances are thus-andso, our credence in the corresponding counterfactual should be thusand-so. At this point, problems arise. The evaluation constraint is in apparent conflict with the very possibility of assigning truth conditions to counterfactuals (chapter ). In the light of this danger, one may start looking for a non-propositional explanation of the relevant data. A theory of this kind emerges from the restrictor view of conditionals (chapter ). On a generalization of this view, we may assume that counterfactual ‘if’-clauses are capable of modifying certain epistemic phrases. On the epistemic level, we may take this to show that the credences in counterfactuals as reported by the evaluation constraint are not ordinary credences in a proposition but rather a special sort of modified credences: credences in the consequent within the scope of the counterfactual supposition of the antecedent. The main problem with this view is that when counterfactuals are embedded into larger contexts, they do not seem to play a different role from when they occur unembedded or within the scope of an epistemic operator. The most natural conclusion to draw from this problem would be to assume that the behaviour of counterfactuals should be given a unified explanation in terms of propositions after all. At this stage, however, we are back where we started, facing the argument that there simply may be no propositions which can do the job. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

But we have also seen in our analysis of the puzzle about truth conditions that it rests on an epistemological principle which does not hold in full generality despite being plausible in many cases. If counterfactuals have an epistemic profile which escapes this principle, we may be able to assign them truth conditions after all. In this chapter, we will explore this possible niche. I will present a semantics for counterfactuals on the basis of which I hope to be able to explain the evaluation of counterfactuals. The solution I offer stays fairly close to standard semantics. Instead of the universal quantifier employed in the standard specification of truth conditions, I will suggest using the epsilon-operator which arbitrarily selects a relevant antecedent-world. As we shall see, the epsilon-operator has some special epistemic properties whereby one may escape the arguments against truth conditions for counterfactuals. 

## .A Proposal 

In a search for adequate truth conditions for counterfactuals, the two main options are either truth conditions which are weaker than the standard truth conditions or truth conditions which are at least as strong as the standard truth conditions. Both options are called into question by the general argument against truth conditions (see chapter ). However, if truth conditions are nonetheless wanted, opting for weaker truth conditions seems to be the more promising possibility, for the argument against weaker truth conditions does not seem quite as watertight as the argument against stronger ones. The argument against weaker truth conditions relies on assumptions about knowability which are incompatible with the existence of unknowable truths. Moreover, they rule out cases in which our epistemic access to a proposition _A_ is such that we can come to know _A_ only by being in a position to know a proposition _B_ stronger than _A_ . Applied to counterfactuals, this opens up the possibility that there may be truth conditions for counterfactuals which are weaker than the standard truth conditions but which we can know to obtain only by being in a position to know that some stronger conditions—possibly the standard truth conditions—obtain. 

The structure of Stalnaker’s () semantics can be seen as providing the basis for truth conditions which are weaker than the standard truth conditions, for if all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds, 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

then a particular relevant antecedent-world is a consequent-world (but not _vice versa_ ). Yet Stalnaker’s original background assumption that there is always a single most relevant antecedent-world is implausible.[] Standard semantics seems to be right in assuming that there are usually many relevant antecedent-worlds. It would be nice to preserve this assumption in an attempt to develop weaker truth conditions along the lines of Stalnaker’s semantics. 

The basic idea will be to substitute in the standard account the epsilonoperator for the universal quantifier. Intuitively, the epsilon-operator _arbitrarily_ selects a world out of the set of relevant antecedent-worlds. A counterfactual will then be true if the consequent is true at the arbitrarily selected antecedent-world. These truth conditions are weaker than the standard truth conditions: if all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds, then an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world is a consequent-world, but an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world may be a consequent-world without all antecedent-worlds being consequentworlds. One can already see how this provides a solution to the puzzle posed by the evaluation of counterfactuals. On the one hand, we can think it likely that an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world is a consequent-world without thinking it likely that all antecedent-worlds are consequent-words. This can happen, for instance, when we think that most but not all antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. On the other hand, we can only know that an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world is a consequent-world if we are in a position to know that all antecedentworlds are consequent-worlds. 

The element of arbitrariness present in such a semantics can be motivated by paralleling ‘would’-expressions with ‘will’-sentences. As remarked in the introductory chapter, it is a common linguistic view that ‘would’ is derived from ‘will’ and can be regarded as a specific kind of past tense form of ‘will’. For this reason, it is a natural assumption that the semantics of ‘would’ should be similar to the semantics of ‘will’. As ‘will’ is of intermediate strength between future necessity and future possibility, the modal character of ‘would’ is likely to be of intermediate strength between counterfactual necessity and counterfactual possibility (see also section .). Now, the semantics for ‘will’ can be fixed—in some way or 

>  In response to the present problem, Stalnaker () suggests a modification of his theory along supervaluationist lines. We will briefly address this modification in section ... 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

other—by binding it to the one of the possibly many futures which will eventually be actual. A similar approach is not possible in the case of ‘would’. With respect to the many relevant counterfactual futures, there is no distinguished one which ultimately outlasts the others, for counterfactual futures do not play themselves out. One way to resolve this conflict is to bind the semantics of ‘would’ to an arbitrary counterfactual future. Before this line of thought can be developed in more detail, we will need to take a closer look at the epsilon-operator. 

## _..The Epsilon-Operator_ 

The epsilon-operator was originally introduced by Hilbert as part of his finitistic program.[] Nowadays, the epsilon-operator is sometimes employed in linguistics to account for _E_ -type pronouns and definite noun phrases.[] Philosophically, the epsilon-operator is of interest, for it is closely related to the use of schematic or arbitrary names in prominent systems of natural deduction. For this reason, issues concerning the epsilon-operator resemble issues in the philosophical debate about arbitrary names and reference to arbitrary objects. 

Let us start by saying something about the syntax of the epsilonoperator. The epsilon-operator is a term forming operator on formulas. If ⌜ _φ(x)_ ⌝ is a formula, then ⌜ _ϵxφ(x)_ ⌝ is a term (and not a formula such as ⌜∀ _xφ(x)_ ⌝). As a result, the expression ⌜ _φ(ϵxφ(x))_ ⌝ is a well-formed formula. Importantly, the variable _x_ is not free in an epsilon-term like ⌜ _ϵxφ(x)_ ⌝. The epsilon-operator acts in this respect like a quantifier which binds _x_ similar to a definite description operator. 

Semantically, an epsilon-term like ⌜ _ϵxφ(x)_ ⌝ can be thought of as denoting an arbitrarily selected object satisfying ⌜ _φ(x)_ ⌝. But in contrast to definite descriptions, no uniqueness assumption about _φ_ is made: an epsilon-term like ⌜ _ϵxφ(x)_ ⌝ is not taken to be empty if there are many _φ_ s. To give a semantic model for epsilon-terms like ⌜ _ϵxφ(x)_ ⌝, we will need a function which selects an object out of the set of _φ_ s. The epsilon-term can then be taken to denote the object so selected.[] Formally, a selection function is a function _s_ with the property that _s(A)_ ∈ _A_ (provided that _A_

>  See e.g. Slater () and von Heusinger (). 

>  This idea stems from Schröter (: ). 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

is non-empty). Then, if _A_ is the extension of the formula ⌜ _φ(x)_ ⌝, we can say that ⌜ _ϵxφ(x)_ ⌝ denotes _s(A)_ . 

Based on the present idea, Asser () has developed a comprehensive model theory for the epsilon-operator. To begin with, we take a firstorder language and supplement it with the epsilon-operator. We may then define a model _M_ for this language to be a triple � _D_ , _I_ , _s_ � with the following properties: 

- _D_ is any non-empty set (the _domain_ of discourse). 

- _I_ is an _interpretation function_ which assigns to any constant an object in _D_ , to any _n_ -ary predicate an _n_ -ary relation over _D_ , and to any _n_ -ary function symbol a function from the _n_ -ary Cartesian product of _D_ into _D_ . 

- _s_ is a function _P(D)_ → _D_ such that _s(A)_ ∈ _A_ if _A_ ̸= Ø. 

The first two components of such a model are familiar. What is new is the selection function: its sole purpose is to account for epsilon-terms. 

The next step would be to define what it means for a formula _φ_ to hold in such a model _M_ relative to a variable assignment _β_ (formally, _M_ , _β_ |= _φ_ ). As it turns out, the fact that the epsilon-operator is a term forming operator on formulas complicates the definition considerably. But the basic idea is simple: add to the standard definition a clause which says that epsilon-terms are assigned their semantic value by the selection function. More formally, assume that _M_ is a model and _β_ a variable assignment. If _v_ is a variable and _a_ an object in the domain, then _β[a] v_ is a variable assignment which differs from _β_ only in assigning _a_ to the variable _v_ . For a set of sentences _�_ and a sentence _φ_ , the expression ⌜ _�_ |= _φ_ ⌝ is defined in the standard way. Now, if _Rβ_ is the denotation function which assigns to each term a referent, then we will define this function in a way such that the following equation is satisfied: 

**==> picture [190 x 20] intentionally omitted <==**

Thus, epsilon-terms are assigned referents by applying the selection function _s_ to the set of objects satisfying the formula which makes up the epsilon-term. 

>  Cf. von Heusinger (: ). 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

Let me briefly comment on epsilon-terms which contain an empty formula. If _φ_ is not true of any objects in the domain, then the epsilonterm ⌜ _ϵvφ_ ⌝ is still assigned a semantic value, namely _s(_ Ø _)_ . Of course, the selected object cannot satisfy _φ_ . To some extent, this may be felt to be counterintuitive: how can a descriptive term be denoting if nothing satisfies the description? However, on the present stipulation, the semantics remains classical which spares us a lot of work.[] But let me point out that in the application to counterfactuals below, nothing will depend on this convention. 

Asser () axiomatized the resulting logic and proved it to be sound and complete. As it turns out, the epsilon-operator is governed by two basic axioms. The first axiom for the epsilon-operator has already been implicitly assumed: 

**Epsilon.** ∃ _vφ(v)_ ⊃ _φ(ϵvφ(v))_ .[] 

In words: If there is an object satisfying _φ_ , then the arbitrary object _ϵvφ(v)_ satisfies _φ_ . This is very intuitive: an arbitrary _φ_ should always be a _φ_ provided that there is a _φ_ . This axiom is a theorem according to the present semantics, for the denotation of an epsilon-term is always an element of the extension of the embedded formula as long as this extension is nonempty. 

The second basic axiom is this: 

**Extensionality.** ∀ _v(φ(v)_ ≡ _ψ(v))_ ⊃ _ϵvφ(v)_ = _ϵvψ(v)_ . 

Informally: If two formulas are materially equivalent, the corresponding epsilon-terms denote the same object. This ensures that the semantic value of an epsilon-term depends only on the extension of the embedded formula. It is clear that this axiom holds in the present semantics, for the selection function takes only the extension of the relevant formula as an argument. 

It is a common thought that a canonical way of proving a universally quantified formula is to show of an arbitrarily selected object that it satisfies this formula. In the light of this, it is a natural question whether a

>  Assume that ⌜∃ _vφ(v)_ ⌝ is a closed formula (a sentence). If we allow it to be open, a proviso is necessary to avoid an unwanted interplay of the variables in the epsilon-term and the quantifiers in the original formula. Cf. Asser (: ). 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

universally quantified sentence ⌜∀ _xφ(x)_ ⌝ is implied by ⌜ _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ ⌝. The question is whether ‘Everything is a _φ_ ’ is implied by ‘An arbitrary object is a _φ_ ’. When we formalize this latter sentence, we somehow need to express that the selection of objects is unrestricted. A good way of doing so seems to be to take a tautologous formula such as ⌜ _x_ = _x_ ⌝ which is logically guaranteed to be true of everything. 

It is not hard to see, though, that this implication does not hold on the present semantics. This is because all that is required for an epsilonformula such as ⌜ _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ ⌝ to be true in a model is that the selection function picks an object which is _φ_ . But this is possible without all objects being _φ_ s. To put the point slightly differently: an epsilon-formula such ⌜ ⌝ as _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ is construed as a formula about a particular object, namely _ϵx(x_ = _x)_ , and this object may be _φ_ without all objects being _φ_ . 

Nevertheless, there is a more qualified relation between epsilon-terms and the universal quantifier which can do justice to the convention of proving a universally quantified formula by proving—given certain side conditions—of an arbitrary object that it satisfies the formula: 

**Epsilon-Terms and Generality.** Let _�_ be a set of sentences which do not contain the epsilon-operator and let _φ(x)_ be a formula which does not contain the epsilon-operator either. Then 

**==> picture [141 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

Less formally: If it follows from a set of sentences that an arbitrary object is _φ_ , then it also follows that everything is _φ_ and _vice versa_ . The side conditions should be no surprise: they exclude that any particular assumptions about _ϵxφ(x)_ enter the stage. 

A remark on how the present observation can be proven. Take a model _M_ which verifies _�_ . Since _�_ does not contain the epsilon-operator, any model which differs from _M_ only in the choice of the selection function will verify _�_ as well. So, _M_ verifies ⌜ _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ ⌝ no matter which ⌜ ⌝ object is assigned to the epsilon-term _ϵx(x_ = _x)_ . Since _φ_ does not contain the epsilon-operator either, the extension of _φ_ is independent of the semantic values of epsilon-terms. Hence, _M_ verifies _φ(x)_ no matter which object is assigned to its free variable showing ⌜ _M_ |= ∀ _xφ(x)_ ⌝. So, if _�_ |= _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ , then _�_ |= ∀ _xφ(x)_ . The other direction is an immediate consequence of universal instantiation.[] 

>  One may also wonder whether the same kind of equivalence holds between ⌜∀ _x(φ(x)_ ⊃ _ψ(x))_ ⌝ (‘All _φ_ -s are _ψ_ -s’) and ⌜ _ψ(ϵxφ(x))_ ⌝ (An arbitrary _φ_ is a _ψ_ ’). But when the universal 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

The relation between epsilon-terms and generality is reflected in the epistemic profile of sentences containing epsilon-terms. We can be justified to a high degree that an arbitrarily selected object has a certain property without being equally justified in thinking that everything has this property. If we know that most but not all objects have a certain property, we are justified to a high degree that an arbitrarily selected object has this property. However, we are not justified in thinking that all objects have this property. The possibility of having high credence in ⌜ ⌝ ⌝ _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ without having high credence in ⌜∀ _xφ(x)_ reflects the fact that a sentence of the form ⌜ _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ ⌝ does not imply ⌜∀ _xφ(x)_ ⌝. The situation changes when we consider knowledge. If we are in a position to know that an arbitrarily selected object has a certain property, we are in a position to know that everything has this property (provided we do not know anything particular about the object in question). Logically, this reflects the fact that if we can prove ⌜ _φ(ϵx(x_ = _x))_ ⌝ (without making any specific assumptions about the epsilon-term), we can prove ⌜∀ _xφ(x)_ ⌝. 

The epistemic profile of sentences containing epsilon-terms is exactly of the kind we are looking for. There are logically stronger sentences, namely the corresponding universally quantified sentences, such that (a) we can be justified in assigning a high subjective probability to the epsilon-sentences without assigning a high subjective probability to these stronger sentences and (b) we can only know the epsilon-sentences to be true if we are in a position to know the corresponding stronger sentences to be true. Thus, epsilon-terms stand to the corresponding universally quantified sentences in the same epistemic relation as counterfactuals seem to stand to the standard truth conditions which are specified by a universal quantifier. For this reason, it seems promising to see whether we can give a semantics of counterfactuals by using the epsilon-operator. 

## _..Arbitrary Truth Conditions_ 

Equipped with the epsilon-operator, we are able to talk about arbitrary worlds in our semantics. For instance, given a world _w_ and a set of worlds _A_ , _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(A))_ is an arbitrary relevant _A_ -world (relative to _w_ ). With this possibility in mind, the proposal I would like to make can be stated as follows: 

quantification is vacuously true, the epsilon-sentence need not hold. However, once _�_ proves ⌜∃ _xφ(x)_ ⌝, the equivalence holds. This problem is avoided in the present theorem, for in classical logic, ⌜∃ _x(x_ = _x)_ ⌝ is a logical theorem and therefore implied by any set of assumptions. 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

**Epsilon-Based Truth Conditions.** Let _w_ be any world and ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ a counterfactual. Then _w_ |= ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ iff 

**==> picture [185 x 12] intentionally omitted <==**

In words: A counterfactual is true at a world _w_ if either there is no relevant antecedent world or, if there is one, the consequent is true at the arbitrarily selected relevant antecedent-world (relative to _w_ ). 

A few remarks on how these truth conditions are fixed. They are specified by a material conditional, the antecedent of which expresses that there is a relevant antecedent-world. If there is none, the counterfactual comes out true. Thus, the present truth conditions share with the truth conditions of standard semantics the feature that counterfactuals with an impossible antecedent are vacuously true. This feature is to a large extent a side effect of the present account, for it is brought about by a clause specifically designed for this case. _Prima facie_ , there is no reason why the core of the present semantics could not be extended in a different way to allow some counterfactuals with an impossible antecedent to be false.[] 

The main part of the specification of truth conditions is contained in the consequent of the material conditional. The selection function _f_ takes the world _w_ and the set � _φ_ � of worlds at which the antecedent is true as arguments and provides the set _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ of relevant antecedentworlds. Now, the epsilon-operator arbitrarily selects one of these worlds. The whole clause is then true if the consequent is true at this arbitrarily selected world. 

To compare the present semantics to the standard semantics, let us recall the formal specification of the standard truth conditions: 

**Standard Semantics.** Let _w_ be any world and ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ a counterfactual. Then _w_ |= ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ iff 

**==> picture [121 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

The present kind of truth conditions is indeed weaker than the standard truth conditions. Suppose that the standard truth conditions are satisfied. If this is because there are no relevant antecedent-worlds, then the 

>  For a recent and extensive defense of the claim that counterpossibles are always true, see Williamson (forthcoming). Considerations against can be found in Nolan () and Brogaard and Salerno (). See also Fine (a,b) for potential counterexamples against the related claim that counterfactuals do not create hyper-intensional contexts. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

arbitrary truth conditions are satisfied as well. Otherwise the set _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ will be non-empty and all worlds in it will verify the consequent of the counterfactual. But then an arbitrarily selected world out of this set has to verify the consequent. So, the standard truth conditions imply the epsilon-based truth conditions. The converse will generally not be the case, for an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world may verify the consequent without all antecedent-worlds doing so. 

Structurally, we have arrived at a kind of Stalnaker semantics. Whether or not a counterfactual is true generally depends on whether the consequent is true at a single world. Stalnaker’s original theory is better described with a definite description operator: the truth conditions of counterfactuals are given by what is true at _the most relevant antecedentworld_ . As already noted, the problem for Stalnaker’s original suggestion is that there is only rarely, if ever, a single most relevant antecedentworld. On the present account, this difficulty is avoided. It is actually an integral part of the present theory that there are usually many relevant antecedent-worlds. Stalnaker () refined his original account by supervaluating over all the candidates for being the closest antecedent-world. From the perspective of the present theory, the precisifications picking a unique closest antecedent-world correspond to the possible arbitrary selections of an antecedent-world out of the set of relevant antecedentworlds (see also section ..). 

_..The Logic of Counterfactuals_ 

So far, I have given an outline of an alternative semantics for counterfactuals. What remains to be done is to show how the logic of counterfactuals can be approached within the present framework. In standard semantics, the logic of counterfactuals can be adjusted by imposing constraints on the selection function _f_ which provides the set of relevant antecedentworlds (we have looked at these constraints in section .). Since the present semantics is given partly in terms of this function, we can influence the logic in this way as well by constraining the sets from which the epsilon-operator selects its referents. As we shall see, one may additionally want to consider introducing a certain kind of coordination constraint which is directly concerned with the epsilon-operator. 

Let us start by seeing how the constraints on the selection function influence the logic of counterfactuals. One requirement is that relevant antecedent-worlds are always antecedent-worlds, i.e. _fw(A)_ ⊆ _A_ . 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

This secures the logical truth of ⌜ _A > A_ ⌝, for if relevant antecedentworlds are always antecedent-worlds, then an arbitrarily selected relevant antecedent-world will be an antecedent-world. If _A_ = Ø, i.e. the antecedent is impossible, the constraint implies that _fw(A)_ = Ø as well, i.e. that there are no relevant antecedent-worlds. This makes counterfactuals with an impossible antecedent vacuously true. 

Another constraint requires that the selection function selects some antecedent-worlds unless there are no antecedent-worlds, i.e. _fw(A)_ = Ø implies _A_ = Ø. Just like in standard semantics, this constraint is important for our specification of truth conditions, too, as it guarantees that only counterfactuals with an impossible antecedent are vacuously true. 

Next there are the centering conditions. Recall that weak centering requires that if an antecedent _A_ is true at a world _w_ , then _w_ ∈ _fw(A)_ , which implies that if the antecedent is true at the actual world, then the actual world is a relevant world. Strong centering makes in this case the actual world the only relevant world, or, more generally, it requires { _w_ } = _fw(A)_ if _w_ ∈ _A_ . Perhaps the strongest reason for imposing the centering conditions is to ensure the validity of _modus ponens_ (we discuss another issue about centering in section .). On standard semantics, weak centering is sufficient for this purpose because if _A_ is true at the actual world and if _B_ is true at all relevant _A_ -worlds, then _B_ is true at the actual world, too, given that the actual world is a relevant _A_ -world. On the present semantics, we would have to adopt strong centering, however. In this case, if the antecedent is true at the actual world, the selected world will be the actual world as it is the only possible choice and so the truth of the corresponding counterfactual will require the truth of its consequent. Weak centering, on the other hand, would leave open the possibility that the selected antecedent-world is not the actual world and so modus ponens could fail. If one favours weak centering over strong centering as a description of the structure of relevance, one would need to impose an additional coordination constraint on the epsilon-operator (see below) or else accept failures of _modus ponens_ for counterfactuals (which strikes me as the less attractive option). 

If one compares the logic reached so far to Stalnaker’s () logic Cor to Lewis’s () official logic VC (which is weaker than the present logic in other respects), then it turns out that there is precisely one interesting principle missing (and some consequences of it). This principle states that 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

counterfactually equivalent propositions can be substituted for each other in the antecedent of a counterfactual: 

**Substitution.** _A > B_ , _B > A_ , _A > C_ |= _B > C_ . 

This principle is quite complex and cannot easily be tested against intuitions. After an early attempt by Tichy () to give a counterexample and a response by Stalnaker (: Ch. ), this principle and its semantic justification have recently been put to closer scrutiny. Tooley () discovered a certain type of counterexample to it the consequences of which have been further explored by Cross ().[] The counterexample by Tooley requires, however, the possibility of backward causation. If one takes the counterexample to be genuine, one might simply stop here and leave the logic as it is (this is the recommendation in Cross ). 

Although I find the counterexample by Tooley _prima facie_ very convincing, there are two considerations which speak in favour of having a backup plan. The first one is simply that it would be nice to prevent a semantics of counterfactuals from being hostage to the fortune that backward causation is really possible. A related thought starts with the observation that there will in any case be large parts of metaphysical space which are free from backward causation. Unless the counterexample can be generalized to cases not involving backward causation, we would expect the substitution principle to hold in such a restricted metaphysical environment. A good theory of counterfactuals should have the resources to bolster this expectation. 

In standard semantics, the substitution principle is validated because the selection function satisfies a constraint which was already mentioned in section .. To recall: 

**Equivalence for Relevance.** Let _w_ be any world, _A_ and _B_ be sets of worlds. If _fw(A)_ ⊆ _B_ and _fw(B)_ ⊆ _A_ , then 

**==> picture [61 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

Now, if the relevant _A_ -worlds are the relevant _B_ -worlds, then the truth of ⌜ _A > C_ ⌝ implies the truth of ⌜ _B > C_ ⌝ and so the substitution principle turns out to hold. 

>  Ahmed () also proposes a counterexample. But see Walters () for a case against it. Further discussion which is particularly relevant for the epsilon-based semantics can be found in Bacon (ms). 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

Actually, this equivalence condition is the consequence of a more general principle holding in standard semantics: 

**Generalized Equivalence.** Let _w_ be any world, _A_ and _B_ be sets of worlds. If _fw(A)_ ∩ _B_ ̸= Ø and _fw(B)_ ∩ _A_ ̸= Ø, then 

**==> picture [177 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

If the starting conditions of the original equivalence constraint are satisfied, then _fw(A)_ ∩ _B_ = _fw(A)_ and _fw(B)_ ∩ _A_ = _fw(B)_ . Hence, the generalized principle would give us _fw(A)_ ⊆ _fw(B)_ and _fw(B)_ ⊆ _fw(A)_ showing that the original principle is indeed implied by the generalized one.[] 

To see why the generalized equivalence principle is a consequence of the standard account, recall that the closeness relation is assumed to be total in the sense that any two worlds are comparable with respect to their closeness to a given third world (they may, of course, still be equally close). The function _f_ then takes a world _w_ and a proposition _A_ to the set of closest _A_ -worlds with respect to _w_ . Now, if some of the closest _A_ -worlds are _B_ -worlds and some of the closest _B_ -worlds are _A_ -worlds, these _(A_ & _B)_ -worlds are all comparable to each other. If one of those worlds, _w_[′] , were part of _fw(A)_ but not of _fw(B)_ , say, then _w_[′] would either be closer to _w_ than the _fw(B)_ -worlds or further away from them. In the first case, _fw(B)_ would not consist of the closest _B_ -worlds, for _w_ is a _B_ -world. In the second case, _fw(A)_ would not consist of the closest _A_ -worlds, for _fw(B)_ contains _A_ -worlds which are closer than _w_[′] . Thus, we see that the substitution principle effectively derives from the idea that the closeness relation is total combined with the general assumption that the truth of a counterfactual depends on what is true at the closest antecedent-worlds (cf. Cross , ). 

Let us now turn to how the substitution principle fares on the present semantics. To clarify its relation to the equivalence condition a little further, suppose that the counterfactuals ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ and ⌜ _B > A_ ⌝ are true. This would mean that the arbitrarily selected relevant _A_ -world is a _B_ -world ( _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(A))_ ∈ _B_ ) and the arbitrarily selected relevant _B_ -world is an _A_ -world ( _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(B))_ ∈ _A_ ). Now, if we further assume 

>  Hans Rott pointed out to me that the somewhat clumsy equivalence condition is provably equivalent to the conjunction of two more natural conditions known from rational choice theory and usually referred to as _α_ and _β_ +. I regret that I have not yet been able to explore these parallels further. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

that the counterfactual ⌜ _A > C_ ⌝ is true, we get the consequence that ⌜ _B > C_ ⌝ is true just in case our assumptions ensure the identity of the selected _A_ -world with the selected _B_ -world, for only then does the selected _A_ -world being a _C_ -world ensure that the selected _B_ -world is a _C_ -world as well. Now, if the equivalence constraint is in place, we get the required identity implication in the special case in which all relevant _A_ -worlds are _B_ -worlds and all relevant _B_ -worlds are _A_ -worlds. Then the relevant _A_ -worlds will coincide with the relevant _B_ -worlds by the equivalence constraint. Since these sets are identical, the selected relevant _A_ -world will be the selected relevant _B_ -world by the extensionality of epsilon-terms. However, if some of the relevant _A_ -worlds are _B_ -worlds but some are not, then the selected relevant _A_ -world might be a _B_ -world without all relevant _A_ -worlds being _B_ -worlds. In such a situation, the equivalence constraint will not be applicable and the relevant _A_ -worlds will be distinct from the relevant _B_ -worlds as some relevant _A_ -worlds are not _B_ -worlds. Still, some relevant _B_ -worlds may be _A_ -worlds and so the selected _B_ -world may be an _A_ -world without the selected worlds being identical. But then the substitution principle could fail. 

The counterexamples to the substitution principle which would still occur even if the equivalence constraint on _f_ is in place are somewhat peculiar. They can come about only in situations in which a counterfactual ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ is true without all relevant _A_ -worlds being _B_ -worlds. As explained below, the latter condition will prevent the original counterfactual ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ from being known. Given that the additional counterexamples to the substitution principle would occur only in such specific circumstances, they may be tolerable despite lacking—as far as I can presently see—intuitive support (they cannot be justified in a way similar to Tooley’s counterexample). 

The present defence can be generalized to show that a possible damage caused by the present semantics will always be confined to a limited range of cases. Call a counterfactual _determinately true_ iff all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds (see also the discussion in section ..). Regarding determinate counterfactuals, the epsilonoperator has no choice except selecting a world at which the consequent is true. Now, given that determinate truth is truth according to standard semantics and the logic in standard semantics can be fixed by constraints on the selection function _f_ , we can, on the present account, recover a possible logic holding for some version of standard semantics as a possible 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

logic for determinate truth. This is because the present semantics is partly given in terms of the same kind of selection function _f_ and so we can adopt whichever constraints seem reasonable from the perspective of standard semantics. Determinate truth will then obey whichever logic seems plausible from the perspective of standard semantics. Hence, counterexamples to some logical principles which may not hold on the present semantics but seem plausible from the perspective of standard semantics can, by this strategy, be ensured to occur only in cases in which truth is not determinate. 

If one wishes to avoid these additional counterexamples to the substitution principle, one would need to introduce a new kind of _coordination constraint_ directly concerned with epsilon-terms. One way of doing this is to constrain the choices open to the epsilon-operator in conditions pertaining to the validity of the substitution principle. In order to deal with possible counterexamples to the substitution principle, such a constraint should not be too strong and would ideally go hand in hand with the equivalence constraint for the relevance function _f_ . Here is a suggestion: 

**Modest Equivalence for Choice.** Let _w_ be any world, _A_ and _B_ be sets of worlds. If _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(A))_ ∈ _fw(B)_ and _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(B))_ ∈ _fw(A)_ , then 

**==> picture [147 x 12] intentionally omitted <==**

In words: If the arbitrary relevant _A_ -world is a relevant _B_ -world and the arbitrary relevant _B_ -world is a relevant _A_ -world, then the selected worlds are identical. If we combine this constraint with the generalized equivalence condition, we can show the substitution principle to hold. Suppose ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ and ⌜ _B > A_ ⌝ are true. Given our semantics, this requires that the arbitrarily selected relevant _A_ -world is a _B_ -world ( _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(A))_ ∈ _B_ ) and the arbitrarily selected relevant _B_ -world is an _A_ -world ( _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(B))_ ∈ _A_ ). By the generalized equivalence constraint, we find that the selected _A_ -world is actually a relevant _B_ -world and the selected _B_ -world a relevant _A_ -world by being in _fw(A)_ ∩ _fw(B)_ . The modest equivalence condition on choice now gives us the identity of the selected worlds and so adding the premiss ⌜ _A > C_ ⌝ would suffice for the truth of ⌜ _B > C_ ⌝. 

Adopting only something like the modest equivalence constraint on choice and feeding it in the generalized equivalence condition on relevance allows for a satisfying treatment of the substitution principle. If the 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

substitution principle holds universally, the two constraints secure its validity. If, however, it has counterexamples like the one suggested by Tooley, this can be assumed to be the case because the equivalence condition on relevance fails: sometimes the relevant _A_ -worlds are _B_ -worlds and the relevant _B_ -worlds are _A_ -worlds, but the two sets of relevant worlds are none the less disjoint. In this situation, the antecedent for the modest equivalence condition on choice cannot be satisfied and so the substitution principle will fail. Accordingly, the likely truth of the substitution principle in most but possibly not all situations could be accounted for by the truth of the equivalence condition on relevance in most but not all situations. 

Adding a coordination constraint like the present one to the picture may constitute a certain cost, for it subtracts somewhat from the idea of having a choice operator which selects its referents on a random basis. Once a coordination constraint is in place, certain choices will no longer be independent of each other. For example, selecting a relevant _A_ -world which happens to be a relevant _B_ -world may require selecting this world also as the relevant _B_ -world under certain circumstances. Clearly, the choices would not be determined, but the whole process would perhaps be better seen as the arbitrary selection of a whole structure representing an assignment of admissible choices rather than a number of (independent) individual choices. If one does not like the introduction of a coordination constraint, one could trade it in for a certain kind of additional counterexample to the substitution principle as described above. For the time being, I would like to leave open which line would be best to take. 

Incidentally, if one adopts a coordination constraint in order to deal with the substitution principle, one could secure the validity of modus ponens merely on the basis of weak centering by imposing another coordination constraint. One would require the epsilon-operator to select the world of evaluation if the antecedent is true at it. Weak centering would ensure that the world of evaluation is in such cases part of the relevant worlds. A coordination constraint could then do the work of strong centering by forcing the epsilon-operator to select the world of evaluation out of the set of relevant worlds. This strategy would prevent counterfactual chances from trivializing for true antecedents. If strong centering is adopted, then the counterfactual chances under a true antecedent are always or , for they will result from conditionalizing on a singleton set consisting of the actual world only. Now, I do not consider this to 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

be much of a problem, for counterfactual chances are derived from how we evaluate counterfactuals. For this reason, I think that the seemingly unnatural consequence of them being trivial for true antecedents does not show anything which could be assessed on independent grounds. The situation would be different if we were concerned with objective physical chances, for it is clear that they can be non-trivial even when conditionalized on true antecedents—the objective chance of my team losing the match given that they miss the penalty may be high but not even if, as a matter of fact, they are going to miss the penalty. But given the difference between the two conceptions of chance, I think adopting strong centering is not necessarily a problem, but if desired, one could avoid it with the help of a coordination constraint. 

## _..The Metaphysics of Arbitrariness_ 

There remains an important issue which I have sidestepped so far: what metaphysical assumptions underlie the use of epsilon-terms in the present semantics? Answering this question has important implications for the metaphysics of counterfactuals. I will only give a brief sketch of some of the available options. To address the issue properly, we would need to venture into the debate about arbitrary reference. In principle, any account of arbitrary reference may be adopted and applied to the present semantics. 

According to our semantics, there is a selection function which selects for every non-empty set of worlds one of its members. An epsilon-term ⌜ _ϵxφ(x)_ ⌝ is taken to denote the object which the selection function selects from the set of objects satisfying ⌜ _φ(x)_ ⌝. This is enough to explain how epsilon-terms function semantically and, in particular, in which logical relations they stand to other sentences. However, adopting this semantics presupposes that there is a selection function which provides epsilon-terms with referents. What is the metaphysical status of this assumption? 

According to a _realist stance_ , there simply is such a selection function which supplies epsilon-terms with referents.[] In its application to counterfactuals, the realist stance corresponds to the assumption that there are 

>  A realist theory of arbitrary reference is defended in Breckenridge and Magidor (). An earlier presentation of this paper at the Ockham Society in Oxford was an important source of inspiration for the present account. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

brute counterfactual facts. An _anti-realist_ may object that there cannot be a distinguished selection function, for one selection function is as good as any other. For this reason, epsilon-terms could not be assumed to have determinate referents reflecting brute counterfactual facts. Perhaps it is merely _as if_ they denote a certain object in an arbitrary way. Other anti-realist stories may be possible as well. 

On a realist conception, epsilon-terms are taken at face value: they are singular terms denoting a particular object, the fact that the descriptive material of an epsilon-term underdetermines this object notwithstanding. Epsilon-terms would pick out their referents in a truly arbitrary way. Of course, the way they pick out their referents would not be completely arbitrary, for the descriptive material partially constrains which objects they might select (an arbitrary _F_ is always an _F_ ). 

The main challenge for the realist is the question of how epsilon-terms are supposed to get their referents. Since epsilon-terms are construed as picking out their referents in an arbitrary way, a realist will not want to specify an additional condition which—together with the predicative component— _determines_ a referent. To some extent, a realist will have to assume that the semantic gods sometimes gamble: the linguistic meaning of an epsilon-term alone does not determine a referent and so the assignment of a referent is to some extent an arbitrary affair. 

To guard against a possible misunderstanding, note that the realist conception of epsilon-terms does not imply that epsilon-terms have different referents on different occasions of use. The choice of the selection function which is the semantic value of the epsilon-operator is made once and for all. If one wishes, one could relax this assumption, but in the context of counterfactuals, this only seems to create problems, for then two utterances of the same counterfactual could be such that one is true while the other is false even though the counterfactual does not contain any context-sensitive elements. In principle, there is also room for a middle position according to which the referent of an epsilon-term is only held fixed over the course of a particular conversation.[] But for present purposes, I assume that the referents of epsilon-terms stay constant. 

On the realist conception, sentences containing the epsilon-operator are generally either true or false. In its application to counterfactuals, 

>  The latter option might square well with an implementation of the present semantics into discourse representation theory. 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

we will find, however, that the truth of certain counterfactuals is sometimes not grounded in particular matters of fact together with the laws of nature. Particular matters of fact and the laws of nature will in general only determine the set of relevant antecedent-worlds. However, if not all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds but the arbitrarily selected world is a consequent-world, then the counterfactual is true not only because of what has happened at the actual world and what its laws are but also because of the arbitrary choice of the antecedent-world. So, there will be some arbitrary counterfactual facts which do not supervene on particular matters of facts together with the laws of nature.[] 

How high this metaphysical cost is taken to be depends on one’s general metaphysical outlook. If one subscribes to anything like Humean supervenience, realism about epsilon-terms is untenable. But if one allows the ultimate supervenience base to be fairly large, one may be more inclined to include counterfactual facts in them as well. For instance, one may already hold that objective chances do not supervene on physical facts: it may seem possible that two worlds unfold in just the same way but are in fact governed by different objective chance distributions. One world may unfold in a way which is quite probable from the perspective of its objective chance distribution, while the other world may unfold in a way which is improbable in the light of its objective chance distribution. If basic facts concerning objective chances are granted, basic counterfactual facts might seem less implausible. 

As an alternative to the realist interpretation, one might endorse an _anti-realist_ conception of epsilon-terms. If the predicative component of an epsilon-term is satisfied by multiple objects, the epsilon-term would not be taken to denote one of these objects. Still, epsilon-terms could be taken to purport to denote an object which has been selected in such a way, but they would not be construed as being capable of really doing so. On one way of understanding such an anti-realist conception, epsilonterms would be used _as if_ the realist construal of them were correct. 

>  There are some suggestions in the literature which go some way towards the realist interpretation of the present theory. At the end of his paper, Hawthorne () makes a brief conjecture that the problems surrounding counterfactuals and chance are perhaps best addressed by postulating that there is always a most relevant antecedent-world. McDermott () offers a somewhat related proposal: although he does not defend a Stalnaker semantics, he takes certain counterfactuals to introduce a realm of unknowable facts. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

The main challenge for an anti-realist conception of epsilon-terms is to give an indication of how epsilon-terms function semantically if they do not denote. I anticipate that there will be more than one option, but here I will sketch only one possibility which employs the familiar technique of supervaluations.[] Application of it will relate the present semantics of counterfactuals to Stalnaker’s supervaluationist theory of conditionals. 

Originally, the technique of supervaluations was developed by van Fraassen () as a tool to treat empty names. Hence, it is a natural question whether epsilon-terms can be accounted for by supervaluationist methods, for an anti-realist takes epsilon-terms, strictly speaking, to be generally empty, the only exception being the case in which the extension of the predicative component contains exactly a single object. On a supervaluationist account, sentences are taken to be either _determinately true_ , _determinately false_ , or _indeterminate_ . It is not hard to see how a natural application of a supervaluationist approach to epsilon-terms might look. The idea would be that a sentence containing an epsilon-term is determinately true iff it is true no matter which object we select out of the extension of the predicative component of the epsilon-term. Likewise, it is determinately false iff we cannot find an object in the extension of the predicative component which would make the sentence true. Otherwise, the sentence would be indeterminate in truth-value. 

If we combine the supervaluationist account of epsilon-terms with the proposal for counterfactuals in terms of epsilon-terms, we will get the following picture. Counterfactuals will either be determinately true, determinately false, or indeterminate. They will be determinately true iff all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. So, the standard truth conditions would turn out to be the conditions under which a counterfactual is determinately true. However, a counterfactual will only be determinately false if no relevant antecedent-world is a consequentworld. If some relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds and some are not, the counterfactual will be said to be indeterminate. In a typical situation, a counterfactual like ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads’ would be indeterminate. Such an account seems to be a little more complex by involving epsilon-terms but otherwise it is 

>  Alternatively, one could give a fictionalist story about epsilon-terms according to which they are used by pretending that they select objects on a random basis. 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

very similar to Stalnaker’s () supervaluationist theory of conditionals (cf. also Lewis : ). 

What happens to the explanation of our credences in counterfactuals if a supervaluationist account of epsilon-terms is adopted?[] The explanation that has been given is based on the idea that evaluating counterfactuals can be understood as thinking about arbitrary antecedent-worlds. To me, it is at least not clear whether this explanation presupposes the realist construal of epsilon-terms, for the question of what to think about an arbitrarily chosen antecedent-world seems to be well posed independently of how we account for epsilon-terms. The worry could be that if epsilon-terms turn out to be non-denoting and are accounted for on supervaluationist lines, one will need to say something about how credences interact with possibly indeterminate sentences. One option is to say that our credences estimate whether an arbitrary supervaluation verifies the sentence in question. In a similar spirit, one may hold that credences in sentences which are given a supervaluationist semantics estimate the fraction of supervaluations which verify the relevant sentence among all admissible supervaluations, possibly weighted by a certain probability distribution.[] Applied to counterfactuals, we would then find that our credences estimate (a) whether an arbitrary supervaluation picking out a relevant antecedentworld is a consequent world or (b) what the fraction is of supervaluations verifying the counterfactual among all supervaluations. In both cases, the right kind of credence seems to be predicted. 

Supervaluationism allows us to underpin an anti-realist understanding of epsilon-terms. Such a story is not without costs: we would have to live with the fact that the semantics of counterfactuals is given within a metalanguage (the language containing the epsilon-operator) for which we can offer only a non-classical (supervaluational) meta-semantics. Thus, the resulting theory of counterfactuals must ultimately be said to be nonclassical: some counterfactual would neither be determinately true nor determinately false. For this reason, I would prefer the realist story if it does not turn out to be untenable (and I will continue to write as if the realist story were correct). 

-  Thanks to Tobias Rosefeldt for pressing me on this. 

>  For further discussion, see Williams (: –). 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

## _..Comparisons_ 

Structurally, and independently of whether we adopt a realist or an antirealist attitude towards epsilon-terms, the present account is a kind of Stalnaker semantics. Whether or not a counterfactual is true depends on whether the consequent is true at a single world. As mentioned before, one difference is that Stalnaker’s original theory is more naturally described with a definite description operator: the truth conditions of counterfactuals are given by what is true at _the most relevant antecedentworld_ . In contrast, it is an integral part of the present theory that there are usually many relevant antecedent-worlds for the epsilon-operator to select. But we could reinterpret Stalnaker’s theory by identifying the most relevant antecedent-world with the arbitrarily selected world. Would such a theory be different from the present model? As far as the modal profile of the proposition expressed by counterfactuals goes, the answer is quite clearly ‘No’: both theories suggest that a counterfactual ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ is true at a set of worlds of the form { _w_ : _sw(A)_ |= _B_ }, where the function _s_ selects the relevant antecedent-world. However, the present theory contains a further component which equips counterfactuals with a specific epistemic profile provided by the specification of their truth conditions in terms of the epsilon-operator. As we shall see, the explanation of credences in counterfactuals relies on the relevant antecedent-world being arbitrarily selected from a larger set of relevant antecedent-worlds. 

The general idea of accounting for the connection between conditionals and conditional probabilities in terms of arbitrary selection has at least one prominent predecessor. Van Fraassen () (see also Bacon ) obtained a limited tenability result for the thesis that probabilities of conditionals are conditional probabilities by considering possible worlds models in which the order of worlds is such that the closest antecedentworlds are positioned as if they were arbitrarily selected from the set of relevant antecedent-worlds. This result is achieved by extending a standard possible worlds model so that every world in the new model is an infinite series of worlds in the original model and then adjusting the order of these worlds in such a way that the closest antecedent-world can be seen as representing an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world.[] Less similar, 

>  As van Fraassen was concerned with a different thesis applying to indicative conditionals, we would still need to see whether his result can be adapted to the evaluation of counterfactuals. 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

but still close in spirit is the random variable approach pursued by Jeffrey () and further explored by Jeffrey and Stalnaker () (see also Kaufmann ). The observation is that a conditional probability can be seen as the expected value of a random variable which assumes intermediate values matching the relevant conditional probability at worlds at which the antecedent is false.[] Given that credences in classical propositions can be seen as the expected value of a random variable which only takes the values and depending on whether the proposition is true or false at the world in question, the random variable approach can be seen as an extension of the classical framework of propositions. However, conditionals would no longer be bivalent. They could have all values in the interval [, ]. An advantage of the present account is that it is more conservative by allowing conditionals to behave like ordinary propositions. 

## .The Evaluation of Counterfactuals 

A major task for the present semantics is to explain the puzzling credences in counterfactuals reflected in the evaluation constraint developed in chapter . The core idea is to explain our credences in counterfactuals by the specific epistemic profile of epsilon-terms: the more antecedentworlds are consequent-worlds, the more likely should we take it to be true that an arbitrary antecedent-world is a consequent-world. If successful, this would show that the evaluation constraint can, despite appearances to the contrary, be explained on the basis of certain kinds of truth conditions for counterfactuals. In the discussion to follow, I will set two special cases aside: counterfactuals with a true antecedent (see section .) and counterfactuals with an impossible antecedent. 

Sentences containing epsilon-terms have a specific epistemic profile. Consider sentences of the form ⌜ _G(ϵxF(x))_ ⌝ saying that an arbitrary _F_ is _G_ . What are their subjective probabilities? It seems that our subjective probabilities concerning such sentences should be sensitive to what we take to be the distribution of _G_ s among the _F_ s, provided we assume that 

>  The random variable approach targets, like van Fraassen, only Adams’s thesis. But it seems to have a natural extension to counterfactuals: where the evaluation of indicative conditionals might consist in estimating a random variable corresponding to our present subjective probabilities, counterfactuals might be evaluated by estimating a random variable corresponding to counterfactual chances. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

there is at least one _F_ . The more _F_ s we take to be _G_ s, the more likely should we take an arbitrary _F_ to be _G_ . If we take few _F_ s to be _G_ , we should think it quite unlikely that an arbitrary _F_ is _G_ . Similarly, if we take most _F_ s to be _G_ s, we should think it quite likely that an arbitrary _F_ is _G_ . In sum, how likely we should take an arbitrary _F_ to be _G_ seems to be coordinated by an estimate of the fraction of _G_ s among the _F_ s. Thus, epsilon-terms induce a certain structure on the subjective probabilities we should assign to sentences containing them: our subjective probabilities should be distributed uniformly over the different candidates which the epsilon-operator might select. 

The relevant sentence in the specification of arbitrary truth conditions for counterfactuals is of the form ⌜ _G(ϵxF(x))_ ⌝. It reads: _ϵw_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(_ � _φ_ � _))_ |= _ψ_ , saying that an arbitrary relevant antecedentworld is a consequent-world. Following the above analysis of such epsilon-sentences, how likely we should take an arbitrary relevant antecedent-world to be a consequence-world is coordinated by an estimate of the fraction of consequent-worlds among the relevant antecedent-worlds. The more relevant antecedent-worlds we take to be consequent-worlds, the more likely should we take an arbitrary antecedent-world to be a consequent-world. 

To see the advocated explanation at work, let us quickly go back to some of our original examples: 

- () If a given coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads, 

- () If I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost, 

- () If McCain had won, Obama would have lost. 

We found that we do not rule out the first counterfactual outright. Since some of the relevant toss-worlds are heads-worlds, we should not rule out that an arbitrary toss-world is a heads-world. That we take the second counterfactual to be probable is because we should have a high credence that an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world is a consequentworld given that most antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. Finally, the last counterfactual is such that all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. In this case, we can be certain that an arbitrary antecedent-world is a consequent-world. 

It seems, then, that the semantics in terms of the epsilon-operator contains the right ingredients to account for the subjective probabilities 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

of counterfactuals. It may not have gone unnoticed, though, that the explanation of our credences in counterfactuals that I have given presupposes that counterfactual chances are uniformly distributed over the relevant antecedent-worlds. What happens when this assumption is relaxed? A second, perhaps less immediate question concerns the formal details of the interaction between probability and counterfactuals. If we add a subjective probability function to the picture, can we show that it accords with the evaluation constraint? The next two subsections focus on these questions. 

## _..Uniformity Reconsidered_ 

On the present analysis, the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals estimate whether an antecedent-world which is chosen according to a uniform probability distribution is a consequent-world. This is because epsilon-terms have been described as selecting their referents in a completely unbiased fashion, not favouring any candidate world over another one. Thus, every relevant antecedent-world is given the same weight. This way of evaluating counterfactuals is in accordance with the evaluation constraint if and only if the counterfactual chances are taken to be uniform, that is if the uniformity constraint is adopted for counterfactual chances. To recall, the evaluation constraint requires that if the counterfactual chances are taken to be so-and-so, our credence in the corresponding counterfactual should be so-and-so. If and only if counterfactual chances are uniform, the counterfactual chances of the consequent under the antecedent equal the chances that an antecedentworld which is selected according to a uniform probability distribution is a consequent-world. For this reason, we will need to focus on the uniformity assumption in some more detail. 

The uniformity assumption appears attractive partly because of its simplicity and partly because it provides a direct answer to the question of what the structure of counterfactual chances is. However, there are certain examples which challenge the uniformity assumption. Think, for instance, of a trick coin whose weight distribution is unbalanced so that it comes up heads with a probability of  _/_ . Now, with respect to such a coin, consider the following counterfactual: 

() If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

Assuming that the coin was not tossed and everything else is normal, it seems that our credence in this counterfactual should be about  _/_ . It is not clear whether this credence can be explained if the uniformity assumption is adopted, for it seems that the distribution of heads-worlds among the relevant antecedent-worlds is only about %. But on this assumption, we would then have to assign a credence of .to the counterfactual which does not seem right. 

Why would it be plausible to assume that the distribution of headsworlds among the relevant antecedent-worlds is still about % despite the fact that the coin is not fair? The thought would be that the unbalanced weight distribution of the coin does not introduce three different possibilities two of which are such that the coin lands heads. This would result in a probability of  _/_ based on the uniformity assumption. Rather, it seems that there remain just two possibilities, namely one in which the coin lands heads and one in which it lands tails. Thus, the unusual weight distribution would not make a difference to the number of possibilities but only to the likelihood of these possibilities. One could, of course, try to resist this line of thought, perhaps by holding that worlds can still be different even if the same things happen in them. If so, then the account can stay as it is. But it would be nice not to be committed to this assumption, which is why we are going to seek for a way of doing without it. 

The epsilon-operator has been construed as an operator which arbitrarily selects an object from the domain of the predicate it is attached to. A selection which is completely arbitrary seems to be a selection according to a uniform probability distribution.[] So, we may see the epsilon-operator as implicitly indexed with a uniform probability function _P_ . When the epsilon-operator is attached to a predicate, it represents an object which is selected from the extension _E_ of the predicate according to the probability distribution _P(_ •| _E)_ , that is with the probability _P_ restricted to the extension of the predicate. So, if the total domain consists of objects and there are four objects in the domain of the predicate, then attaching the epsilon-operator to the predicate denotes an object which has been selected with probability  _/_ (and not  _/_ ). 

>  As indicated earlier, there are certain domains over which there is no uniform distribution. In such cases, it is not clear what to say about the structure of the epistemic profile induced by the epsilon-operator, for a non-uniform distribution does not seem right either. I have to leave this problem open. 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

Once we describe the epsilon-operator this way, it is only a small step to recognize it as a special case of a more general conception. There is no need to start with a uniform distribution _P_ . Instead, we could allow for probability distributions which assign different weights to the objects in the total domain. Then applying the epsilon-operator to a predicate would represent an object in the domain of the predicate which has been selected by a weighted choice process. The epsilon-operator as construed so far would be a special case when _P_ is a uniform distribution. An epsilonterm such as ⌜ _ϵPxF(x)_ ⌝ would represent an object in the extension _E_ of _F_ which has been selected from _E_ according to the probability distribution _P(_ •| _E)_ . It does not seem to be much of an additional cost to accept the use of epsilon-terms which represent weighted choices. 

Let us now apply the present idea to the case which is our main concern. The relevant domain for the epsilon-operator is the underlying set of possible worlds. The evaluation constraint makes use of a probability distribution Ch over this set of possible worlds. The counterfactual chance of _B_ under _A_ is then the probability Ch _[A] w[(][B][)]_[, that is the] chance of _B_ conditionalized on the set of relevant _A_ -worlds. This chance distribution comes from Ch by conditionalizing on the set of relevant antecedent worlds, i.e. it is defined as Ch _(B_ | _fw(A))_ . We may now consider epsilon-operators indexed with such a chance distribution. They will represent antecedent-worlds selected according to the true counterfactual chance distribution of the world _w_ . For instance, an epsilon-term like ⌜ _ϵ_ **Ch** _w_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _fw(A)))_ ⌝ will represent a world selected from the set _fw(A)_ with probability Ch _(_ •| _fw(A))_ . Based on this, we can extend the semantics for counterfactuals in the following way: 

**Arbitrary Truth Conditions Extended.** Let _w_ be any world and ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ a counterfactual. Then _w_ |= ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ iff 

**==> picture [191 x 12] intentionally omitted <==**

Thus, in the most interesting cases, a counterfactual is true at a world of evaluation _w_ if the consequent is true at a relevant antecedent-world selected according to the counterfactual chance distribution of _w_ . It is clear that this resolves the present issue. On the assumption that the counterfactual chances of _B_ under _A_ is _x_ , we should think it _x_ -likely that the selected antecedent-world is a consequent-world, for the antecedentworld is represented as being selected according to the counterfactual 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

chances under _A_ . Thus, by building the counterfactual chance distribution into the epsilon-operator, the present semantics can now explain the evaluation constraint even in cases in which the counterfactual chance distribution is not uniform. 

## _..Modelling the Epistemic Space_ 

Although our semantics is formal, the explanation of how subjective probabilities attach to counterfactuals has been somewhat informal. We have derived the epistemic profile of counterfactuals from the epistemic profile of epsilon-sentences. Epsilon-terms present their referents under a veil of ignorance: we can know only with certain probabilities to which object they refer. This feature is key for using them to explain our uncertainty about counterfactuals. Can it be formally modelled? Can we give, for instance, a toy model in which the probabilities of counterfactuals can be seen as the probabilities of certain sets of worlds at which the epsilonsentences are true? This task proves to be much more difficult than it may initially seem. In this section, I explain what difficulties one is confronted with and briefly indicate how one may try to find one’s way around them. 

To keep things simple, let us focus on a particular case. Suppose our space of metaphysical possibilities consists of a hundred worlds, _w_ , _. . ._ , _w_ . Assume further that we have already ruled out all worlds but the first nine, _w_ , _. . ._ , _w_ . These comprise the domain of our epistemic possibilities over which our credences are defined. For simplicity, we may assume that our credence function _Cr_ is uniform, giving each world the same weight. Further, let us choose a coin-toss counterfactual as our main example. We may assume that there is no uncertainty over the relevant toss-worlds. So, to all of the nine worlds _wi_ the selection function _f_ assigns the same set of relevant antecedent-worlds. Assume that these are the ten worlds _w_ , _. . ._ , _w_ , of which the first five are heads-worlds and the last five are tails-worlds. Then our evaluation constraint would predict a credence of  _/_ in the counterfactual ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads’ (⌜ _A > B_ ⌝). If we now apply our semantics and look at the worlds at which the counterfactual comes out true, i.e. at the set { _w_ : _w_ |= ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝}, can we show that it is assigned credence  _/_ by the function _Cr_ ? The answer is straightforwardly negative. There are two ways of seeing this. Let me explain them in turn. 

Given that we have assumed that there is no uncertainty about which worlds are relevant—for all worlds _w_ , _. . ._ , _w_  it is the set 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

_M_ := { _w_ , _. . ._ , _w_ }—the epsilon-operator will select the same arbitrary world out of _M_ relative to all our epistemic possibilities. Assume it selects _w_ . This is a heads world and so the counterfactual would be true. But it would then be true at all our epistemic possibilities, for there is no uncertainty concerning the set of relevant worlds, and hence our credence would turn out to be . Similarly, if a tails-worlds had been selected, say _w_ , the counterfactual would have been false, but it would have been false at all epistemic possibilities, and so a credence of would have been predicted. No matter which world the epsilon-operator selects, the resulting proposition is one we are not uncertain about. 

There is a more general way of seeing the problem. Simply note that no proposition is assigned credence  _/_ by our function _Cr_ . As the probability is uniformly distributed over nine worlds, any proposition is assigned a credence of _n/_ , with _n_ ranging from to . But because is not divisible by , for no such _n_ we have _n/_  =  _/_ . So, no matter which proposition is expressed by the counterfactual, it is not going to receive credence  _/_ .[] 

What has gone wrong? I will suggest that the problem comes from equating the modal profile of a sentence with its epistemic profile. It is clear that the two can sometimes come apart. Take the sentence ‘Hesperus is Phosphorus’. This sentence is metaphysically necessary and so its modal profile can be adequately represented by the set of all possible worlds. However, the sentence is not epistemically necessary—it is not irrational to assign to it a credence less than under certain circumstances. Hence, its epistemic profile should not be represented by the set of all possible worlds. Conversely, consider a sentence like ‘There is a thinker’. It may be epistemically necessary but it is clearly not metaphysically necessary. Moreover, the guise under which a modal profile is presented can make for epistemic differences. The sentence ‘Hesperus is Hesperus’ has a different epistemic profile than ‘Hesperus is Phosphorus’, although the two sentences have the same modal profile (they are both necessary). With this in mind, it is an immediate concern that something similar might happen when epsilon-terms are involved. After all, they have quite a special sense: they present their referents by only partially describing them and leaving what is left open to chance. We may therefore suspect that 

>  Effectively, we have illustrated that a triviality result for indicative conditionals based on finite domains and proven in Hájek and Hall (: ) extends to counterfactuals on the present evaluation constraint. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

our model above was too coarse-grained to capture the epistemic profile of epsilon-sentences. 

What could a better model look like? Take world _w_ , which is an epistemic possibility, and assume that as a matter of fact, the counterfactual is true at _w_ , i.e. a heads-world has been selected relative to _w_ . Can we therefore, from the perspective of _w_ , be certain that the counterfactual is true? Not really, it seems, for there is no way of knowing this. From the perspective of _w_ , the counterfactual might be true but it might equally well be false. The reason is that the epsilon-term does not reveal whether a heads-world or a tails-world has been selected. So, from the perspective of _w_ , there are really two epistemic possibilities: one in which a heads-world gets selected and one in which a tails-world gets selected. If we want to model the epistemic space accordingly, we should pair the metaphysical possibility _w_  with two epistemically possible worlds _w_ [][and] _[ w]_ [][. The same] should be done to our credence. If _w_  deserves a credence of  _/_ , then this bit of probability should be redistributed over _w_ [][and] _[ w]_ [][according] to how likely they are from the perspective of _w_ . In our sample case, they are both equally likely because the counterfactual chance of a headsworld and a tails-world are each  _/_ . Consequently, the two epistemic worlds should each be assigned probability  _/_  ·  _/_  =  _/_ . If we do this for all our original nine metaphysical worlds, we arrive at an epistemic space consisting of the worlds _w_ [][,] _[ w]_ [][,] _[ . . .]_[ ,] _[ w]_ [][,] _[ w]_ [][.][In][exactly][half][of] them is the counterfactual true and given that the probability distribution remains uniform, our counterfactual will receive a credence of  _/_ as desired. 

We could go on and push the construction further. The first thing to note is perhaps that in order to account for all possible consequents a counterfactual with the antecedent ‘if the coin had been tossed’ might have, there are more than two epistemic possibilities emerging from the perspective of _w_ . To any world in _M_ —the set of relevant antecedentworlds relative to _w_ —there is the possibility that this world gets selected. So, more realistically, we should represent the epistemic possibilities relative to _w_  by ten worlds _w_ [][,] _[ . . .]_[ ,] _[ w]_ [][corresponding to the ten worlds] in _M_ . This will not alter the probability of the coin-toss counterfactual, for in half of them the counterfactual will be true, but it will now also allow us to calculate the subjective probability of other counterfactuals with the same antecedent. The next step would be to incorporate counterfactuals with a different antecedent. Here we could simply repeat the procedure on 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

the already constructed space. So, step by step, we would split our starting worlds up into more and more epistemic possibilities corresponding to the possible selections of the epsilon-operator. Each time a world gets split up, its probability is redistributed on the newly emerging worlds with the weights specified by the counterfactual chances. If the counterfactual chances are uniform as in our example, each new world gets the same share, but if necessary, we can redistribute the probability with the weights assigned by the counterfactual chances. Ultimately, then, we arrive at an epistemic space which represents all the epistemic possibilities introduced by (non-nested) counterfactuals. (In much more generality, constructions of this kind have been studied by van Fraassen () and more recently by Bacon () in the context of satisfying a version of Adams’s thesis for indicative conditionals. I have to leave it as an open question how the techniques they develop can be adapted to the present case.[] ) 

In conclusion, an expansion of the domain of possibilities seems to be what is required for constructing an adequate epistemic space in which to represent the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals. In a way, this reflects the idea that counterfactuals, and conditionals more generally, allow us to draw new distinctions we could otherwise not make (cf. Stalnaker ). We will come back to this issue in chapter , for it is closely related to possible triviality results for counterfactuals. 

## .Knowability and Assertability 

The present semantics for counterfactuals predicts a certain limitation of our epistemic access to counterfactuals. As we have seen, we can only know an arbitrary _F_ to be _G_ if all _F_ s are _G_ s. If only some _F_ s are _G_ , we cannot know whether an arbitrarily selected _F_ is _G_ , for we cannot know which object has been selected. By the specification of truth conditions in terms of the epsilon-operator, it follows then that we can— special cases aside—only know that a counterfactual is true if all relevant 

>  I should also admit that I have described the construction without any possible coordination constraints in mind. It should be noted that neither van Fraassen nor Bacon attempt to satisfy the substitution principle (Bacon argues explicitly against it), so it is not unlikely that if coordination constraints are imposed, further complexities will arise. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. We may therefore conjecture the following constraint to hold:
