**Unknown Counterfactuals.** If not for all _w_[′] in _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ : _w_[′] |= _ψ_ , then the counterfactual _φ > ψ_ is in _w_ not known to be true. The phrase ‘is not known’ is to be understood as ‘is not known by anyone at any time’. Note that this does not generate a modal claim: the constraint merely states that certain counterfactuals are not known in certain worlds. So, these counterfactuals need not be unknowable in the strong sense of it being metaphysically impossible to know them. We will return to this issue shortly. 

An important point is not captured by the present constraint. To see this, note that counterfactuals would satisfy the constraint even if they had the standard truth conditions. However, this would be so because the counterfactual would be false under the specified conditions and for this reason unknown. The situation is interestingly different with respect to the truth conditions in terms of the epsilon-operator. A counterfactual may be true at a world _w_ because the arbitrary antecedent-world is a consequent-world despite the fact that not all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. In such a case, the counterfactual will be true but unknown. 

Structurally, we can identify this epistemic feature by noticing that under certain circumstances neither the counterfactual nor its negation can be known. Since either the counterfactual or its negation is true, this gives us cases in which true counterfactuals are not known. Let us call a sentence _undecided_ iff neither it nor its negation is known to be true. Then we would have the following principle: 

**Undecided Counterfactuals.** If neither for all _w_[′] in _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ : _w_[′] |= _ψ_ nor for all _w_[′] in _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ : _w_[′] |= ¬ _ψ_ , then the counterfactual ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ is undecided in _w_ . 

In words: if neither all antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds nor all antecedent-worlds are non-consequent-worlds, then a counterfactual is undecided. This is a direct consequence of our semantics and the constraint on counterfactual knowledge. If the first condition holds, then the counterfactual ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ is not known and if the second condition holds, then the counterfactual ⌜ _φ >_ ¬ _ψ_ ⌝ is not known. But on the present semantics, ⌜¬ _(φ > ψ)_ ⌝ logically implies ⌜ _φ >_ ¬ _ψ_ ⌝: a negation of a 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

counterfactual can only be true if the antecedent is not impossible and the arbitrary antecedent-world is not a consequent-world which makes it a non-consequent-world (this is related to the fact that conditional excluded middle holds on the present semantics; see section .for discussion). Given this, it is clear that we cannot know in the specified situation that ⌜¬ _(φ > ψ)_ ⌝ is true, for otherwise we could infer ⌜ _φ >_ ¬ _ψ_ ⌝ which we cannot know. So, neither ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ nor ⌜¬ _(φ > ψ)_ ⌝ will be known. But, of course, one of the two sentences will be true, although we cannot know which. This, then, captures the main epistemic consequence of the present semantics: there are true counterfactuals which are not known for semantic reasons.[] 

Such a limitation of our epistemic access to counterfactuals squares well with the amount of counterfactual knowledge we are willing to ascribe to ourselves. A paradigm example of a counterfactual which, in typical circumstances, turns out to be undecided is a counterfactual like ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads’. We do not take ourselves to know that this counterfactual is true. More importantly, we do not take ourselves to know that this counterfactual is false either as one might expect on the standard account. Rather, we are happy to say that we do not know _whether_ the coin would have landed heads if it had been tossed.[] This intuition can also be expressed by saying that there is no way of telling how the coin would have landed. Similarly in the other cases we have discussed. 

>  The present principles are not without exceptions if they are taken to apply to counterfactuals which contain the epsilon-operator. A counterfactual of the form ‘If _φ_ had been true, then the world would have been exactly like the arbitrarily selected relevant _φ_ -world’ can be known without all relevant antecedent-worlds verifying the consequent. Such counterexamples come about because the consequent of the counterfactual states something which can be known a priori to be satisfied by the arbitrary antecedent-world. Thanks to Timothy Williamson for pointing this out to me. 

>  I take this to be an intuitive datum of moderate strength. It is clear that proponents of the standard account or error theorists like Hájek () will disagree, claiming that we _can_ know that such counterfactuals are false. To defend their position, they will need an explanation of why we are pre-theoretically more inclined to take an agnostic stance. Others may explain our tendency to take an agnostic stance not by positing limited epistemic access, but by holding that there is no matter of fact which determines the counterfactuals’ truthvalue (they would be metaphysically indeterminate). My claim here is only that general lack of knowledge in such cases provides a good explanation, not that it is the only possible explanation. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

Although the present account implies a certain kind of scepticism concerning certain counterfactuals, it should be noted that there is no obstacle from the present point of view that many counterfactuals can be known and are known to be true. In whichever situation the standard semantics predicts that a counterfactual is true because all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds, the present account is compatible with the counterfactual being known. Since there are still many cases in which the standard truth conditions are satisfied, there are still many cases in which a counterfactual may be known.[] To some extent, the standard truth conditions turn out to approximate the knowability conditions of counterfactuals. 

Finally, let us briefly return to an issue mentioned earlier. As they stand, the principles concerning the epistemic access to counterfactual facts do not have any modal force. They only state that certain counterfactuals are not known under certain circumstances. Does the reasoning behind these principles suggest anything stronger? A stronger claim would be that the counterfactuals under consideration cannot be known in the strong sense of it being metaphysically impossible that they are known. But such a stronger claim is not warranted. The present semantics does not imply that there are true counterfactuals which are strictly unknowable. The reason why this is so is simple: nothing in the present semantics suggests that if for a world _w_ neither all worlds in _fw(A)_ are _ψ_ -worlds nor all worlds in _fw(A)_ are ¬ _ψ_ -worlds, then there is no world _w_[′] such that either all worlds in _fw_ ′ _(A)_ are _ψ_ -worlds or all worlds in _fw_ ′ _(A)_ are ¬ _ψ_ -worlds. But if there is such a world _w_[′] , then it is compatible with the present semantics that the corresponding counterfactual is known in _w_[′] despite the fact that it is not known in _w_ . An example might help to illustrate this. Suppose in the actual world a given lottery is fair. Then the semantics suggests that the counterfactual ‘If I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost’ is unknown. This is, of course, compatible with there being a possible world in which the lottery is rigged such that all tickets are determined to lose. In such a world, I may know the counterfactual to be true by knowing of the manipulation. So, the present account does not predict that the counterfactuals which are not known for semantic reasons are also strictly unknowable. 

>  Exactly how many counterfactuals are knowable will depend on how broad or narrow one’s concept of relevance is. If typically a larger class of antecedent-words is relevant, fewer counterfactuals will be knowable, and _vice versa_ . Compare the discussion in section .. 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

Let me make a few remarks on assertions of counterfactuals. Of course, what to say will to a large extent depend on a general outlook on assertion. To illustrate the situation with a particular example, I will here follow Williamson (, : ch. ) in assuming that the norm of assertion is knowledge: one should assert something only if one knows it. Given the constraint on counterfactual knowledge, we can derive a constraint on the assertability conditions of counterfactuals: 

**Assertability of Counterfactuals** . If not for all _w_[′] in _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ : _w_[′] |= _ψ_ , then the counterfactual ⌜ _φ > ψ_ ⌝ is not assertable in _w_ . 

A counterfactual can only be known if all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds, and a counterfactual should only be asserted if it is known to be true. Thus, the constraint follows from the knowability conditions of counterfactuals together with the general assumption that only what is known should be asserted. 

Now, it seems to be a datum that in typical situations one should neither assert outright a counterfactual like ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads’ nor ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed tails’. But on the present account, one of the two counterfactuals will be true. Initially, the fact that neither of the two counterfactuals is assertable may be levelled as an objection against the present theory, for it may be taken to suggest that neither of the two counterfactuals is true. By the principle above, however, the present datum can be explained in a straightforward way: the two counterfactuals should not be asserted because they are not known to be true.[] Besides, it can also be explained why there is the feeling that the non-assertability of these counterfactuals is somewhat more robust than in other cases in which a sentence happens to be non-assertable in a given situation. This would be due to the fact that in typical situations our epistemic access to such counterfactuals is limited for principled reasons: if neither all antecedent-worlds are consequentworlds nor all antecedent-worlds are non-consequent-worlds, there is nothing we can do to find out whether such a counterfactual is true. 

>  A further question concerns what one should say about the assertability of counterfactuals which have a ‘high chance’ consequent instead of one with middling chances. If one takes the former to be assertable, one will probably take issue with the knowledge rule for assertion, at least this is what one would have to do on the present semantics for counterfactuals. 

counterfactuals and arbitrariness  

## .Revisiting the Puzzle 

The basic argument against truth conditions starts out with a puzzle concerning the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals. High subjective probability of a counterfactual seems to be compatible with low subjective probability that the standard truth conditions obtain, while knowledge of a counterfactual seems to put us in a position to know that the standard truth conditions obtain. We have already seen that the present semantics underwrites these two features concerning the epistemic profile of counterfactuals by showing that it gives rise to the evaluation constraint. 

As already pointed out, the truth conditions in terms of the epsilonoperator are weaker than the standard truth conditions: if the standard truth conditions obtain, the present truth conditions will obtain as well but not always _vice versa_ . So, from the perspective of the present semantics, there is no need to question the part of the puzzle which suggests that if counterfactuals have truth conditions, they must be weaker than the standard truth conditions. Instead, what one will need to cast doubt on is the claim that if counterfactuals have truth conditions, they must be at least as strong as the standard truth conditions. 

As we have seen, this claim is based on the principle that if a proposition _p_ does not imply a proposition _q_ , then it is possible to know that _p_ without being in a position to know that _q_ . We also saw that this principle cannot hold in full generality. Not only is it falsified by unknowable truths, it is also called into question by propositions which can only be known to be true by knowing a slightly stronger proposition. 

Now, on the present account, the proposition expressed by a counterfactual relates to the proposition it is taken to express according to the standard semantics in a particularly interesting way. Counterfactuals are construed as weaker than the standard truth conditions. But in order to know that a counterfactual is true, one needs to be in a position to know the slightly stronger proposition that the standard truth conditions obtain. For we can only know that an arbitrarily selected antecedent-world is a consequent-world if we are in a position to know that all antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. Thus, on the present account, the proposition expressed by a counterfactual and the proposition it is assumed to express on the standard semantics constitute a counterexample to the implication principle. Hence, if the puzzle is 

 counterfactuals and arbitrariness 

turned into an argument against truth conditions for counterfactuals, the step in which this implication principle is applied can be resisted. 

Thus, we can win back truth conditions for counterfactuals by trading in a certain kind of epistemicism for counterfactuals: there are principled limits on our epistemic access to counterfactual facts. The puzzling credences in counterfactuals typically reflect this kind of limitation. Given that we have identified only the implication principle as a possible loophole in the argument against truth conditions, epistemicism seems to be without alternatives if the evaluation of counterfactuals is to be explained in terms of truth conditions. 

## .Conclusion 

On the present theory, counterfactuals involve an element of arbitrariness. They are true iff an arbitrarily selected relevant antecedent-world is a consequent-world. These truth conditions are weaker than the standard truth conditions, for they do not imply that all relevant antecedent-worlds are consequent-worlds. Nevertheless, given the element of arbitrariness, counterfactuals have a more demanding epistemic profile than their truth conditions might suggest. In order to know that a counterfactual is true, the standard truth conditions have to be satisfied. But in situations of uncertainty, less is required: that the truth conditions of counterfactuals are satisfied is still supported to a high degree if most but not all relevant antecedent-worlds are known to be consequent-worlds. This helps solve the puzzling evaluation of counterfactuals, which suggests that our credences go with an estimate of a possibly weighted fraction of the consequent-worlds among the relevant antecedent-worlds. 

What we have not yet seen is how this theory fares with respect to other problems in the theory of counterfactuals which have little to do with probability. Problems of this kind will be the topic of the next chapter. I hope this will further sharpen the picture of counterfactuals suggested by the present theory. 

## **** 

## Applications 

The present semantics has various features which bear on longstanding issues in the debate about counterfactuals, like the duality thesis or conditional excluded middle. It is worth seeing how it resolves these issues. In some, perhaps even in most cases, the present semantics makes the somewhat more controversial prediction regarding these issues. For this reason, the following discussion is also intended as a partial defence of the epsilon-based semantics. 

There are some questions which are missing from our discussion: the presuppositions of counterfactuals, Sobel sequences and disjunctive antecedents, to name just a few. This is not because I think that these issues are not important, it is rather because the account developed so far does not bear in any direct way on them and can be combined with various, but perhaps not all approaches to solve these problems. 

## .Counterfactuals with a True Antecedent 

On a semantics like the present one which binds the truth-value of a counterfactual to what is true at a single world, the validity of _modus ponens_ has the consequence that the actual truth of antecedent and consequent is sufficient for the truth of the corresponding counterfactual. That is, the following principle (‘CI’ for _conjunction implication_ ) holds: 

**==> picture [114 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

The reason is the following. Suppose _A_ and ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ are true at the actual world, _w_ . If modus ponens is valid, then _B_ must be true at _w_ , too. But this is only ensured if the relevant _A_ -world on which the truth of ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ depends is _w_ . If it were some other world _w_[′] , _B_ would be true at _w_[′] but could be false at _w_ . In general, then, the truth of _A_ at _w_ will make _w_ the 

 applications 

relevant antecedent-world. Yet this implies that if _A_ and _B_ are true at any world _w_ , the counterfactual ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ will be true at _w_ , for its truth will depend only on whether _B_ is true at _w_ . So, on any kind of Stalnaker semantics, one cannot have modus ponens without having the conjunction implication principle. This is different in standard semantics: there one can have modus ponens without the conjunction principle by imposing weak centering while rejecting strong centering (cf. Lewis : –). 

As we have seen, the easiest way of validating modus ponens on the present semantics is to adopt strong centering: if _A_ is true at _w_ , then _fw(A)_ = { _w_ }—if the antecedent is true at the world of evaluation _w_ , the set of relevant antecedent-worlds contains only _w_ . In this case, the epsilonoperator is forced to select the world of evaluation for true antecedents. If antecedent and consequent are true at the world of evaluation, the consequent will thus be true at the selected world and so the counterfactual will be true, too. As was pointed out in section .., an alternative way of validating modus ponens would be to only adopt weak centering for the selection function _f_ , but to ensure via a coordination constraint on the selection of antecedent-worlds that the epsilon-operator has to select the world of evaluation if the antecedent is true at it. 

To simplify the discussion, I will presuppose that modus ponens is valid for counterfactuals. This assumption is not trivial as one would like to check whether the potential counterexamples to modus ponens for indicative conditionals have interesting analogues in the counterfactual case.[] In any event, it should be clear that modus ponens will hold in a wide range of cases. So we might as well, for the purposes of our discussion, pretend that the present semantics is committed to the universal validity of the conjunction principle. 

Potential counterexamples to the conjunction principle typically consist of an antecedent _A_ and a consequent _B_ where there is (i) no connection between _A_ and _B_ or (ii) not the right kind of connection. An instance of (i) is proposed by Fine (: ): 

() If I were to raise my little finger, there would be rainfall this winter, where we should suppose that the finger will indeed be raised and that there will be rainfall. Instances of (ii) concern cases where the antecedent 

>  Counterexamples to modus ponens are proposed in MacFarlane and Kolodny () and McGee (). 

applications  

made the consequent unlikely, did not make it likely enough or led to it only through an indeterministic process (the first example is adopted, with a twist, by Bennett : f. in response to Lewis’s :  discussion of the same counterfactual; the second one is due to Fine : , and the third one to Bennett : ; for further discussion, see Walters ): 

- () If Caspar had come, the party would have been a good one (the party was good, but Caspar’s presence almost ruined it). 

- () If he had worked hard, he would have passed (he worked hard, but he passed only because he cheated, too). 

- () If the coin were tossed, it would fall heads. 

In response to these kinds of cases, and in particular to the discussion in Bennett (: §), Walters () has given the conjunction principle an extensive defence (see also the reply by Ahmed and the response by Walters ). Walters observes that the truth of Morgenbesser counterfactuals would be hard to reconcile with failures of the conjunction principle. To recall, suppose, for instance, that a coin was tossed and came up heads. We might then say 

- () If I had bet on heads, I would have won. 

But given that winning after betting on heads implies that the coin came up heads and that it must have been tossed before that, it seems incoherent to accept this counterfactual while denying that the coin would have come up heads if it had been tossed. If you take it not to be the case that the coin would have come up heads if it had been tossed, why think that you would have won if you had bet on heads? Walters develops this observation into a precise argument for the conjunction principle. However, his argument relies on principles like the substitution principle (see section ..) which are not part of the base logic resulting from the present semantics. So, although I am very sympathetic to the overall spirit of Walters’s argument, it is not clear whether it can be adopted from the perspective of the present semantics. 

Instead of giving a positive argument for the conjunction principle, I will only sketch what I take to be a promising line of defence against the potential counterexamples. The strategy will be to follow Lewis (: f.) in finding a pragmatic explanation of why these counterfactuals strike us 

 applications 

as odd. I think there is some initial justification for this approach. It does not seem that we have clear intuitions that the candidate counterfactuals are false. We would not, I take it, go ahead and assert their negation. Still, it seems fair to say that there is something wrong with them, but that need not necessarily be falsity. 

A first thought in search of a pragmatic explanation might be that with the conjunction principle in place, conjunctions are at least as strong as the corresponding counterfactuals. Even more, they are strictly stronger, for counterfactuals do not imply the corresponding conjunction. In the light of this, asserting a counterfactual merely on the basis of the conjunction means to assert something weaker where one could have asserted something stronger (cf. Lewis : ). Gricean maxims suggest that this is inappropriate. Unfortunately, it is unclear whether this kind of explanation is on the right track.[] In situations in which we assert something weaker than we could have, it is typically possible to regain appropriate‘ ness by adding something like _. . ._ indeed [the stronger assertion]’. For example, after saying ‘Mary is part of the committee’ while knowing that she is actually the chairwoman, I could continue ‘ _. . ._ indeed, she is the chair of the committee’. Now consider one of the problematic counterfactuals followed-up with the corresponding conjunction: ‘If the coin were tossed, it would fall heads, indeed, it will be tossed and it will fall heads’. It is somewhat unclear to me what one should say about this sentence. It sounds a bit funny, but I don’t hear it as clearly infelicitous either. Tentatively, I conclude that the logical relation between counterfactuals and the corresponding conjunction may well play a role in a pragmatic explanation, but does not on its own provide the material for a full story. 

Trying to exploit the fact that a conjunction is logically stronger than the corresponding counterfactual in giving a pragmatic explanation may seem to run contrary to the first impression about the potential counterexamples. At first sight, it seems that the counterfactuals communicate something—a relevant connection between antecedent and consequent perhaps—which goes beyond the corresponding conjunction (rather than the other way around). Proponents of the counterexamples take this to suggest that counterfactuals are not implied by the corresponding conjunction. But one could explore the possibility that the added elements 

>  Thanks to Alan Hájek for helping me with this problem. 

applications  

of information are only pragmatically conveyed rather than semantically expressed. The basic idea would be that as hearers, we make inferences about the grounds on which a counterfactual is asserted. If the context does not provide any specific clues, we may assume that the grounds are typical or normal. Normal grounds for asserting, for instance, that the party would have been a good one if Caspar had come or that he would have passed if he had worked hard are ones where Caspar’s presence boosts the party or where his working hard was the only reason for his success. If the grounds for asserting a counterfactual are not normal as it is the case when they merely consist in holding the conjunction, then asserting the counterfactual may be misleading unless it is a salient feature of the context that the grounds are different from what they normally are. 

A third, not necessarily independent aspect which is likely to bear on the present issue has to do with the presuppositions of counterfactuals. It is generally agreed that counterfactuals do not presuppose (nor semantically imply) the falsity of the antecedent (see the cases discussed in Anderson and Stalnaker : f.). Nevertheless, asserting a counterfactual seems only appropriate, at least in typical situations, if it is an epistemic possibility in the context that the antecedent is false (cf. von Fintel ). If it is common ground between all parties of the conversation that the antecedent is true, then using a counterfactual seems inappropriate (to confirm, consider ‘We all agree that Jacky was in the bank. # If she had been in the bank, _. . ._ ’). This hypothesis is compatible with the observation by Anderson and Stalnaker that a speaker may use a counterfactual in order to argue for the truth of the antecedent, for in these cases the addressees do not yet grant, or are not assumed to grant, the truth of the antecedent. 

If these three factors all play a role, then contexts in which the problematic counterfactuals are fine have to meet at least three conditions: (i) the speaker should not know that the conjunction is true or else the context should not require her to be maximally informative, (ii) the context should make it clear that the counterfactual is asserted on non-normal grounds and (iii) the truth of the antecedent should not be common ground. It is not clear whether contexts of this kind can always be found. But here is a way of doing this for (), suggested to me by Alan Hájek: ‘There will be rainfall this winter whatever I do. If I were to pray for a drought, there would be rain. If I were to gather all the world’s leading scientists to try to prevent rain, there would be rain. If I were to raise 

 applications 

my little finger, there would be rain _. . ._ ’. In this context, the problematic counterfactual sounds absolutely fine. Part of the reason is that the context makes it clear that the counterfactual is not asserted due to a relevant connection between antecedent and consequent, but rather because of a lack thereof. It is harder to find a similar context for a coin toss case because we typically do not have knowledge about future coin tosses as long as the underlying process is chancy. But suppose I say: ‘I just talked to a time traveller, though I am going to tell you only this: If the coin were tossed, it would fall heads’. Again, the counterfactual seems fine. 

As far as I can see, one has to be even more creative regarding the remaining two problem cases. Let us try to do it for the third case only. To recall, a candidate worked hard and passed, but he passed only because he cheated, too. Can we embed this scenario into a context to make ‘If he had worked hard, he would have passed’ appropriate? Suppose I am the secretary at a philosophy department. I know the results of the exam, but I take my obligation not to tell anyone very seriously, though I do not mind giving hints. A friend of the candidate is worried that he may not have passed because one may have found out about his cheating or perhaps cheating was not even enough and he should have worked harder, for he is always so lazy. Now I say: ‘I cannot tell you whether he passed. But I can tell you this: If he had worked hard, he would have passed’. Admittedly, it is a somewhat curious thing to say, but as far as pragmatics are concerned, it strikes me as felicitous. Given the severe constraints on cases for which we would expect such counterfactuals to be fine, it should be no wonder that they are rare and require a fair amount of creativity to be set up. 

## .Duality 

A prominent hypothesis, put forward by Lewis (: ch. .), is that ‘would’-counterfactuals and ‘might’-counterfactuals are duals of each other. The duality thesis can be given by the following schema (the arrow ‘♦→’ represents ‘might’-counterfactuals): 

**Duality.** _(φ > ψ)_ ≡¬ _(φ_ ♦→¬ _ψ)_ . 

For instance, the following two counterfactuals would be equivalent: 

() If Terry had scored, Chelsea would have won. 

applications  

- () It is not the case that if Terry had scored, Chelsea might not have won. 

By exchanging the negation sign, the truth of a ‘might’-counterfactual is supposed to imply the falsity of the corresponding ‘would’counterfactual: 

- () If the coin had been tossed, it might have landed tails. 

implies, by duality, the falsity of the counterfactual 

- () If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads. 

This example also shows that the duality thesis fails on the present account. It is clear that (a) if the coin had been tossed, it might have landed tails and (b) if the coin had been tossed, it might have landed heads. But our semantics has it that either (a) if the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads or (b) if the coin had been tossed, it would have landed tails, for the arbitrarily selected antecedent-world is either a headsworld or a tails-world. No matter which world gets selected, either the (a)-pair of a ‘might’-counterfactual and a ‘would’-counterfactual or the corresponding (b)-pair constitutes a counterexample to the duality thesis. 

Yet the duality thesis is not unproblematic. Consider the following sentences, which draw on Stalnaker (: f.): 

- () If I had bought a lottery ticket, I might have won, but I am fairly sure that if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would not have won. 

- () If I had bought a lottery ticket, I might have won, but it is likely that if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would not have won. 

These sentences are clearly assertable in appropriate circumstances. But this should not be so if the duality thesis were true, for it construes the two embedded counterfactuals as logically equivalent to the negations of each other. But instances of a schema like ‘ _p_ , but it is likely that ¬ _p_ ’ are, for reasons akin to Moore’s paradox, not assertable. This is evidence against duality.[] 

>  A possible comeback for a defender of the duality thesis would be to explain the plausibility of the two sample sentences by reconstructing the epistemic phrases as having narrow scope only. For problems with this strategy, see section .. 

 applications 

Even if the duality thesis is assumed to fail, it would be nice to explain why the duality thesis appears attractive. For a start, it does not seem difficult to account for ‘might’-counterfactuals in the present framework. Since the semantics is based on a function selecting the set of relevant antecedent-worlds, we can, for example, incorporate the standard approach to ‘might’-counterfactuals: a ‘might’-counterfactual of the form ⌜ _φ_ ♦→ _ψ_ ⌝ is true iff _ψ_ is true in at least one relevant _φ_ -world.[] More formally: 

**==> picture [279 x 24] intentionally omitted <==**

Of course, the standard truth conditions are dual to the truth conditions for ‘might’-counterfactuals so defined. Recall from section .that ‘□[→] ’ was simply defined to have the standard truth conditions without any claim that it corresponds to natural language counterfactuals. On the present picture, it is naturally seen as expressing counterfactual necessity: 

**Counterfactual Necessity.** _w_ |= _(φ_ □[→] _ψ)_ iff ∀ _w_[′] ∈ _fw(_ � _φ_ � _)_ : _w_[′] |= _ψ_ . 

We may ask whether the box-arrow can be expressed in English. As far as I know, there are no ‘must’-counterfactuals in English, where one would simply substitute a ‘must’ for the ‘might’ in a ‘might’counterfactual.[] Perhaps, the following construction with ‘have to’ comes closest to the box-arrow in English: 

>  On the standard account, a ‘might’-counterfactual is not treated as a ‘would’counterfactual the consequent of which is governed by ‘might’ with a higher scoping ‘would’ left implicit. See Swanson (: ) for discussion of this latter possibility. Cf. also Geurts () and Leslie (). It may be, however, that the latter analysis would result in the same truth conditions for simple ‘might’-counterfactuals. Some constraints on which worlds are accessible for the modal operator would need to be imposed, for it is implausible to assume that a ‘might’-counterfactual expresses that something would have been metaphysically possible if the antecedent had been true. It may then be that the set of relevant antecedentworlds is the best candidate for providing the set of accessible worlds. 

>  On this account, a ‘might’-counterfactual is false if its antecedent is impossible, but this effect could be removed by imposing an appropriate proviso. Another issue concerns the centering conditions: If strong centering is adopted for the selection function _f_ in order to account for ‘would’-counterfactuals, one could consider lifting this requirement in accounting for ‘might’-counterfactuals. 

>  This may have a simple explanation: if the hypothesis mentioned in the introduction is correct that the governing modal in the consequent of a counterfactual always assumes a past tense form such as ‘would’ or ‘might’ (the past tense of ‘will’ and ‘may’), then there are no ‘must’-counterfactuals in English because ‘must’ does not have a past tense. 

applications  

() If Terry had scored, Chelsea would have had to win. 

Such counterfactuals seem to be judged false if at least one of the relevant antecedent-worlds is not a consequent-world. For instance, it is clear that the following counterfactual is false: 

- () If the coin had been tossed, it would have had to land heads. 

Since the coin might have landed tails, it seems to follow that it would not have had to land heads. Conversely, if it would not have had to land heads, it might have landed tails. Whether counterfactual necessity can really be expressed by ‘have to’-counterfactuals is something I would like to leave open.[] But for the purposes of this section, I will tentatively assume that they can be. 

As defined, all three kinds of counterfactual statements operate semantically on the same set of relevant worlds. One is accounted for by a universal quantifier, the other by the variable binding epsilon-operator, and the last one by an existential quantifier. This can account for logical relations between them. Setting the case of an impossible antecedent aside, the following three counterfactuals turn out to be of descending strength: 

- () If the coin had been tossed, it would have had to land heads. 

- () If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads. 

- () If the coin had been tossed, it might have landed heads. 

Thus, ‘would’-counterfactuals are construed as being of intermediate strength: they are sandwiched between counterfactual necessity and counterfactual possibility. 

The descending strength of the three types of counterfactuals is modelled in close parallel to the variable strength of statements about the future. Compare: 

- () The coin has to land heads. 

- () The coin will land heads. 

- () The coin might land heads. 

>  Clearly, the English sentences are ‘would’-counterfactuals whose consequent is modalized. So, the suggested semantics is not faithful to the most likely syntactic structure of these sentences. Nevertheless, the assigned truth conditions may still be correct (cf. footnote ). 

 applications 

If we focus on an objective reading of ‘might’ and ‘has to’, the three sentences seem to be of descending strength in a way similar to the counterfactuals above. To a first approximation, the first sentence is true iff in all presently possible futures the coin lands heads, the second is true iff in the actual future the coin lands heads, and the last one is true iff in some presently possible future the coin lands heads. This parallels our semantics of the corresponding counterfactuals. Of course, the parallel can only go so far: there is a sense in which sentences about the future have a day of reckoning, while ‘would’-sentences in the scope of a counterfactual antecedent do not. The parallel is restored by arbitrarily picking a counterfactual future. 

In fact, this may be a fairly close parallel. As briefly mentioned in the introductory chapter, it is a common linguistic hypothesis that ‘would’ is a certain kind of past tense form of ‘will’. Given this, one would expect that ‘would’ inherits the modal character of its origin ‘will’. Since ‘will’sentences express propositions of intermediate strength about the future, it is much more natural to assume that ‘would’-sentences express intermediate propositions as well rather than that they express a certain kind of counterfactual necessity as the standard account predicts. Alternatively, it may be possible to treat ‘will’ as a necessity operator operating on the set of all possible futures. It then would make sense to treat ‘would’ in a similar way. However, I take it to be the more plausible option that ‘will’sentences are not expressions of future necessity.[] 

With these considerations in mind, let us get back to the question of why the duality thesis appears attractive. Here we may note an interesting phenomenon. Although a ‘might’-counterfactual and a corresponding ‘would’-counterfactual with the negation signs filled in can both be true, the present account has it that they cannot both be known to be true. For, if the relevant ‘might’-counterfactual is true, there will be among the relevant antecedent-worlds a world at which the consequent of the ‘would’-counterfactual is false. By the knowability constraint 

>  Note that philosophers such as Thomason () or MacFarlane () who advocate a non-standard account of future contingents actually endorse a non-classical view. A sentence of the form ⌜Will _φ_ ⌝ is said to be true iff _φ_ is true at all possible futures, false iff _φ_ is true at no possible future and neither true nor false otherwise. The clause for truth may look as if they treat ‘will’ as a necessity operator, but the clause for falsity makes it evident that this is not the case, for a statement of necessity would be false if the relevant sentence is false at _some_ possible future rather than being false at _all_ possible futures. 

applications  

(see section .), the ‘would’-counterfactual can therefore not be known. Hence, the conjunction with the ‘might’-counterfactual cannot be known and would, by the knowledge account of assertion, not be assertable. In effect, this explanation deems sentences like 

- () If Terry had scored, Chelsea would have won, but Chelsea might (still) have lost (even) if Terry had scored. 

to be Moore-paradoxical: they are consistent, but the second conjunct undermines the possibility of knowing the first one. Thus, although duality fails on the semantic level, it has a true pragmatic projection: the relevant pairs of ‘might’-counterfactuals and ‘would’-counterfactuals are not co-assertable.[] 

One application of the failure of duality concerns the recent debate about counterfactuals and objective chance.[] On the assumption of non-trivial objective chances, many ‘might’-counterfactuals seem true. By duality, many ‘would’-counterfactuals would come out as false. For instance, there being a small objective chance that _q_ conditional on _p_ seems to support the claim that if it had been the case that _p_ , it might have been the case that _q_ . Given a slight objective chance that the plate flies off sideways, it seems true that 

() If I had dropped the plate, it might not have been broken. 

By duality, we would have to infer the falsity of 

- () If I had dropped the plate, it would have been broken. 

In an indeterministic world, many cases have the underlying structure of this example, which would force many ‘would’-counterfactuals to be 

>  DeRose () and Stalnaker () also give pragmatic explanations of the phenomenon (for critical discussion, see Hájek ). However, their explanations differ from the present one in that they take ‘might’-counterfactuals to be epistemic or quasiepistemic expressing something like the epistemic possibility of the corresponding ‘would’counterfactual. See also the discussion of _weak counterfactual scepticism_ in Eagle (ms). In contrast, ‘might’-counterfactuals can be construed as non-epistemic or ontic on the present semantics. 

>  See Hájek (), Hawthorne (), and Williams (). Many thanks to Tobias Rosefeldt for drawing my attention to this application. 

 applications 

false.[] In this way, the duality thesis threatens many counterfactuals with falsity.[] On the present account, there is no need to go to that extreme. Since the duality thesis fails, the truth of many ‘might’-counterfactuals does not imply the falsity of many ‘would’-counterfactuals. Although the plate might have flown off sideways, we can still be fairly confident that it would have been broken. 

What we have seen is that even if many ‘might’-counterfactuals are true, the corresponding ‘would’-counterfactuals need not be false. In showing this, I went along with the assumption, endorsed in Hájek () and considered favourably in Hawthorne (), that a positive conditional physical chance implies the corresponding ‘might’-counterfactual. Let me just add that this is an assumption which is not implied by the present account, although it could be added to it. What is a consequence of the present account is that the truth of a ‘might’-counterfactual is implied by there being a positive counterfactual chance of the consequent under the antecedent. But a positive physical chance need not be taken to always imply a positive counterfactual chance. Hence, it is compatible with the present account that a plate flying off sideways is just too remote a possibility for being included in the set of relevant antecedent-worlds (cf. Williams  in response to Hawthorne ). On such a conception, we might even be able to know, and therefore be able to assert, that the plate would not have flown off sideways if it had been dropped (cf. also section .). 

## .Conditional Excluded Middle 

On the present account, there is a certain kind of flexibility concerning the logic of counterfactuals. By adding or omitting constraints on the selection function _f_ and possibly also through what I have called coordination constraints, the logic of counterfactuals can be influenced. 

>  It is not quite clear to what extent the assumption of indeterminism is essential to this argument. Even in a deterministic world, many (but perhaps less) ‘might’-counterfactuals of the envisaged kind still seem to be true. Cf. Hájek (). 

>  Hájek () embraces this argument. In the postscript to Lewis (), Lewis suggests resolving the conflict by positing an ambiguity in ‘might’-counterfactuals. On the reading which is captured by the duality thesis, the obtaining of the relevant objective chances would not, according to Lewis, make the counterfactual true. On a second ‘would-be-possible’reading, the ‘might’-counterfactual is taken to be true without obeying duality and therefore would not force a corresponding ‘would’-counterfactual to be false. 

applications  

However, there is also a limit to this flexibility: certain inferences will be valid even if no constraints are imposed. So, there is a certain _base logic_ for counterfactuals which results already from the general shape of the present semantics. There is one important consequence of this base logic which is controversial in the debate about counterfactuals: the validity of _conditional excluded middle_ . 

**Conditional Excluded Middle.** _(φ > ψ)_ ∨ _(φ >_ ¬ _ψ)_ . 

In words: if _A_ had been the case, _B_ would have been the case, or, if _A_ had been the case, _B_ would not have been the case. This principle is valid on the present semantics: if the antecedent is impossible, then both counterfactuals are vacuously true and if the antecedent is not impossible, then the selected _φ_ -world will either be a _ψ_ -world or a ¬ _ψ_ -world, in which case either ⌜ _(φ > ψ)_ ⌝ or ⌜ _(φ >_ ¬ _ψ)_ ⌝ will be true. In contrast, conditional excluded middle is invalid according to standard semantics, for it may be that neither all relevant _φ_ -worlds are _ψ_ -worlds nor all _φ_ -worlds are ¬ _ψ_ -worlds. 

Lewis (: ) calls this principle ‘the principal virtue and the principal vice of Stalnaker’s theory’, but he admits that denying it sounds incoherent: 

Given conditional excluded middle, we cannot truly say such things as this: 

_It is not the case that if Bizet and Verdi were compatriots, Bizet would be Italian; and it is not the case that if Bizet and Verdi were compatriots, Bizet would not be Italian; nevertheless, if Bizet and Verdi were compatriots, Bizet either would or would not be Italian._ 

[ _. . ._ ] I want to say this, and think it is probably true; [ _. . ._ ] But off hand, I must admit, it does sound like a contradiction. (Lewis : ) 

This indicates that conditional excluded middle enjoys a certain amount of intuitive plausibility.[] But I do not want to play this card too strongly, for an opponent of conditional excluded middle might explain its plausibility as resulting from scope confusions as Williamson () does: instances of conditional excluded middle might be confused with 

>  Intuitions may vary. If one thinks that a chance-related counterfactual such as ‘If the coin had been tossed, it would have landed heads’ is false and not merely unknown, one may lack the intuition about the corresponding instance of conditional excluded middle because one is inclined to reason that it has two false disjuncts. 

 applications 

instances of the schema ⌜ _φ > (ψ_ ∨¬ _ψ)_ ⌝ which is valid according to any reasonable semantics for counterfactuals. 

Lewis builds a case against conditional excluded middle by connecting the issue with the duality thesis: 

Another manifestation of Stalnaker’s Assumption is that, except in the vacuous case, the difference between ‘would’ and ‘might’ counterfactuals is lost. [ _. . ._ ] But surely English ‘would’ and ‘might’ counterfactuals do sometimes differ in truth value, and not only in the vacuous case. Stalnaker therefore cannot define the ‘might’ counterfactual as I have done. (Lewis : ) 

No doubt that there is a difference between ‘would’-counterfactuals and ‘might’-counterfactuals. Lewis’s claim that this difference would be lost if a Stalnaker semantics were adopted presupposes that a ‘might’counterfactual ⌜ _φ_ ♦→ _ψ_ ⌝ is—via the duality thesis—assigned the truth conditions of the corresponding ‘would’-counterfactual ⌜¬ _(φ >_ ¬ _ψ)_ ⌝. If ‘might’-counterfactuals were given this semantics, then the relevant difference would be lost, for if the antecedent is not impossible, ⌜¬ _(φ >_ ¬ _ψ)_ ⌝ is equivalent to ⌜ _(φ > ψ)_ ⌝ on a Stalnaker semantics and so, by duality, ⌜ _φ_ ♦→ _ψ_ ⌝ would be equivalent to ⌜ _(φ > ψ)_ ⌝. 

There are a couple of things I would like to say in response. First of all, there are good reasons to reject the duality thesis.[] But if duality fails, it is actually an advantage of the present account that it does not account for ‘might’-counterfactuals in terms of ‘would’-counterfactuals by deploying the duality thesis. Secondly, we have also seen how ‘might’counterfactuals may be assigned truth conditions without adopting the duality hypothesis. In fact, the proposed truth conditions agree (modulo the limit assumption—see next section) with the truth conditions Lewis assigns to them. It is only that the truth conditions for ‘might’counterfactuals are not defined in terms of ‘would’-counterfactuals. But I see no prior reason why such an inter-definability should be a desideratum. As we would not expect that the truth conditions of simple ‘might’sentences can be defined in terms of simple ‘will’-sentences, there is no prior reason to suppose that we can define the truth conditions of ‘might’counterfactuals in terms of ‘would’-counterfactuals. Such a reason only emerges if we make the further assumption that ‘would’ is a necessity modal, but this is an assumption which can only be justified in the context 

>  Cf. Williams (), who defends conditional excluded middle along similar lines. 

applications  

of a theory of counterfactuals—it is not a constraint we should impose prior to investigation. 

## .The Limit Assumption 

There is a background assumption of the present proposal which is sometimes considered to be controversial for a reason quite similar to why conditional excluded middle can be doubted. The semantics presupposes that there is always a set of relevant antecedent-worlds. The existence of such a set is what is sometimes questioned, or so it may seem. 

As introduced by Lewis (: f.), the _limit assumption_ says that to any antecedent which is not impossible, there is a set of closest antecedentworlds. What the limit assumption thereby excludes is the possibility that there may be a world _w_ and an antecedent _φ_ , such that there are closer and closer _φ_ -worlds without there ever being a set of closest _φ_ -worlds (relative to _w_ ). Lewis finds the limit assumption doubtful: 

Unfortunately, we have no right to assume that there always are a smallest antecedent-permitting sphere and, within it, a set of closest antecedent-worlds. Suppose we entertain the counterfactual supposition that at this point there appears a line more than an inch long. (Actually it is just under an inch.) There ′′ are worlds with a line [′′] long; worlds presumably closer to ours with a line  [] ; ′′ worlds presumably still closer to ours with a line  [] ; worlds presumably still closer _. . ._ . But how long is the line in the _closest_ worlds with a line more than an inch long? (Lewis : ) 

Assuming that the length of the line is the relevant dimension of comparison, Lewis gives here an example of an infinite sequence of antecedent-worlds which are closer and closer to the actual world. Of course, one may question this particular example (cf. the footnote in Lewis : ). But I see no reason to doubt that an example with the relevant structure can be given. For all we know, the present example might be just fine. Therefore, we should conclude that the limit assumption is false. 

Does the failure of the limit assumption challenge the present account? The semantics presupposes that there is always a set of relevant antecedent-worlds. If the set of relevant antecedent-worlds is conceived of as the set of closest antecedent-worlds, then Lewis’s example would show that there is not always a set of relevant antecedent-worlds. The present 

 applications 

semantics would be based on a false background assumption. However, I do not want to make this identification: the set of relevant antecedentworlds is not to be thought of as consisting exclusively of the closest worlds.[] 

To get an idea of what the conception of relevance which I have in mind amounts to, let us apply it to Lewis’s case. In the right circumstances, I would assume that the set of relevant worlds may well contain for any lengths between and inches a world in which the line has that length. Of course, it will not contain for any length a world in which the line is of that length: no world in which the line covers the whole width of the page, say, may be relevant. There will be an upper limit. What the upper limit is will depend on the details of the example, for instance, on whether the author played with the thought of drawing a really long line or not, etc. Besides, we should reckon with the possibility that it will often be a vague matter what the upper limit is. 

Let us look at a counterfactual which utilizes the supposition Lewis considers: 

() If the line had been longer than one inch, it would have been ′′  [] long. 

For Lewis, this counterfactual is false, I take it, and can, given appropriate circumstances, be known to be false (cf. Lewis : ).[] On the present account, the counterfactual _might_ be true. Worlds at which the line is  [] ′′ long can be assumed to be among the relevant antecedent-worlds. But the set of relevant antecedent-worlds can also be assumed to contain worlds at which the line is shorter and worlds at which the line is longer. By the knowability constraint, we can therefore not know whether the line would have been  [] ′′ long if it had been longer than one inch. 

>  Cf. Edgington (: ). Bennett (: §), following a suggestion by Stalnaker (), adopts the limit assumption but tries to construe it in a way different from Lewis by making closeness more coarse-grained and context-dependent so that in certain contexts different lengths of the line would not make for more or less closeness. If the context-dependence is subtracted from this proposal, then it seems to be quite similar to the present suggestion. 

>  Modifying the standard account accordingly comes at a cost, though. As Pollock (: –) stresses, the inference from ⌜ _φ > ψi_ ⌝ and ⌜ _φ > ψj_ ⌝ to ⌜ _φ > (ψi_ ∧ _ψj)_ ⌝ can no longer be iterated infinitely many times and thus shows a behaviour different from ordinary conjunction introduction. This point has recently been emphasized by Fine (a,b). 

applications  

A consequence of this way of construing the set of relevant worlds is that the corresponding ‘might’-counterfactual comes out true: 

() If the line had been longer than one inch, it might have been ′′  [] long. 

To my ears, this counterfactual sounds true. If so, this is some evidence for the package deal of combining a certain conception of relevance with a certain semantics for ‘might’-counterfactuals.[] 

In sum, I suggest the following resolution of the issue concerning the limit assumption. Lewis is right in rejecting it. Sometimes there is no set of closest antecedent-worlds. But the set of relevant antecedent-worlds at work in a semantics for counterfactuals need not be assumed to consist solely of the closest antecedent-worlds. Rather, it may contain antecedentworlds which are more or less close to the actual world. Then the truth conditions of counterfactuals can still be specified in terms of a set of relevant worlds. 

>  For Lewis, the ‘might’-counterfactual comes out false, partly due to his semantics for ‘would’-counterfactuals without the limit assumption, partly due to his account of ‘might’counterfactuals in terms of the duality thesis. 

## **** 

## Triviality 

For indicative conditionals, there is a certain class of formal arguments—the so-called _triviality results_ —which make trouble for any truth-conditional semantics which is supposed to underpin something like Adams’s thesis.[] The triviality results received their name because they establish, in some way or other, that a given version of Adams’s thesis can only be satisfied by a very small class of rather poor probability functions. Some, perhaps most of the authors which have provided a triviality result (e.g. Lewis ) interpret the triviality results as arguments against Adams’s thesis.[] If one instead holds on to Adams’s thesis, then these results can be seen as arguments against truth conditions for indicative conditionals (cf. Edgington : ). Although the complexity of (some of) the triviality results makes it somewhat harder to evaluate their philosophical impact, they have proven to be very powerful and are often considered to be the biggest threat to anyone who tries to combine a version of Adams’s thesis with truth conditions for indicative conditionals. Therefore, it is a natural question whether the evaluation constraint for counterfactuals as developed in chapter , which has a strong claim for being the counterfactual analogue of Adams’s thesis, gives rise to similar triviality results for counterfactuals. As it turns out, this is indeed the case. Leitgeb (a) and Williams () have provided triviality results for counterfactuals (see also fn.  

>  The original triviality results are due to Lewis (cf. Lewis , c). An overview of various results is given by Hájek and Hall (). 

>  In the context of the triviality results, Adams’s thesis is usually taken to concern propositions, not sentences. Hence, on a sentential interpretation, the triviality results leave Adams’s thesis unchallenged. However, if sound, they would show that one cannot reinterpret the probabilities of conditionals qua sentences as probabilities of propositions expressed by these sentences. 

triviality  

in the previous chapter).[] This would challenge the present semantics, for it comes with the promise to underpin the evaluation constraint for more than a trivial class of probability functions. Before looking at these results, I will start by evaluating Lewis’s discussion of imaging, for he proves not only an interesting tenability result for Stalnaker conditionals, but also shows that only Stalnaker conditionals can validate the relevant kind of evaluation constraint. 

## .Lewis on Imaging 

In his discussion of imaging, Lewis () shows that the non-generalized imaging constraint can be satisfied by the corresponding Stalnaker truth conditions. Recall that the non-generalized version of imaging is defined by assuming uniqueness about the selected antecedent-worlds. So, for any antecedent _A_ and any world _w_ , the set of relevant antecedent-worlds _fw(A)_ contains exactly one world, _wA_ . The Stalnaker truth conditions can then be defined by setting 

(S) _(w_ |= _A > B)_ iff _(wA_ |= _B)_ , 

i.e. a counterfactual is true at world _w_ iff the consequent is true at the single most relevant antecedent-world _wA_ . The proposition expressed by the counterfactual is then the set of worlds { _w_ : _wA_ |= _B_ }. What is the probability of this set? 

Suppose _P_ is defined over a finite set of worlds _W_ . Then 

() _P(_ { _w_ : _wA_ |= _B_ } _)_ =[�] _w_ : _wA_ |= _B[P][(][w][)]_[ =][ �] _w_[′] ∈ _B_ � _w_ : _wA_ |= _w_[′] _[ P][(][w][)]_[.] 

But the latter just is the non-generalized image _P[A] (B)_ (cf. section .). The probability of a Stalnaker conditional is the image of the consequent under the antecedent: 

() _P[A] (B)_ = _P(A > B)_ . 

The reason is simple: in evaluating a Stalnaker conditional, we have to ask, for every epistemically possible world, whether the antecedent-world closest to it is a consequent-world. But this means that we could equally 

>  I have also learned through the references in Leitgeb (a) and Schwarz (ms) that Rachael Briggs has proven a triviality result for a closely related evaluation constraint in an unpublished manuscript. 

 triviality 

well ask which of the worlds closest to some epistemically possible worlds are consequent-worlds. If we weight the worlds with probabilities, we see that in evaluating a Stalnaker conditional, we shift the probability of an epistemically possible world to the closest antecedent-worlds which are consequent-worlds. 

So, Stalnaker truth conditions satisfy the corresponding nongeneralized imaging constraint. This bears on possible triviality results in the following way: a triviality result which rules out the possibility of satisfying the non-generalized imaging constraint will show definitely too much. 

Lewis () has also proven an important converse of this: only Stalnaker conditionals satisfy suppositional constraints like the imaging constraint. The result is very general. We first need an abstract characterization of assigning a probability _P(B_ || _A)_ to a proposition _B_ under a supposition _A_ . A natural idea is to generalize the basic properties of conditional probability. Lewis considers the following three constraints on suppositions (cf. also the discussion in Joyce : ch. ): 

(C) _P(A_ || _A)_ = . 

(C) If _P(A)_ = , then _P(_ •|| _A)_ = _P_ . 

(C) If _P(B_ || _A)_ = and _P(A_ || _B)_ = , then _P(_ •|| _A)_ = _P(_ •|| _B)_ . 

The first constraint requires that a supposition should be made certain under itself. The second requirement is that if the supposition is already certain, making it should not change anything. A supposition should be integrated by making minimal changes only—if none are needed, nothing gets changed. The idea of minimal revision is in the background of the third constraint as well. If integrating _A_ at minimal costs makes _B_ certain and integrating _B_ at minimal costs makes _A_ certain, then both suppositions require the same changes to integrate them (this is a probabilistic variant of the equivalence condition on relevance; cf. .). It is easily verified that conditional probability and imaging, both the generalized and the non-generalized version, have these three features. 

Now, given an arbitrary suppositional operation ||, we may ask whether there is a conditional operator → which satisfies 

**==> picture [122 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

This is a generalization of Adams’s thesis and the imaging constraint. For any kind of supposition, we may ask whether there is a conditional 

triviality  

such that the probability of the conditional is the probability of the consequent under the supposition of the antecedent. Lewis shows that if a conditional satisfies this equation for all probability functions _P_ and all sentences _A_ and _B_ , then it is a Stalnaker conditional, i.e. we can find a selection function such that the conditional is true at a world just in case the consequent is true at the single selected antecedent-world. In other worlds, only Stalnaker conditionals are capable of satisfying suppositional constraints like (Sup). 

The idea is the following. Consider a probability function _Pw_ which concentrates all probability on _w_ . In other words, _P_ is omniscient about _w_ . Now, if we engage in a supposition from the perspective of _Pw_ , what are the resulting probabilities, i.e. how does _Pw(_ •|| _A)_ behave? The core observation Lewis makes is that the resulting probability function must also concentrate all probability on a single world. If it were otherwise, we would find a _B_ such that  _< Pw(B_ || _A) <_ . By (Sup), this would imply  _< Pw(A_ → _B) <_ , which cannot be, for _Pw_ assigns only values of and . As _Pw_ is decided about all matters of fact including conditionals, it cannot assign any intermediate values. But then _Pw(_ •|| _A)_ will also concentrate all probability on a single world _w_[′] so that we can set _wA_ := _w_[′] . This defines a Stalnaker conditional and given the properties of ||, it is well behaved, i.e. it satisfies the constraints standardly imposed on a selection function. For instance, the first constraint, (C), guarantees that the world _w_[′] is an _A_ -world and (C) secures that if _A_ is already true at _w_ , then _w_[′] = _w_ . 

In sum, a Stalnaker conditional underwrites imaging and only Stalnaker conditionals satisfy suppositional constraints like imaging. To some extent, this is reassuring, for the present semantics assigns counterfactuals Stalnaker type truth conditions by binding the truth-value of a counterfactual to what happens at a single world. On the other hand, it aims to validate the generalized version of imaging. For this reason, Lewis’s observation does not yet provide a safe harbour for this account. As a matter of fact, we shall see shortly that the observation Lewis makes about decided probability functions like _Pw_ can actually be levelled against any attempt to satisfy the generalized imaging constraint. 

## .Leitgeb’s Observation 

Leitgeb (a) uses the the same observation Lewis makes to argue against the idea that the probability of a counterfactual could go with 

 triviality 

the corresponding conditional objective chance (see also the discussion in Schwarz ms). Although the proposal Leitgeb targets is not quite the evaluation constraint adopted here (he focusses on Skyrms’s account), this difference proves inessential and I will discuss the objection as if it were directed against the generalized imaging account. Like Lewis, Leitgeb observes that for a probability function _Pw_ centred on a single possible world, we have 

() _Pw(A > B)_ = or _Pw(A > B)_ = , 

depending on whether or not the counterfactual is true at _w_ . 

Now suppose that we adopt the generalized imaging account. Then for some world _w_ , the set of relevant _A_ -worlds is not a singleton set and so there will be a possible consequent _B_ such that the counterfactual chance of _B_ under _A_ relative to _w_ will be an intermediate value, say  _/_ . In this case, the image of _B_ under _A_ will be  _/_ relative to _w_ and because _Pw_ is not uncertain about which world is actual, the imaging constraint requires 

() _Pw(A > B)_ = _P[A] (B)_ =  _/_ , which contradicts our earlier conclusion that it must be either or . This is a quick argument against any (bivalent) semantics which pretends to underpin the generalized imaging constraint. What can we say? 

On an intuitive picture, one may feel tempted to deny the assumption that a probability function centred on a single world must assign maximal or minimal probability to any statement, including counterfactuals. If we think of _Pw_ as comprising all the information which can possibly be gathered in _w_ , it may still be that some propositions remain undecided. As we saw, the semantics in terms of the epsilon operator actually predicts that in a case in which the counterfactual chance assumes an intermediate value like  _/_ , the counterfactual will be undecided in _w_ . So, if _Pw_ represents a possible state of knowledge in _w_ , it will not decide the counterfactual. 

However, thinking of _Pw_ as a possible state of knowledge is probably not the intended interpretation relevant to a triviality result like Leitgeb’s. Here _Pw_ is simply constructed by conditionalizing a probability function on the singleton set containing _w_ and it is assumed that a possible world is maximal in the sense that it decides all propositions, including counterfactuals. So, by construction, _Pw_ assigns probability or to any counterfactual. 

triviality  

But if _Pw_ is certain about the counterfactual, it evidently possesses inadmissible information. Direct information about the truth-value of a counterfactual is the clearest example of inadmissibility. In such a case, the evaluation constraint simply does not apply as we have detailed in our discussion in chapter (Schwarz (ms) makes a similar point). The Principal Principle type formulation of it is explicitly cautious in this regard: the principle only applies to situations in which no inadmissible information is possessed. The imaging constraint does not come with such a proviso, but this is, as we argued, a reason to think that it may not hold in full generality. It was exactly because of this possible difference to the Principal Principle type formulation that we should feel committed to the imaging constraint only under the assumption that issues about admissibility do not play any role. 

In sum, a credence function like _Pw_ does not always represent a possible state of knowledge on the semantics I favour. More important in the context of the triviality result is, however, the observation that it contains inadmissible information by deciding the truth-value of the counterfactual. In such cases, the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals defended here is not applicable, for it is only intended for situations in which we do not possess information about the counterfactual which goes beyond the information about the counterfactual chances. 

## .Williams’s Extension of Lewis’s Triviality Result 

In a recent paper, Williams () argues that Lewis’s () original triviality result can be extended to counterfactuals. His argument is based on Skyrms’s constraint as discussed in chapter , but Williams reckons with the possibility that it may call for some restrictions. Lewis’s original result aims at a reductio of the propositional version of Adams’s thesis, namely that there is a connective ∗ such that for all propositions _A_ and _B_ , modelled as sets of possible worlds, and a class of probability functions _P_ : _P(A_ ∗ _B)_ = _P(B_ | _A)_ . 

To begin with, let us note two basic facts about conditional probabilities. First, conditional probabilities, if defined, satisfy the following equation (where _B_ is the complement of _B_ , i.e. _B_ ’s negation): 

() _P(A)_ = _P(A_ | _B)_ · _P(B)_ + _P(A_ | _B)_ · _P(B)_ . 

 triviality 

In words: the probability of a proposition _A_ is, for any _B_ , the probability of _A_ given _B_ weighted by the probability of _B_ plus the probability of _A_ given _B_ weighted by the probability of _B_ . Formally, this follows from the definition of conditional probability as _[P][(] P[A] (_[∩] _B)[B][)]_[. By multiplica-] tion with _P(B)_ , this reduces the first summand to _P(A_ ∩ _B)_ and similarly the second one to _P(A_ ∩ _B)_ . Hence, the result follows from _P(A)_ = _P(A_ ∩ _B)_ + _P(A_ ∩ _B)_ : the probability of _A_ can be packaged into the probability of _A_ ∩ _B_ and _A_ ∩ _B_ . 

The second fact applies to two probability functions _PB_ and _PB_[which] are defined as _P(_ •| _B)_ and _P(_ •| _B)_ , that is, they come from _P_ by conditionalizing on _B_ and _B_ respectively. Now note that _PB(B_ | _A)_ = because it equals _P(B_ | _A_ ∩ _B)_ ; for analogous reasons, ~~_P_~~ _B_ ~~_[(]_~~ _[B]_[|] _[A][)]_[ =][ . This is already] all Lewis needs. 

To get his original result, we start by applying the first fact to the potential conditional proposition and get 

() _P(A_ ∗ _B)_ = _P(A_ ∗ _B_ | _B)_ · _P(B)_ + _P(A_ ∗ _B_ | _B)_ · _P(B)_ . By the definition of _PB_ and ~~_P_~~ _B_ ~~[,]~~[ this simplifies to] 

() _P(A_ ∗ _B)_ = _PB(A_ ∗ _B)_ · _P(B)_ + _PB_ ~~_[(]_~~ _[A]_[ ∗] _[B][)]_[ ·] _[ P][(] B)_ . 

If we now apply the propositional version of Adams’s thesis to _PB_ and ~~_P_~~ _B_ ~~[,]~~ we find 

() _P(A_ ∗ _B)_ = _PB(A_ | _B)_ · _P(B)_ + _PB_ ~~_[(]_~~ _[A]_[|] _[B][)]_[ ·] _[ P][(] B)_ . Given the second fact about probabilities, the conditional probabilities are and respectively. We can therefore conclude that 

() _P(A_ ∗ _B)_ =  · _P(B)_ +  · ¬ _B_ = _P(B)_ . 

The final conclusion says that the probability of the conditional must equal the probability of the consequent. However, this cannot generally be the case and contradicts Adams’s thesis for many choices of the relevant probability function. We may note that Lewis’s result only requires that if Adams’s thesis holds for a probability function _P_ , it also holds for probability functions which come from _P_ by conditionalization. 

Williams points out that the proof is formally valid and so does not need to presuppose anything about the kind of probability represented by _P_ as long as the relevant kind obeys the standard laws of probability. For this reason, we may as well interpret the result as also showing that there is 

triviality  

no conditional connective such that the _objective chance_ of a conditional is always the corresponding conditional objective chance. Given that Skyrms’s version of the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals operates with objective chances, this interpretative possibility is a potential source of trouble. 

But in order to run Lewis’s result on counterfactuals, Williams needs to bridge the gap between the subjective probabilities and the objective chances which are connected in the evaluation constraint, for the evaluation constraint does not say that the objective chance of a counterfactual is the corresponding conditional objective chance. It rather says that the subjective probability of a counterfactual should be so-and-so on the assumption that the corresponding objective chances are so-and-so. 

To close this gap, Williams considers a scenario where all the relevant objective chances are known. We may assume that the the relevant subjective probability function _P_ comes from an initial probability function by updating it (only) with the relevant chance information. This is helpful in two ways. First, it avoids the problem that the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals only makes a conditional claim, for if the chances are known, i.e. the subjective probability that they are so-andso is , Skyrms’s version forces them to actually match those chances. Hence, we get 

() _P(A > B)_ = Ch _(B_ | _A)_ . 

Secondly, the assumption that all the chances are known helps extend Lewis’s result also in another respect. If all the relevant chances are known, then in particular the chances of a counterfactual being true are known. This makes Lewis’s original Principal Principle applicable, for given that _P_ was only given information about chances, it is clear that it cannot contain any inadmissible information. Thus, we see that our credence in the counterfactual should also go with the objective chance of the counterfactual: 

**==> picture [124 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

If we know that the objective chance of the counterfactual being true is Ch _(A > B)_ , our credence in the counterfactual should be Ch _(A > B)_ . But combining the two equations now gives us 

() Ch _(A > B)_ = Ch _(B_ | _A)_ . 

 triviality 

This bridges the gap between subjective probabilities and objective chances. By the evaluation constraint, the subjective probability of a counterfactual should be the corresponding objective conditional chance. By Lewis’s Principal Principle, the subjective probability of a counterfactual should also be the unconditional objective chance of the counterfactual. But then the conditional objective chance has to match the unconditional objective chance which instantiates a version of Adams’s thesis with objective chances substituted for subjective probabilities. Given that the kind of probability does not matter for Lewis’s proof, we can reduce a propositional interpretation of the resulting thesis to absurdity, or so Williams suggests. 

There are a couple of issues with Williams’s argument. The first question is perhaps whether the proof is formally valid. There is reason to doubt its validity having to do with the fact that in order to run Lewis’s original triviality result on the chance function Ch, we do not only need the instance () = ‘Ch _(A > B)_ = Ch _(B_ | _A)_ ’, but also two further instances which come from Ch by conditionalizing on _B_ and _B_ respectively: 

**==> picture [132 x 27] intentionally omitted <==**

The question is whether these two further instances are also implied by the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. If not, then the starting assumptions for Lewis’s result are not met. Although the evaluation constraint would imply certain instances identifying the objective chance of a counterfactual with the corresponding conditional chance, it would not yield enough such instances for Lewis’s result to kick in.[] 

Now, as far as I can see, these further instances are not implied by the evaluation constraint. To make the case more vivid, consider a simple coin toss counterfactual ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝. The evaluation constraint on counterfactuals may then imply that the subjective probability of ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ should equal the objective chance of the coin coming up heads a certain while before it would have been tossed, say at time _t_ . So, we should write more precisely 

>  Schwarz (ms) makes a similar point, but his argument is based on the observation that the relevant class of chance functions may not be closed under conditionalization, not that the relevant equations are not implied by the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. 

triviality  

**==> picture [128 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

Suppose further we update the chance function with _B_ , i.e. with the information that the coin came up heads. Is the equation 

**==> picture [130 x 12] intentionally omitted <==**

implied by the evaluation constraint? First of all, note that this equation is quite implausible. After updating, the chance of _B_ , i.e. that the coin came up heads, is and so our credence in the counterfactual would have to be . But nothing in the evaluation constraint suggests this: what is suggested is that the credences in counterfactuals should go with objective chances taken at certain points in time from which on the history of the relevant counterfactual worlds are allowed to depart from the actual history. It says nothing about how the credences in counterfactuals should relate to objective chances which are conditionalized on the information contained in the consequent of the counterfactual (or its negation). Hence, it seems we cannot simply run Lewis’s triviality result on the instances of equations about objective chance, for the evaluation constraint does not imply enough of such instances. This does not mean that the equations it does imply might not be problematic, but Lewis’s original result cannot be used to show this. 

Another difficulty concerns the role of admissibility in the application of the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. This problem is best seen when we consider Williams’s argument as applied to the evaluation constraint defended in chapter , where it is given in the form of a Lewisian Principal Principle. I do think that the same problem already arises in the application of Skyrms’s constraint, but it would be harder to make out. In any case, seeing how the argument fares under the constraint I ultimately favour is what presently matters most. 

The evaluation constraint defended in chapter is formulated in terms of conditional counterfactual chances and not in terms of ordinary physical chances. On the other hand, Lewis’s Principal Principle is formulated in such terms. As a result, the two core assumptions about objective chance in Williams’s argument will concern on the present interpretation, strictly speaking, two different kinds of probability: physical chances and counterfactual chances. To keep the two distinct, let us write, only for the purposes of the present discussion, ‘PCh’ for physical chances as they figure in Lewis’s Principal Principle and ‘CCh’ for counterfactual chances 

 triviality
