as they figure in the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals (so far, we have occasionally used ‘Ch’ both for physical and for counterfactual chances). 

Although we have seen that (conditional) physical chances and counterfactual chances do not always coincide, there are still many instances for which we can assume that they have the same value. A case in point is a coin toss counterfactual. We may assume that the conditional physical chance taken at the relevant point in time of the coin coming up heads conditional on being tossed is  _/_ just like the corresponding counterfactual chance. So, we can surely find a counterfactual ⌜ _A > B_ ⌝ for which 

() PCh _(B_ | _A)_ = CCh _(B_ | _fw(A))_ =  _/_ .[] 

(Recall here that counterfactual chances are computed by conditionalizing on the set of relevant antecedent-worlds and not by conditionalizing on all antecedent-worlds.) 

By identifying the two kinds of chance over a certain range of cases, Williams’s argument regains its applicability. But something interesting happens to his assumption that all the relevant objective chances are known. In a first step, this assumption is used to apply to the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals in order to conclude that the subjective probability of the counterfactual matches the objective chances. Given that the evaluation constraint defended in chapter is cast in terms of counterfactual chances, what has to be assumed is that the relevant counterfactual chances are known: 

() _P(_ CCh _(B_ | _fw(A))_ =  _/_  _)_ = . 

This much is unproblematic: although we are sometimes uncertain about what the counterfactual chances are, we can in principle become certain about these matters. A simple coin toss counterfactual may well be a case in which we know the counterfactual chances. By the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals, it would follow that our credence in the counterfactual matches the corresponding counterfactual chance provided no 

>  It is perhaps worth noticing that on the non-generalized version of imaging, the counterfactual chances will always be or , for _fw(A)_ will be a singleton set. In this case, the value  _/_ is, of course, not possible nor is any other intermediate value. But if only the values and are assumed, a resulting equation of the form Ch _(A > B)_ = Ch _(B_ | _A)_ is unproblematic and does not run into triviality. So, the implicit assumption of intermediate values is why Williams’s argument is compatible with Lewis’s tenability result. 

triviality  

inadmissible information is possessed on top of that (this will become important later on): 

() _P(A > B)_ = CCh _(B_ | _fw(A))_ =  _/_ . 

But now note that knowing the counterfactual chances does not make Lewis’s Principal Principle applicable. In order to apply it, we also need to know the relevant physical chances, in particular the unconditional physical chance of the counterfactual being true: 

() _P(_ PCh _(A > B)_ = _x)_ = , for some _x_ . With Lewis’s Principal Principal, we can then conclude that 

() _P(A > B)_ = PCh _(A > B)_ . Our credence in the counterfactual matches its objective chance. Given that it also matches the corresponding counterfactual chance and that we have chosen the example so that the latter does not differer from the conditional physical chance in this particular case, we can still derive the problematic equation 

() PCh _(A > B)_ = PCh _(B_ | _A)_ . 

The physical chance of the counterfactual would equal the corresponding conditional physical chance. 

Although we have already seen that this equation does not lead to triviality via Lewis’s original result, it could still be a potential source of trouble. For this reason, it is worth seeing whether we can avoid this conclusion. It has been derived in much the same way as Williams suggests in his argument, but we had to make an additional assumption about what the respective agent is supposed to know. Not only did we have to assume that the agent knows the conditional counterfactual chances, the agent was also supposed to know the unconditional physical chance of the counterfactual being true (see () and ()). The former assumption is uncontroversial: conditional counterfactual chances are frequently known. The latter assumption, on the other hand, is very strong: knowing the unconditional physical chance of a counterfactual being true seems hard. When the truth value of a counterfactual is still undecided, how can we know the unconditional chance of a counterfactual being true? It does not seem that we have any direct epistemic access to facts of this kind. Of course, we do have access to conditional physical chance. 

 triviality 

If there were a valid bridge principle linking conditional physical chances of counterfactuals to unconditional ones, this would explain how such chances could be known. But the assumption of such a bridge principle is what Williams’s argument is directed against and what we are presently looking to avoid. So, it seems like an open question how the assumption that the unconditional physical chances are known could be justified. 

But perhaps these kinds of chances are only hard but not impossible to know. So, let us grant for the sake of argument that they can be known. This leads to what I take to be the most serious problem with this assumption. We have already mentioned in chapter that information about unconditional physical chances is likely to be inadmissible in the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. Lewis’s original Principal Principle and the structurally similar formulation of the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals come with different conceptions of admissibility. We have put the difference in terms of _o_ -admissibility versus _c_ -admissibility. For instance, although information about the future is inadmissible in the original principle (if it bears on the proposition in question), it is admissible in the evaluation of counterfactuals. On the other hand, information about unconditional chances, which is clearly admissible in the original principle, is not generally admissible in the evaluation of counterfactuals. This is most easily seen for cases in which the unconditional chance of a counterfactual is or at some late point _t_ in time simply because the truth-value of the counterfactual is settled at _t_ . But these may not be the only cases: the unconditional chances of counterfactuals may bear more directly on the truth-value of a counterfactual than the corresponding conditional counterfactual chances. For this reason, it is possible to reject the assumption that the unconditional physical chances are known as inadmissible in the application of the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals. In practice, this issue is unlikely to ever come up, for, as argued above, we will hardly, if ever, have any direct information about the unconditional chances of counterfactuals. 

## .Conclusion 

In closing, let me address a potential puzzlement one could have with respect to the position I have been defending in response to Williams’s argument. It might seem that equating the unconditional physical chance 

triviality  

of a counterfactual with the corresponding conditional chance is quite congenial to the overall spirit of the picture I have been pushing for. After all, this assumption is not so different from the suggestion that the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals should equal, on ideal reflection, certain conditional chances. In both cases, conditional probabilities are linked with unconditional ones. There are, of course, some differences. The subjective probabilities of counterfactuals do not quite go with the corresponding chances conditional on the antecedent, but rather with the chances conditional on the relevant antecedent-worlds. Moreover, they are not supposed to always match them, but rather to estimate them. All this led to describing the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals in terms of the probability of the consequent under the image of the antecedent. 

On the theory I am envisaging, there is a further reason to be sceptical about a direct link between physical chances of counterfactuals and conditional physical chances. The explanation of the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals rests on the specific epistemic profile induced by the epsilon operator. As was pointed out in section .., it is to be expected that the epistemic profile of counterfactuals does not go hand in hand with their modal profile. Whereas subjective probabilities target the epistemic profile of sentences, objective chances are sensitive to their modal profile. While the subjective probability that Hesperus is Phosphorus can rationally assume any value between and , the objective chance of this proposition is always due to its metaphysical necessity. So, on the present theory, the link between the subjective probabilities of counterfactuals and conditional chances essentially depends on the specific epistemic profile counterfactuals are assumed to have. There is no direct way of extending this link to the level of objective chances, for the latter are only sensitive to the modal profile of the relevant sentence or proposition. 

To sum up, I have pursued two lines of defence against the threat of triviality. The first one is to see whether the probability functions constructed in the course of a triviality result contains any inadmissible information. It is at this point where it is crucial to keep in mind that just like the original Principal Principle, the evaluation constraint on counterfactuals is only applicable to probability functions not containing inadmissible information. So, in the context of possible triviality results, the focus should be on the Principal Principle type formulation of the evaluation constraint and not on the one in terms of imaging, for 

 triviality 

the imaging constraint is overly general in this regard. A second line of defence concerns the possibility of identifying under certain conditions subjective probabilities and more objective kinds of probability in order to make the original results for indicative conditionals applicable to counterfactuals. Here it is important to be aware that even if some such identifications are possible, it does not follow that we get a full-blown version of Adams’s thesis with objective chances substituted for subjective probabilities. The evaluation constraint on counterfactuals does not imply an objectified version of Adams’s thesis. 

## **** 

## Concluding Remarks 

In situations of uncertainty, counterfactuals are evaluated in a puzzling way. Our uncertain judgements about counterfactuals deviate dramatically from what standard semantics would predict. Finding a theory of counterfactuals which can explain counterfactual reasoning in situations of uncertainty was the main goal of this study. 

The proposed semantics comes with a somewhat refined picture of counterfactuals. Whereas counterfactuals are in standard semantics conceived of as expressing a certain kind of _restricted necessity_ —truth of the consequent in _all_ relevant antecedent-worlds—the present theory describes counterfactuals as being of intermediate strength: weaker than counterfactual necessity but stronger than counterfactual possibility. In this regard, counterfactuals resemble future contingents. The truthvalue of a future contingent is eventually settled by how the world happens to unfold. Something similar is not possible in the counterfactual case: we cannot wait and see how a counterfactual future evolves. This may be the source of why counterfactuals involve an element of arbitrariness: which counterfactual future fixes the truth-value of a counterfactual is to some extent an arbitrary affair. 

Quite clearly, this can only be a first step in resolving the problems surrounding counterfactuals and probability. There are quite a few issues left open. These concern the prospects of explaining the relevant data pragmatically or error-theoretically, or through a combination of two such approaches. It would also be desirable to go back to the data about indicative conditionals and see whether a similar explanation in terms of the epsilon-operator can be given there. By way of conclusion, let me briefly comment on these issues. 

 concluding remarks 

## .Error Theories 

The problems surrounding conditionals and probability are multifarious. More often than not the relevant evidence seems to pull in different directions. In the light of this, one may expect the best overall theory to be best only by a small margin, emerging as the winner only after a comprehensive cost-benefit analysis. If one thinks that any account connecting conditionals with conditional probabilities will have some costs, one may want to see whether a positive account of the data does in fact fare better than an error theory. According to an error theory, the data we have considered to motivate the present theory should not be taken to be genuine. Rather, they would be described as reflecting a certain kind of error we are prone to making in our use of conditionals in contexts of uncertainty. 

An error theory is probably best pictured as advocating a simple and straightforward semantics for counterfactuals, standard semantics or a version of a strict analysis, say. The data which suggest that a different semantics is called for would then have to be explained away as stemming from a certain kind of error. For instance, one might hold that we are simply unjustified in being fairly confident that we would have lost if we had bought a lottery ticket. Really, we should not invest any credence in this counterfactual, for it is knowably false, as predicted, for instance, by the standard account. To a large extent, the way we think about counterfactuals would be fundamentally mistaken. On the linguistic level, the interaction of counterfactuals with epistemic phrases would be seen as simply reflecting this mistake. Accordingly, there would be no need to explain how a sentence like ‘It is likely that if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost’ can be true (or acceptable) in a given situation because it would, despite appearances, not be true (or acceptable) at all. 

As I see it, an error theory of the envisaged kind would be particularly costly, for the kind of error we are supposed to make would be fairly global, quite systematic and to a large extent irreversible. We would be mistaken about almost all types of counterfactuals and in evaluating counterfactuals we would be following a principle, namely the evaluation constraint, for which there is, in fact, no rationale. Given the pervasiveness of the postulated error, a theory of this kind strikes me as a last resort. Now, this is not to say that an error theory must be false. After all, errors of the envisaged kind can, and perhaps have been made. But as long as 

concluding remarks  

we can give a positive account which is not too costly—and both the modifier view sketched in chapter and the theory I ultimately favour seem to satisfy this condition—a brute error theory seems to be the least attractive option. 

To be fair, we should note that there are ways to soften the consequences of an error theory. For a start, one may offer truths in the neighbourhood of the predicted falsehoods. Although most of the sentences which constitute evidence for the evaluation constraint would not be true or acceptable, certain variants of them may be true or acceptable. Ascriptions of subjective probability to the whole counterfactual may be substituted by ascriptions of objective chance which attach to the consequent of the counterfactual only. Such a strategy seems to be employed by Bennett (: ch. ) (see, in particular, Bennett : ) and is also considered by Hájek () in response to a slightly different but related problem (see the brief discussion in section .). To illustrate the approach, consider one of the sentences we considered: 

() It is likely that if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost. Although this sentence comes out false on an error theory, the suggestion would be that a close variant of the sentence would still be true: 

- () If I had bought a lottery ticket, there would have been a high chance that I would lose. 

On this strategy, a wide scope epistemic expression would be replaced by a narrow scope objective expression. 

The idea that sentences of the latter kind are true or acceptable when sentences of the former kind merely appear to be acceptable seems to work for some cases, but it will not always work. A first type of counterexample are cases in which the epistemic expression reflects an estimate of the corresponding counterfactual chances (this was mentioned already in the introduction). It might be an epistemic possibility that the lottery was rigged in my favour which would have made we a winner no matter what. If this possibility is sufficiently unlikely, we would still think it likely that we would have lost if we had bought a ticket. But it is not clear that the corresponding counterfactual with the objective chance ascription in the consequent would be acceptable for us when construed along the lines of standard semantics. There will then be an epistemic possibility according to which our ticket would have won no matter what. Hence, 

 concluding remarks 

a counterfactual saying that there would have been a high chance of losing does not seem to be fully acceptable. 

Another type of counterexample comes from cases where the chances do not seem to be the same across all relevant worlds (Hájek considers similar cases). Suppose me buying a lottery ticket sometimes but not very often causes an assistant in the company to rig the lottery in my favour. Then it seems that in some relevant worlds the lottery will be rigged, so it will not be true that the chance of my ticket losing will be high in all relevant worlds. On standard semantics, the narrow scope counterfactual will thus be false. On the other hand, given that the assistant does not help me most of the time and given that without any help it is vastly unlikely that I win, I should still think it likely that if I had bought a ticket, I would have lost. 

It seems, then, that the strategy of offering acceptable sentences in the neighbourhood of the erroneous counterfactuals can at best be a partial success. Of course, one would like to check whether there are better alternatives to the strategy considered here, but I am not aware of any promising candidates (see also section .of the introduction). It would therefore seem that an error theorist should look for further ways of diminishing the costs of her theory. 

An error theorist can give a fairly good explanation of how an error in evaluating counterfactuals could come about. For example, Leitgeb (a,b) (see also Leitgeb ) suggests that the troublesome credences may well not be credences that the proposed truth conditions obtain but rather—following Adams (, ) and Edgington (, )—credences arrived at under the counterfactual supposition of the antecedent. According to Leitgeb, there are two ways of engaging epistemically with a counterfactual: (i) estimating how likely the truth conditions are satisfied and (ii) estimating how likely the consequent is satisfied under the counterfactual supposition of the antecedent. In a context of utterance, the second kind of credence might be pragmatically conveyed despite the fact that the first kind will always be literally communicated. 

The challenge for such a dual account would be to uncover cases where our epistemic attitudes do indeed reflect what is predicted by (i) on the proposed truth conditions. Is there a sense in which we deem it to be certainly false that if we had bought a lottery ticket, we would have lost? As far as I can see, evidence that counterfactuals play a two-fold role in how they interact with epistemic modals and epistemic attitude verbs is 

concluding remarks  

hard to find, but I do take this to be an important question for future research. Another question would be by which process the second kind of credences, the ones which are captured by the evaluation constraint, would be pragmatically conveyed. I briefly comment on this in the next section, for at this point an error theory would actually join forces with a pragmatic explanation of the relevant data. 

## .Pragmatic Explanations 

Could the data be explained pragmatically? Some such story should accompany a possible error theory, but it also seems an interesting option in its own right, the more so the more systematic a story one could tell. As briefly mentioned in the introductory chapter, there have been attempts to explain the interaction of indicative conditionals and conditional probabilities in pragmatic terms. Perhaps the most influential account of this kind was Jackson’s (, ) idea that the assertion of an indicative conditional conventionally implies that the corresponding conditional probability is high (see also the postscript to Lewis in Lewis b[] ). Something similar could be tried for counterfactuals. Simply assume that the assertion of a counterfactual conventionally implies that our estimate of the corresponding counterfactual chances is high. We should observe, however, that this explains at best a small portion of the relevant data. It could explain why we might still find a counterfactual like ‘If I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost’ assertable (if we do find it assertable—I, for one, do not think that it is assertable; see section .). But as it stands this approach leaves the interaction of conditionals with epistemic phrases completely unexplained. Why is the qualified sentence ‘It is likely that if I had bought a lottery ticket, I would have lost’ acceptable? Relatedly, how are the puzzling credences in counterfactuals to be explained? Something more, or perhaps something else entirely would be needed to give a pragmatic explanation of the problematic data. 

A more promising approach would be, I think, to explore the possibility of a pragmatic take on the modifier view. On the modifier view, counterfactual ‘if’-clauses are capable of modifying the domain of an epistemic expression. A pragmatic interpretation of this approach could 

>  It seems an open question to me what connection, if any, Lewis saw between Jackson’s approach and the restrictor analysis he proposed elsewhere. 

 concluding remarks 

be derived in analogy to pragmatic constraints on quantifier domains. Context often imposes restrictions on the domain of a quantifier and depending on one’s theory of domain restriction, some or even most restricted interpretations are derived pragmatically. In a similar vein, one might hold that counterfactual ‘if’-clauses induce a pragmatic constraint on the interpretation of epistemic domains by raising to salience a modified interpretation. On such a picture, the problematic data would have a pragmatic explanation which stays very close to the modifier view as described in chapter . 

Of course, a pragmatic variant of the modifier view can be expected to inherit the problem of embeddings the modifier view seems to have. But given that a pragmatic approach does not necessarily have to aim at a strict rule through which the interpretations of the problematic sentences can be derived, it is possible that a more lenient view on this problem might be defendable. Although one would have to see what the details will look like, a pragmatic approach of this kind seems to be a good fallback position if neither the positive theory defended here nor the semantic construal of the modifier view can be brought to work. 

## .Unifying the Theory? 

Perhaps the most important open problem concerns the possibility of a unified theory of conditionals. I have not tried to relate the present theory of counterfactuals to indicative conditionals. But given that the data concerning the two kinds of conditionals are quite similar, one may reasonably hope for a similar explanation in both cases. 

It is not very hard to give an outline of what a corresponding theory for indicative conditionals might look like. Assume that the domain of the epsilon-operator are the contextually relevant epistemic possibilities. Assume further that there is a subjective probability distribution _P_ over them. Now suppose that the epsilon-operator selects one of the epistemic antecedent-worlds with the conditional probability induced by _P_ . 

Formally, the truth conditions would look very much like the truth conditions for counterfactuals, with the epistemically possible antecedent-worlds in the indicative case playing the role of the relevant antecedent-worlds in the counterfactual case (cf. especially the explication of truth conditions at the end of section ..). They could be 

concluding remarks  

displayed roughly like this, with _Cw_ being a set of salient epistemically possible worlds and _Pw_ being a probability distribution over _Cw_ (relative to a world _w_ ): 

**Possible Truth Conditions for Indicatives.** Let _w_ be any world and ⌜ _φ_ → _ψ_ ⌝ an indicative conditional. Then _w_ |= ⌜ _φ_ → _ψ_ ⌝ iff 

_(_ ∃ _w_[′] ∈ _(Cw_ ∩ � _φ_ � _))_ ⊃ _(ϵPw w_[′] _(w_[′] ∈ _(Cw_ ∩ � _φ_ � _))_ |= _ψ)_ . 

According to these truth conditions, an indicative conditional is vacuously true iff its antecedent is epistemically impossible. Otherwise, it is true iff the consequent is true at an arbitarily selected epistemically possible antecedent-world. 

The difference between indicative conditionals and counterfactuals would be mainly a difference in the kind of modality they are associated with: epistemic in the indicative case, metaphysical in the counterfactual case (cf. Weatherson ). A further difference would be a buildin context-dependence of indicative conditionals. As in the case of the restrictor view, one would not necessarily have to assume that indicative conditionals are always sensitive to the domain of overt epistemic modals. One could even allow for contexts _C_ containing only the world of evaluation, so that indicative conditionals could be read as material conditionals. 

A number of issues would have to be addressed. Can one justify a version of Adams’s thesis on the basis of these truth conditions (or slight variants thereof)? _Prima facie_ at least, there is hope for a positive answer. If _P_ corresponds to our subjective probabilities, then the probability that a consequent-world gets selected seems to be the relevant conditional probability.[] Which role exactly should be played by context, how is it determined and what constrains it? How should the logic of indicative conditionals be approached? Here, the discussion of the logic of counterfactuals could provide some guidance. Moreover, there are a great many further puzzles and problems about indicative conditionals which would have to be taken into view. A whole lot more would have to be said. 

Just as in the counterfactual case—probably even more so—, a prime rival of such an approach would be an account along the lines of the restrictor view. An important question would therefore be whether the 

>  Cf. the recent work in Bacon (). 

 concluding remarks 

problem of embeddings as discussed in section .can be reproduced for indicative conditionals. _Prima facie_ , this seems to be the case. Consider: 

- () It is likely that if Jacky does not play this weekend, she’ll get angry. 

- () Therefore, it is likely that there is someone who will get angry if she does not play this weekend. 

The inference seems valid. There is an inconsistency in saying ‘It is likely that if Jacky does not play this weekend, she’ll get angry, but it is not likely that there is someone who will get angry if she does not play this weekend’. To make the problem more vivid, suppose that Jacky tends to get angry when she is not on the team, but occasionally accepts sitting on the bench. Moreover, no other player is determined to get angry on that condition, although some very well might. Given such a background, we cannot reconstruct the embedded conditional as restricting a covert necessity operator, neither in the premise nor in the conclusion (though see Rothschild ). On the other hand, the ‘if’-clause in the conclusion cannot be assumed to restrict the probability operator due to the interferring quantifier. As in the counterfactual case, the most natural reaction would be to account for the conditional construction by finding a (covert) operator so that the probability of the conditional corresponds to the conditional probability. The epsilon-operator promises to do just that.[] 

One will have to control for the possibility that the problematic inference cannot be explained in some other way. Perhaps the nominal quantifier should be reconstructed as scoping over the epistemic phrase and out of the ‘that’-clause ( _prima facie_ this strikes me as implausible; it also runs against epistemic modals tending to take widest scope). Perhaps the conditional in the conclusion is interpreted as the material conditional? That would make the embedded quantified conditional certain (which it does not seem to be), for it would already be verified by one player playing, which is something bound to happen. Perhaps the embedded 

>  Having a conditional proposition available for anaphoric reference would help with ‘that’s likely’-data as discussed in von Fintel (, ) (but see Kratzer : ). There is also some independent evidence for Stalnaker-type truth conditions stemming from embeddings of conditionals under quantifiers. See Higginbotham (, ) and Klinedinst (). 

concluding remarks  

conditional is given a more objective interpretation (would this still validate the inference?).[] 

As far as I am aware, the transition problem for indicative conditionals does not extend to all constructions for which the restrictor view has a plausible story. For instance, it does not seem to extend to adverbs of quantification. Therefore, the proposal would not be to substitute the envisaged truth conditions for the restrictor account across the board, unless one finds alternative explanations for the remaining cases which suggest a restrictor story. In other words, an epsilon-based semantics for conditionals need not be seen as opposing the restrictor view of conditionals, for the epsilon-operator can naturally be construed as as another option for the implicit operator the restrictor view postulates for unembedded conditionals. 

More generally speaking, I am open to the possibility that the aim of giving a unified account may lead to a reassessment of the weight of certain arguments I have given. If, for instance, it should turn out that the present account cannot be plausibly extended to indicative conditionals and the restrictor view proved to be the only viable explanation of how indicative conditionals embed under quantifiers and operators, it would be tempting to instead adopt the modifier view of counterfactuals—which squares well with a restrictor view on indicatives—and somehow live with the costs. 

## .Conclusion 

Uncertain counterfactuals constitute, as I see it, a deep challenge to our understanding of how their semantics works. Both the credences we are inclined to assign to counterfactuals and the epistemic phrases we attach to them form a very puzzling pattern. As we have seen, the problem might even run so deep that the very possibility of associating counterfactuals with truth conditions is called into question. I think the standard account alone, without any extra pragmatic or semantic elements added to it, cannot meet the challenge. In my eyes, the modifier view, construed either semantically or pragmatically, stands a good chance of providing a satisfying account. An even better way, I believe, is to 

>  I received valuable input on these issues from the participants of a workshop on conditionals in Hamburg . 

 concluding remarks 

construe ‘would’-counterfactuals in close analogy to ‘will’-sentences. Just like a ‘will’-sentence typically concerns a single and as yet unknown or even undetermined future, ‘would’-sentences are about single worlds to which we do not always have full epistemic access. If a counterfactual (or, more generally, any conditional) has a false antecedent, the counterfactual world associated with it is not fully determined. This is, I suggest, the reason why the semantics of counterfactuals should contain an arbitrary element: the single counterfactual world we seem to think and talk about can only be fixed arbitrarily.
